Like those coming from the South, taking goods produced in the south and entering the city through the south gate, so too those coming by way of mindfulness of feeling (*vedanā*), cultivating mindfulness of feeling in nine ways, descend to a single *nirvāṇa* by means of the Noble Path produced by the power of cultivating mindfulness of feeling.

Like those coming from the West, taking goods produced in the west and entering the city through the west gate, so too those coming by way of mindfulness of the mind (*citta*), cultivating mindfulness of the mind in sixteen ways, descend to a single *nirvāṇa* by means of the Noble Path produced by the power of cultivating mindfulness of the mind.

Like those coming from the North, taking goods produced in the north and entering the city through the north gate, so too those coming by way of mindfulness of phenomena (*dharma*), cultivating mindfulness of phenomena in five ways, descend to a single *nirvāṇa* by means of the Noble Path produced by the power of cultivating mindfulness of phenomena.

Thus, it should be understood that the four foundations of mindfulness (*satipaṭṭhāna*) are spoken of as one in terms of refuge (*saraṇa*) and convergence to oneness (*ekattasamosaraṇa*), and as four in terms of object (*ārammaṇa*).

What are the four? The question is one intending to speak.

"Here" means in this teaching (*śāsana*).

"Monks (*bhikkhave*)" is an address to individuals who accept the *dharma*.

"Monks (*bhikkhū*)" is a designation of individuals who accomplish practice (*paṭipatti*).

Even other gods and humans accomplish practice, but because of the excellence of the practice, and because of the manifestation of the state of a monk (*bhikkhubhāva*), he said "monks (*bhikkhū*)".

Among those who accept the Buddha's teaching (*anusāsani*), the monk (*bhikkhu*) is the best, because of his capacity to receive all kinds of teachings.

Therefore, because of the excellence, he said "monks (*bhikkhū*)".

When one is taken, the rest are also taken; like in royal processions, when the king is taken, the rest of the retinue are as if taken.

And whoever undertakes this practice (*paṭipatti*), that person is called a monk (*bhikkhu*); thus, because of the manifestation of the state of a monk (*bhikkhubhāva*) through practice (*paṭipatti*), he said "monks (*bhikkhū*)".

Whether a follower is a god or a human, they go to the state of being called monks (*bhikkhū*), as he said:

What are the four? Here, monks (*bhikkhave*), monks (*bhikkhū*)…

Even if adorned, he should live evenly,

calm, restrained, controlled, practicing chastity;

having set aside violence towards all beings,

he is a *brāhmaṇa*, he is an ascetic (*śramaṇa*), he is a monk (*bhikkhū*)".

(Dhammapada 142);

"*Kāya* (body)" means *rūpakāya* (form body).

Indeed, here, *rūpakāya* (form body) is understood as the body (*kāya*) by way of the collection of parts (*aṅga* and *paccaṅga*) and phenomena (*dharma*) such as hairs and so on, like a herd of elephants, a concourse of chariots, and so on.

And as with the sense of a collection (*samūhaṭṭhena*), so also with the sense of the focus of what is filthy (*kucchitānaṃ āyaṭṭhena*).

For indeed, the body (*kāya*) is also the place of origin (*āyoti*) of what is disgusting (*kucchitānaṃ*), of what is utterly repulsive (*paramajegucchānaṃ*).

*Āyo* means place of origin (*uppattideso*).

Herein lies the meaning of the word (*vacanattho*).

"From that place they come forth (*āyanti tato*)," therefore it is *āyo*.

Who comes forth? Disgusting hairs and so on (*kucchitā kesādayo*).

Thus, the body (*kāya*) is the place of origin (*āyo*) of what is disgusting (*kucchitānaṃ*).

In the *kāya* (body). *Kāyānupassī* (one who contemplates the body) means one who is in the habit of contemplating the body (*kāye anupassanasīlo*), or one who is contemplating the body (*kāyaṃ vā anupassamāno*).

Having said "*kāye* (in the body)", why again "*kāyānupassī* (one who contemplates the body)"? The second taking of *kāya* is done for the purpose of showing non-confusion, separation, and seeing the nature of density, detachment, and so on (*asammissato vavatthānaghanavinibbhogādidassanatthaṃ katanti veditabbaṃ*).

Therefore, there is no contemplation of feeling (*vedanā*) in the body (*kāye*), nor contemplation of mental objects (*cittadhamma*), but rather only contemplation of the body (*kāyānupassīyevāti*); thus, by the seeing of only the mode of contemplation of the body in the property (*vatthusmiṃ*) called the body (*kāyasaṅkhāte*), isolation (*vavatthānaṃ*) from non-confusion (*asammissato*) is shown.

Similarly, there is no contemplation of a single phenomenon (*ekadhamma*) apart from the parts of the body (*aṅgapaccaṅga*), nor is there contemplation of man or woman apart from hairs and so on (*kesalomādi*); and even though the body (*kāya*) here is designated as a collection (*samūhasaṅkhāto*) of the elements (*bhūta*) and derived forms (*upādāya*), even there is no contemplation of a single phenomenon (*ekadhamma*) apart from the elements (*bhūta*) and derived forms (*upādāya*); but rather, just as one who contemplates chariot components is like one who contemplates a collection of parts (*aṅgapaccaṅgasamūha*), just as one who contemplates the components of a city is like one who contemplates a collection of hairs and so on (*kesalomādisamūha*), just as one who peels away the layers of a banana tree trunk or one who unwraps an empty fist, so too is one who contemplates a collection of elements (*bhūta*) and derived forms (*upādāya*); thus, by the seeing of the property (*vatthuno*) called the body (*kāyasaṅkhātassa*) by way of the collection (*samūhavasena*) in various ways (*nānappakārato*), the nature of density and detachment (*ghanavinibbhogo*) is shown.

Here, neither a body (*kāyo*) apart from the collection of elements that have been spoken of (*yathāvuttasamūha*), nor a woman (*itthī*) nor a man (*puriso*) nor any other phenomenon (*dhammo*) is seen; but beings (*sattā*) make wrong attachments (*micchābhinivesaṃ karonti*) to just the collection of phenomena that have been spoken of (*yathāvuttadhammasamūhamatteyeva*).
Therefore, the ancients said:

A *kāyānupassī* [contemplator of the body].

"He sees what is not seen, and does not see what is seen;

The fool, not seeing, is bound; being bound, he is not freed."

It has been said to show the meaning of seeing the non-essential etc. By the word "ādi" here, this meaning should also be understood.

For it has been said: "This person contemplates in this body only the body; he does not contemplate anything else."

Just as in a mirage, though it is waterless, there are those who see water, but in this transient, suffering, soulless, and unlovely body, he does not see permanence, pleasure, self, or loveliness; but rather, the *kāyānupassī* only contemplates the collection of qualities of transiency, suffering, soullessness, and unloveliness, thus it has been said.

Or rather, that which was said further on, "Here, *bhikkhus*, a *bhikkhu* having gone to the forest, etc… he breathes in mindfully," in this way the body ending in the skin, sinews, bones, etc., produced from the powder of breaths, etc., was spoken of, and that which was said, "Here someone contemplates the earth body as impermanent, the water body, the fire body, the air body, the hair body, the body hair body, the skin body, the flesh body, the blood body, the sinew body, the bone body, the bone marrow body" (Paṭisambhidāmagga 3.35), in the *Paṭisambhidāmagga* the body is spoken of; because of not contemplating all of that in this very body, the meaning of contemplating the body in the body should be understood in this way as well.

Or rather, because of not contemplating "I" or "mine" in the body by whomever must be grasped in this way, but rather contemplating the various collections of things like hair and body hair etc., in the body, the *kāya* designated as a collection of things like hair and body hair etc., this meaning should be seen.

Moreover, from contemplating the body as impermanent, not permanent, etc., in succession, according to the method that has come down in the *Paṭisambhidāmagga*, and also from contemplating the *kāya* designated as a collection of forms characterized by impermanence etc., in the body, [one is] a contemplator of the body in the body; this meaning should also be seen.

Thus, indeed, this *bhikkhu*, having undertaken the practice of contemplating the body in the body, contemplates this body as impermanent, not permanent, by way of the seven contemplations beginning with the contemplation of impermanence.

He contemplates as subject to suffering, not as pleasant.

He contemplates as not-self, not as self.

He becomes dispassionate, he does not delight; he causes greed to fade away, he does not inflame it; he causes cessation.

He does not originate; he relinquishes, he does not grasp.

He, contemplating it as impermanent, abandons the perception of permanence; contemplating it as subject to suffering, he abandons the perception of pleasure; contemplating it as not-self, he abandons the perception of self; becoming dispassionate, he abandons delight; causing greed to fade away, he abandons greed; causing cessation, he abandons originating; relinquishing, he abandons grasping, thus it should be understood.

“He dwells” means he moves.

"*Ātāpī*" means zeal, because he heats up the defilements in the three existences; that is why it is called zeal, it is a name for energy.

Because he has zeal, he is *ātāpī*.

"*Sampajāno*" means he is endowed with knowledge designated as clear comprehension.

"*Satimā*" means he is endowed with mindfulness that takes hold of the body.

But this [mindfulness], because it apprehends the object by mindfulness and contemplates by wisdom, since indeed there is no contemplation called contemplation without mindfulness, therefore he said, "And I, *bhikkhus*, declare mindfulness to be useful in every way" (Saṃyutta Nikāya 5.234).

Therefore, here, by [the words] "he dwells contemplating the body in the body," the establishment of mindfulness of contemplating the body is spoken of.

Or rather, because to the non-zealous there is the obstacle of mental lassitude, and the uncomprehending is confused in choosing the right means and avoiding the wrong means, and the unmindful is incapable of relinquishing the right means and grasping the wrong means, therefore that object of meditation does not succeed for him.

Therefore, in order to show those qualities by the power of which it succeeds, it should be understood that this was said: "zealous, clearly comprehending, mindful."

*Viharati, ātāpī, sampajāno, satimā.*

Thus, having shown the limb of association with the establishment of mindfulness of contemplating the body, now in order to show the limb of abandoning, it is said, "having removed covetousness and displeasure in the world."

There, "*vineyya*" means having removed by temporary suppression or by the Vinaya of its parts.
The world *loke* means in that very body.

Here, the body *kāya* is understood as the world *loka* due to its nature of crumbling and decaying.

Because covetousness *abhijjhā* and grief *daumanassa* are not abandoned in the body alone, but also in feeling *vedanā* and so on.

Therefore, the Vibhanga says, "Even the five aggregates of clinging *pañcapi upādānakkhandhā* are the world *loka*."

Or this is stated through a method of extracting the meaning of those phenomena, because it is known as the world *lokasaṅkhātattā*.

But what he said – “There, what is the world *loka*? It is just the body *kāyo* that is the world *loko*" – this is the meaning here.

It should be seen that the connection is thus: eliminating covetousness *abhijjhā* and grief *daumanassa* in that world *loke*.

Because here, sense desire *kāmacchando* is included by the grasping of covetousness *abhijjhā*, and anger *byāpādo* goes into the collection by the grasping of grief *daumanassa*, therefore, it should be understood that the abandonment of the hindrances *nīvaraṇappahānaṃ* is stated by showing the pair of strong phenomena included in the hindrances *nīvaraṇapariyāpannabalavadhammadvaya*.

Eliminating covetousness *abhijjhā* and grief *daumanassa* in the world *loke*, eliminating in the world *loke*.

Specifically, here, by overcoming covetousness *abhijjhāvinayena*, satisfaction *anurodhassa* rooted in bodily happiness *kāyasampattimūlakassa*; by overcoming grief *domanassavinayena*, opposition *virodhassa* rooted in bodily misery *kāyavipattimūlakassa*; and by overcoming covetousness *abhijjhāvinayena*, delight *abhiratiyā* in the body *kāye*; by overcoming grief *domanassavinayena*, non-delight *anabhiratiyā* in cultivating the body *kāyabhāvanāya*; by overcoming covetousness *abhijjhāvinayena*, casting aside *pakkhepassa* unreal things *abhūtānaṃ* such as beauty and pleasure *subhasukhabhāvādīnaṃ* in the body *kāye*; and by overcoming grief *domanassavinayena*, the abandonment *pahānaṃ* of removing *apanayanassa* real things *bhūtānaṃ* such as ugliness and misery *asubhāsukhabhāvādīnaṃ* in the body *kāye* is stated.

By that, yogic power *yogānubhāvo* and yogic skill *yogasamatthatā* of the yogin *yogāvacarassa* are shown.

Indeed, this is yogic power *yogānubhāvo*, that is, being free from satisfaction and discontent *anurodhavirodhavippamutto*, bearing dislike and delight *aratiratisaho*, and being without casting aside the unreal *abhūtapakkhepabhūtāpanayanavirahito*.

Being free from satisfaction and discontent *anurodhavirodhavippamutto*, bearing dislike and delight *aratiratisaho*, casting aside the unreal *abhūtaṃ apakkhipanto* and not removing the real *bhūtañca anapanayanto*, he is skilled in yoga *yogasamattho hotīti*.

Another method *nayo* is that in "lives contemplating the body in the body *kāye kāyānupassī*," the subject of meditation *kammaṭṭhānaṃ* of contemplation *anupassanāya* is stated.

Here, by the stated abiding *vuttavihārena* in "abiding *viharati*," the protection of the body *kāyapariharaṇaṃ* of the one who has the subject of meditation *kammaṭṭhānikassa* is [explained]; in "ardent *ātāpī*," and so on, Right Exertion *sammappadhānaṃ* is [explained] by energy *ātāpena*; by mindfulness and clear comprehension *satisampajaññena*, the subject of meditation proper in all circumstances *sabbatthakakammaṭṭhānaṃ*, or the means of protecting the subject of meditation *kammaṭṭhānapariharaṇūpāyo*, is [explained].

Or, by mindfulness *satiyā*, quietude *samatho* that is obtained *paṭiladdha* by way of contemplation on the body *kāyānupassanāvasena*; by clear comprehension *sampajaññena*, insight *vipassanā*; it should be understood that the power of cultivation *bhāvanābalaṃ* by overcoming covetousness and grief *abhijjhādomanassavinayena* is stated.

In the Vibhanga *vibhaṅge*, however, in "contemplating *anupassī*," there, "What is contemplation *anupassanā*? That wisdom *paññā*, understanding *pajānanā*, search *vicayo*, research *pavicayo*, searching the Truth *dhammavicayo*, discerning *sallakkhaṇā*, specifying *upalakkhaṇā*, counter-specifying *paccupalakkhaṇā*, skill *paṇḍiccaṃ*, adroitness *kosallaṃ*, dexterity *nepuññaṃ*, proficiency *vebhabyā*, thought *cintā*, investigation * upaparikkhā*, extensive wisdom *bhūrīmedhā*, guiding principle *pariṇāyikā*, insight *vipassanā*, clear comprehension *sampajaññaṃ*, goad *patodo*, knowledge *paññā*, faculty of knowledge *paññindriyaṃ*, power of knowledge *paññābalaṃ*, weapon of knowledge *paññāsatthaṃ*, palace of knowledge *paññāpāsādo*, light of knowledge *paññāāloko*, radiance of knowledge *paññāobhāso*, splendor of knowledge *paññāpajjoto*, jewel of knowledge *paññāratanaṃ*, non-delusion *amoho*, investigation of phenomena *dhammavicayo*, right view *sammādiṭṭhi*, this is called contemplation *anupassanā*.

By this contemplation *imāya anupassanāya*, he is furnished with *upeto hoti*... he is endowed with *samannāgato*, therefore he is called contemplating *anupassī*.

"Abiding *viharatī*" means he behaves *iriyati*, proceeds *pavattati*, protects *pāleti*, maintains *yapeti*, manages *yāpeti*, conducts himself *carati*, abides *viharati*, therefore he is called abiding *viharati*.

"Ardent *ātāpī*" means there, what is energy *ātāpaṃ*? That mental *cetasiko* exertion of effort *vīriyārambho*, resoluteness *nikammo*, perseverance *parakkamo*, endeavor *uyyāmo*, effort *vāyāmo*, enthusiasm *ussāho*, impetuosity *ussoḷhī*, strength *thāmo*, steadfastness *dhiti*, unremitting perseverance *asithilaparakkamatā*, not laying down the burden *anikkhittaddandatā*, not giving up the duty *anikkhittadhuratā*, taking up the duty *dhurasampaggāhī*, energy *vīriyaṃ*, faculty of energy *vīriyindriyaṃ*, power of energy *vīriyabalaṃ*, right effort *sammāvāyāmo*, this is called energy *ātāpaṃ*.

By this energy *iminā ātāpena* he is furnished with *upeto hoti*... he is endowed with *samannāgato*, therefore he is called ardent *ātāpī*.

"Clearly comprehending *sampajāno*" means there, what is clear comprehension *sampajaññaṃ*? That wisdom *paññā*, understanding *pajānanā*, search *vicayo*, research *pavicayo*, searching the Truth *dhammavicayo*, discerning *sallakkhaṇā*, specifying *upalakkhaṇā*, counter-specifying *paccupalakkhaṇā*, skill *paṇḍiccaṃ*, adroitness *kosallaṃ*, dexterity *nepuññaṃ*, proficiency *vebhabyā*, thought *cintā*, investigation * upaparikkhā*, extensive wisdom *bhūrīmedhā*, guiding principle *pariṇāyikā*, insight *vipassanā*, clear comprehension *sampajaññaṃ*, goad *patodo*, knowledge *paññā*, faculty of knowledge *paññindriyaṃ*, power of knowledge *paññābalaṃ*, weapon of knowledge *paññāsatthaṃ*, palace of knowledge *paññāpāsādo*, light of knowledge *paññāāloko*, radiance of knowledge *paññāobhāso*, splendor of knowledge *paññāpajjoto*, jewel of knowledge *paññāratanaṃ*, non-delusion *amoho*, investigation of phenomena *dhammavicayo*, right view *sammādiṭṭhi*, this is called clear comprehension *sampajaññaṃ*.

By this clear comprehension *iminā sampajaññena* he is furnished with *upeto hoti*... he is endowed with *samannāgato*, therefore he is called clearly comprehending *sampajānoti*.

"Mindful *satimā*" means there, what is mindfulness *sati*? That mindfulness *sati*, recollection *anussati*, memory *paṭissati sati*, remembering *saraṇatā*, retention *dhāraṇatā*, non-confusion *apilāpanatā*, non-forgetfulness *asammusanatā sati*, faculty of mindfulness *satindriyaṃ*, power of mindfulness *satibalaṃ*, right mindfulness *sammāsati*, this is called mindfulness *sati*.

By this mindfulness *imāya satiyā* he is furnished with *upeto hoti*... he is endowed with *samannāgato*, therefore he is called mindful *satimāti*.

Contemplating *anupassī*, abiding *viharatī*, ardent *ātāpī*, clearly comprehending *sampajāno*, mindful *satimā*.

Eliminating covetousness and grief in the world *vineyya loke abhijjhādomanassa* means there, what is the world *loko*? Just the body *kāyo* is the world *loko*.

Even the five aggregates of clinging *pañcapi upādānakkhandhā* are the world *loko*, this is called the world *loko*.

There, what is covetousness *abhijjhā*? That desire *rāgo*, intense desire *sārāgo*, compliance *anunayo*, fondness *anurodho*, delight *nandī*, delight in desire *nandirāgo*, intense desire of the mind *cittassa sārāgo*, this is called covetousness *abhijjhā*.

There, what is grief *domanassaṃ*? That mental *cetasikaṃ* discomfort *asātaṃ*, mental *cetasikaṃ* pain *dukkhaṃ*, discomfort *asātā* and painful * dukkhā* feeling *vedanā* born of mind-contact *cetosamphassajā*, this is called grief *domanassaṃ*.
Thus, in this world, both covetousness (*abhijjhā*) and displeasure (*domanassaṃ*) are abandoned, completely abandoned, calmed, pacified, have ceased, are caused to cease, are applied, are purified, are dried up, are thoroughly dried up, and are eradicated. Therefore, it is said, "having removed covetousness and displeasure in the world" (Vibha. 357-362).

**Having Removed Covetousness and Displeasure in the World**: The meaning of these words has been stated in this way.

In accordance with this, this method of the commentary corresponds; thus, it should be understood.

This much is the explanation of the meaning of the section on the Mindfulness of the Body (*kāyānupassanā-satipaṭṭhāna*).

Now, concerning feelings (*vedanāsu*)...

...concerning the mind (*citte*)...

...concerning mental objects (*dhammesu*), he dwells contemplating mental objects, etc., "having removed covetousness and displeasure in the world." Here, the purpose of repeating *vedanāsu vedanānupassī* ("contemplating feeling in feelings"), and so forth, should be understood in the same way as explained in the section on the Mindfulness of the Body (*kāyānupassanā*).

Contemplating feeling in feelings (*vedanāsu vedanānupassī*).

Contemplating mind in mind (*citte cittānupassī*).

Concerning mental objects, he dwells contemplating mental objects. Here, *vedanā* ("feeling") refers to the three feelings; these are only mundane (*lokiyā eva*).

Consciousness (*citta*) is also only mundane (*lokiyaṃ*), and so too are mental objects (*dhammā*).

Their distinction will be evident in the section on analysis (*niddesavāre*).

However, here, just as feeling is to be contemplated, so too, contemplating them, he should be understood as "contemplating feeling in feelings" (*vedanāsu vedanānupassī*).

This is the method regarding the mind and mental objects.

And how is feeling to be contemplated? Pleasant feeling is suffering (*dukkhato*), painful feeling is a dart (*sallato*), and neither-pleasant-nor-painful feeling is impermanent (*aniccato*).

As it was said:

Contemplating feeling in feelings (*vedanāsu vedanānupassī*): Feeling (*vedanā*):

"Whoever has seen happiness as suffering (*dukkhato*), and seen pain as a dart (*sallato*),

Whoever has seen the peaceful that is neither-pleasant-nor-painful as impermanent (*aniccato*);

He, indeed, is a bhikkhu of right view (*sammaddaso bhikkhu*), who dwells calmed."

(Saṃ. Ni. 4.253);

Moreover, all these should be contemplated as "suffering" (*dukkhā*).

It was said, "Whatever is experienced, that I call suffering" (*taṃ dukkhasminti vadāmī*) (Saṃ. Ni. 4.259).

And, they should also be contemplated as happiness and suffering (*sukhadukkhato*).

As it was said, "Pleasant feeling is agreeable while it lasts and is disagreeable when it changes" (*sukhā vedanā ṭhitisukhā vipariṇāmadukkhā*) (Ma. Ni. 1.465). All of it should be explained in detail.

Moreover, they should also be contemplated in terms of impermanence (*aniccādi*) and non-self (*anatta*).

The remainder will be evident in the section on analysis (*niddesavāre*).

Concerning the mind and mental objects, the mind, in particular, should be contemplated by way of its various distinctions of object (*ārammaṇa*), dominance (*adhipati*), co-nascence (*sahajāta*), plane (*bhūmi*), action (*kamma*), result (*vipāka*), and activity (*kiriyā*), and by way of the contemplations of impermanence, etc., and by way of the distinctions of attachment (*sarāga*), etc., that are presented in the section on analysis (*niddesavāre*).

Mental objects (*dhammā*) should be contemplated by way of their own characteristics (*salakkhaṇa*), general characteristics (*sāmaññalakkhaṇa*), the emptiness of phenomena (*suññatadhamma*), and the contemplations of impermanence (*aniccādi*) and non-self (*anatta*), and by way of the distinctions of peace (*santa*), etc., that are presented in the section on analysis (*niddesavāre*).
The rest is as already stated.

If, in the meditator’s body, called the world ( *kāyasaṅkhāte loke*), covetousness (*abhijjhā*) and grief (*domanassaṃ*) are abandoned, in the worlds of his feelings (*vedanā*) and so forth too, these are abandoned.

Still, everywhere, the abandoning has been stated by way of the different types of persons and by way of the diversity of the thought-unit, in which the development of the different subjects of the Arousing of Mindfulness (*satipaṭṭhāna bhāvanā*) takes place.

Since what is abandoned in one place is abandoned in the remaining places, therefore it should be understood that this was said to show the abandonment there as well.

</p> <p rend="centre">The exposition of the sections is finished.

</p> <p rend="subhead">Explanation of Mindfulness of the Body: The Section on Mindfulness of Breathing</p>
374
<p n="374" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">374</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Kathañca <pb ed="V" n="2.0215"/><pb ed="P" n="2.0291"/> pana, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu araññagato vā rukkhamūlagato vā suññāgāragato vā nisīdati pallaṅkaṃ ābhujitvā ujuṃ kāyaṃ paṇidhāya parimukhaṃ satiṃ upaṭṭhapetvā. So satova assasati, satova passasati. Dīghaṃ <pb ed="T" n="2.0326"/> vā assasanto ‘dīghaṃ assasāmī’ti pajānāti, dīghaṃ vā passasanto ‘dīghaṃ passasāmī’ti pajānāti. Rassaṃ vā assasanto ‘rassaṃ assasāmī’ti pajānāti, rassaṃ vā passasanto ‘rassaṃ passasāmī’ti pajānāti. ‘Sabbakāyapaṭisaṃvedī assasissāmī’ti sikkhati <pb ed="M" n="2.0232"/>, ‘sabbakāyapaṭisaṃvedī passasissāmī’ti sikkhati. ‘Passambhayaṃ kāyasaṅkhāraṃ assasissāmī’ti sikkhati, ‘passambhayaṃ kāyasaṅkhāraṃ passasissāmī’ti sikkhati.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, dakkho bhamakāro vā bhamakārantevāsī vā dīghaṃ vā añchanto ‘dīghaṃ añchāmī’ti pajānāti, rassaṃ vā añchanto ‘rassaṃ añchāmī’ti pajānāti evameva kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu dīghaṃ vā assasanto ‘dīghaṃ assasāmī’ti pajānāti, dīghaṃ vā passasanto ‘dīghaṃ passasāmī’ti pajānāti, rassaṃ vā assasanto ‘rassaṃ assasāmī’ti pajānāti, rassaṃ vā passasanto ‘rassaṃ passasāmī’ti pajānāti. ‘Sabbakāyapaṭisaṃvedī assasissāmī’ti sikkhati, ‘sabbakāyapaṭisaṃvedī passasissāmī’ti sikkhati, ‘passambhayaṃ kāyasaṅkhāraṃ assasissāmī’ti sikkhati, ‘passambhayaṃ kāyasaṅkhāraṃ passasissāmī’ti sikkhati. Iti <pb ed="P" n="2.0292"/> ajjhattaṃ vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati, bahiddhā vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati, ajjhattabahiddhā vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati. Samudayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati, vayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati, samudayavayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati. ‘Atthi kāyo’ti vā <pb ed="T" n="2.0327"/> panassa sati paccupaṭṭhitā hoti yāvadeva ñāṇamattāya paṭissatimattāya anissito ca viharati, na ca kiñci loke upādiyati. Evampi kho <note>evampi (sī. syā. pī.)</note>, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati.</p> <pb ed="P" n="2.0292"/> <pb ed="T" n="2.0327"/> <note>evampi (sī. syā. pī.)</note> <p rend="centre">Ānāpānapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.</p> <p rend="subhead">Kāyānupassanā iriyāpathapabbaṃ</p>
374. But how, monks, does a monk abide contemplating the body as a body? Here, monks, a monk, having gone to the forest, to the root of a tree, or to an empty place, sits down; having folded his legs crosswise, set his body erect, and established mindfulness in front of him.

Mindfully he breathes in, mindfully he breathes out.

Breathing in long, he understands: ‘I breathe in long’; or breathing out long, he understands: ‘I breathe out long.’ Breathing in short, he understands: ‘I breathe in short’; or breathing out short, he understands: ‘I breathe out short.’

He trains himself, thinking: “I will breathe in, conscious of the whole body." He trains himself, thinking: “I will breathe out, conscious of the whole body.

He trains himself, thinking: “I will breathe in, calming the whole bodily process.’ He trains himself, thinking: “I will breathe out, calming the whole bodily process.

“ Just as a skilled turner or his apprentice, when making a long turn, understands: ‘I make a long turn’; or, when making a short turn, understands: ‘I make a short turn’; so too, breathing in long, a *bhikkhu* understands: ‘I breathe in long’… breathing out short, he understands: ‘I breathe out short.’

He trains himself, thinking: “I will breathe in, conscious of the whole body." He trains himself, thinking: “I will breathe out, conscious of the whole body. He trains himself, thinking: “I will breathe in, calming the whole bodily process.’ He trains himself, thinking: “I will breathe out, calming the whole bodily process.’

So he abides contemplating body as body internally, contemplating body as body externally, abides contemplating body as body both internally and externally.

He abides contemplating arising phenomena in the body, contemplating vanishing phenomena in the body, he abides contemplating both arising and vanishing phenomena in the body.

Or his mindfulness that ‘There is a body’ is maintained to the extent of knowledge & remembrance. And he remains independent, unsustained by not clinging to anything in the world.

And that, monks, is how a monk abides contemplating body as body.

The Ānāpāna Section is finished.

The Kāyānupassanā Iriyāpatha Section
<p n="374" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">374</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> Idāni seyyathāpi nāma cheko vilīvakārako thūlakilañjasaṇhakilañjacaṅkoṭakapeḷāpuṭādīni upakaraṇāni kattukāmo ekaṃ mahāveṇuṃ labhitvā catudhā bhinditvā tato ekekaṃ veṇukhaṇḍaṃ gahetvā phāletvā taṃ taṃ upakaraṇaṃ kareyya, evameva bhagavā satipaṭṭhānadesanāya sattānaṃ anekappakāraṃ <pb ed="P" n="3.0762"/> visesādhigamaṃ kattukāmo ekameva sammāsatiṃ ‘‘cattāro satipaṭṭhānā. Katame <pb ed="M" n="2.0353"/> cattāro? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharatī’’tiādinā nayena ārammaṇavasena catudhā bhinditvā tato ekekaṃ satipaṭṭhānaṃ gahetvā kāyaṃ vibhajanto ‘‘kathañca bhikkhave’’tiādinā nayena niddesavāraṃ vattumāraddho.</p> <p rend="bodytext">Tattha <hi rend="bold">kathañcā</hi>tiādi vitthāretukamyatāpucchā. Ayaṃ panettha saṅkhepattho – bhikkhave, kena ca <pb ed="V" n="2.0317"/> pakārena bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharatīti? Esa nayo sabbapucchāvāresu. <hi rend="bold">Idha bhikkhave bhikkhū</hi>ti bhikkhave imasmiṃ sāsane bhikkhu. Ayañhettha idhasaddo sabbappakārakāyānupassanānibbattakassa puggalassa sannissayabhūtasāsanaparidīpano aññasāsanassa tathābhāvapaṭisedhano ca. Vuttañhetaṃ ‘‘idheva bhikkhave, samaṇo…pe… suññā parappavādā samaṇebhi aññehī’’ti (ma. ni. 1.139). Tena vuttaṃ ‘‘imasmiṃ sāsane bhikkhū’’ti.</p> <hi rend="bold">kathañcā</hi> <pb ed="V" n="2.0317"/> <hi rend="bold">Idha bhikkhave bhikkhū</hi> <p rend="bodytext"><hi rend="bold">Araññagato vā rukkhamūlagato vā suññāgāragato vā</hi>ti idamassa satipaṭṭhānabhāvanānurūpasenāsanapariggahaparidīpanaṃ. Imassa hi bhikkhuno dīgharattaṃ rūpādīsu ārammaṇesu anuvisaṭaṃ cittaṃ kammaṭṭhānavīthiṃ otarituṃ na icchati, kūṭagoṇayuttaratho viya uppathameva dhāvati. Tasmā seyyathāpi nāma gopo kūṭadhenuyā sabbaṃ khīraṃ pivitvā vaḍḍhitaṃ kūṭavacchaṃ dametukāmo dhenuto apanetvā ekamante mahantaṃ thambhaṃ nikhaṇitvā tattha yottena bandheyya. Athassa so vaccho ito cito ca vipphanditvā palāyituṃ asakkonto tameva thambhaṃ upanisīdeyya vā upanipajjeyya vā, evameva imināpi bhikkhunā dīgharattaṃ rūpārammaṇādirasapānavaḍḍhitaṃ duṭṭhacittaṃ dametukāmena rūpādiārammaṇato apanetvā araññaṃ vā rukkhamūlaṃ vā suññāgāraṃ vā pavisitvā tattha satipaṭṭhānārammaṇatthambhe satiyottena <pb ed="P" n="3.0763"/> bandhitabbaṃ. Evamassa taṃ cittaṃ ito cito ca vipphanditvāpi pubbe āciṇṇārammaṇaṃ alabhamānaṃ satiyottaṃ chinditvā palāyituṃ asakkontaṃ tamevārammaṇaṃ upacārappanāvasena upanisīdati ceva upanipajjati ca. Tenāhu porāṇā –</p> <hi rend="bold">Araññagato vā rukkhamūlagato vā suññāgāragato vā</hi> <pb ed="P" n="3.0763"/> <p rend="gatha1">‘‘Yathā thambhe nibandheyya, vacchaṃ damaṃ naro idha;</p> <p rend="gathalast">Bandheyyevaṃ sakaṃ cittaṃ, satiyārammaṇe daḷha’’nti.</p> <p rend="bodytext">Evamassetaṃ <pb ed="M" n="2.0354"/> senāsanaṃ bhāvanānurūpaṃ hoti. Tena vuttaṃ ‘‘idamassa satipaṭṭhānabhāvanānurūpasenāsanapariggahaparidīpana’’nti.</p> <pb ed="M" n="2.0354"/> <p rend="bodytext">Apica yasmā idaṃ kāyānupassanāya muddhabhūtaṃ sabbabuddhapaccekabuddhasāvakānaṃ visesādhigamadiṭṭhadhammasukhavihārapadaṭṭhānaṃ ānāpānassatikammaṭṭhānaṃ itthipurisahatthiassādisaddasamākulaṃ gāmantaṃ apariccajitvā na sukaraṃ sampādetuṃ, saddakaṇḍakattā jhānassa. Agāmake pana araññe sukaraṃ yogāvacarena idaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ pariggahetvā ānāpānacatutthajjhānaṃ nibbattetvā tadeva <pb ed="V" n="2.0318"/> jhānaṃ pādakaṃ katvā saṅkhāre sammasitvā aggaphalaṃ arahattaṃ pāpuṇituṃ, tasmāssa anurūpasenāsanaṃ dassento bhagavā, ‘‘araññagato vā’’tiādimāha.</p> <pb ed="V" n="2.0318"/> <p rend="bodytext">Vatthuvijjācariyo viya hi bhagavā. So yathā vatthuvijjācariyo nagarabhūmiṃ passitvā suṭṭhu upaparikkhitvā ‘‘ettha nagaraṃ māpethā’’ti upadisati, sotthinā ca nagare niṭṭhite rājakulato mahāsakkāraṃ labhati, evameva yogāvacarassa anurūpasenāsanaṃ upaparikkhitvā ‘‘ettha kammaṭṭhānamanuyuñjitabba’’nti upadisati, tato tattha kammaṭṭhānamanuyuñjantena yoginā anukkamena arahatte patte ‘‘sammāsambuddho vata so bhagavā’’ti mahantaṃ sakkāraṃ labhati.</p> <p rend="bodytext">Ayaṃ pana bhikkhu dīpisadisoti vuccati. Yathā hi mahādīpirājā araññe tiṇagahanaṃ vā vanagahanaṃ vā pabbatagahanaṃ vā nissāya nilīyitvā vanamahiṃsagokaṇṇasūkarādayo mige gaṇhāti, evameva ayaṃ araññādīsu kammaṭṭhānaṃ anuyuñjanto <pb ed="P" n="3.0764"/> bhikkhu yathākkamena cattāro magge ceva cattāri ariyaphalāni ca gaṇhāti. Tenāhu porāṇā –</p> <pb ed="P" n="3.0764"/> <p rend="gatha1">‘‘Yathāpi dīpiko nāma, nilīyitvā gaṇhatī mige;</p> <p rend="gatha2">Tathevāyaṃ buddhaputto, yuttayogo vipassako;</p> <p rend="gathalast">Araññaṃ pavisitvāna, gaṇhāti phalamuttama’’nti.</p> <p rend="bodytext">Tenassa parakkamajavayoggabhūmiṃ araññasenāsanaṃ dassento bhagavā ‘‘araññagato vā’’tiādimāha. Ito paraṃ imasmiṃ ānāpānapabbe yaṃ vattabbaṃ siyā, taṃ visuddhimagge vuttameva. <hi rend="bold">Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, dakkho bhamakāro vā</hi>ti idañhi upamāmattameva <hi rend="bold">iti ajjhattaṃ vā kāye</hi>ti idaṃ appanāmattameva ca tattha anāgataṃ, sesaṃ āgatameva.</p> <hi rend="bold">Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, dakkho bhamakāro vā</hi> <hi rend="bold">iti ajjhattaṃ vā kāye</hi> <p rend="bodytext">Yaṃ <pb ed="M" n="2.0355"/> pana anāgataṃ, tattha <hi rend="bold">dakkho</hi>ti cheko. <hi rend="bold">Dīghaṃ vā añchanto</hi>ti mahantānaṃ bherīpokkharādīnaṃ likhanakāle hatthe ca pāde ca pasāretvā dīghaṃ kaḍḍhanto. <hi rend="bold">Rassaṃ vā añchanto</hi>ti khuddakānaṃ dantasūcivedhakādīnaṃ likhanakāle mandamandaṃ rassaṃ kaḍḍhanto. <hi rend="bold">Evameva kho</hi>ti evaṃ ayampi bhikkhu addhānavasena ittaravasena ca pavattānaṃ assāsapassāsānaṃ vasena dīghaṃ vā assasanto dīghaṃ assasāmīti pajānāti…pe… passasissāmīti sikkhatīti. Tassevaṃ sikkhato assāsapassāsanimitte cattāri jhānāni uppajjanti, so jhānā vuṭṭhahitvā assāsapassāse vā pariggaṇhāti jhānaṅgāni vā.</p> <pb ed="M" n="2.0355"/> <hi rend="bold">dakkho</hi> <hi rend="bold">Dīghaṃ vā añchanto</hi> <hi rend="bold">Rassaṃ vā añchanto</hi> <hi rend="bold">Evameva kho</hi> <p rend="bodytext">Tattha <pb ed="V" n="2.0319"/> assāsapassāsakammiko ‘‘ime assāsapassāsā kiṃ nissitā? Vatthunissitā. Vatthu nāma karajakāyo, karajakāyo nāma cattāri mahābhūtāni upādārūpañce’’ti evaṃ rūpaṃ pariggaṇhāti. Tato tadārammaṇe phassapañcamake nāmanti. Evaṃ nāmarūpaṃ pariggahetvā tassa paccayaṃ pariyesanto avijjādipaṭiccasamuppādaṃ disvā ‘‘paccayapaccayuppannadhammamattamevetaṃ, añño satto vā puggalo vā natthī’’ti <pb ed="P" n="3.0765"/> vitiṇṇakaṅkho sappaccayanāmarūpe tilakkhaṇaṃ āropetvā vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhento anukkamena arahattaṃ pāpuṇāti. Idaṃ ekassa bhikkhuno yāva arahattā niyyānamukhaṃ.</p> <pb ed="V" n="2.0319"/> <pb ed="P" n="3.0765"/> <p rend="bodytext">Jhānakammikopi ‘‘imāni jhānaṅgāni kiṃ nissitāni, vatthunissitāni, vatthu nāma karajakāyo jhānaṅgāni nāmaṃ, karajakāyo rūpa’’nti nāmarūpaṃ vavatthapetvā tassa paccayaṃ pariyesanto avijjādipaccayākāraṃ disvā ‘‘paccayapaccayuppannadhammamattamevetaṃ, añño satto vā puggalo vā natthī’’ti vitiṇṇakaṅkho sappaccayanāmarūpe tilakkhaṇaṃ āropetvā vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhento anukkamena arahattaṃ pāpuṇāti. Idamekassa bhikkhuno yāva arahattā niyyānamukhaṃ.</p> <p rend="bodytext"><hi rend="bold">Iti ajjhattaṃ vā</hi>ti evaṃ attano vā assāsapassāsakāye kāyānupassī viharati. <hi rend="bold">Bahiddhā vā</hi>ti parassa vā assāsapassāsakāye. <hi rend="bold">Ajjhattabahiddhā vā</hi>ti kālena attano, kālena parassa assāsapassāsakāye. Etenassa paguṇakammaṭṭhānaṃ aṭṭhapetvā aparāparaṃ sañcaraṇakālo kathito. Ekasmiṃ kāle panidaṃ ubhayaṃ na labbhati.</p> <hi rend="bold">Iti ajjhattaṃ vā</hi> <hi rend="bold">Bahiddhā vā</hi> <hi rend="bold">Ajjhattabahiddhā vā</hi> <p rend="bodytext"><hi rend="bold">Samudayadhammānupassī</hi><pb ed="M" n="2.0356"/><hi rend="bold">vā</hi>ti yathā nāma kammārassa bhastañca gaggaranāḷiñca tajjañca vāyāmaṃ paṭicca vāto aparāparaṃ sañcarati, evaṃ bhikkhuno karajakāyañca nāsapuṭañca cittañca paṭicca assāsapassāsakāyo aparāparaṃ sañcarati. Kāyādayo dhammā samudayadhammā, te passanto ‘‘samudayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharatī’’ti vuccati. <hi rend="bold">Vayadhammānupassī vā</hi>ti yathā bhastāya apanītāya gaggaranāḷiyā bhinnāya tajje ca vāyāme asati so vāto nappavattati, evameva kāye bhinne nāsapuṭe viddhaste citte ca niruddhe assāsapassāsakāyo nāma nappavattatīti kāyādinirodhā assāsapassāsanirodhoti evaṃ passanto ‘‘vayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharatī’’ti vuccati. <hi rend="bold">Samudayavayadhammānupassī vā</hi>ti kālena samudayaṃ kālena vayaṃ anupassanto. <hi rend="bold">Atthi kāyoti vā panassā</hi>ti kāyova atthi, na satto, na puggalo, na itthī, na puriso, na attā, na attaniyaṃ, nāhaṃ, na mama, na koci, na kassacīti evamassa sati paccupaṭṭhitā hoti.</p> <hi rend="bold">Samudayadhammānupassī</hi> <pb ed="M" n="2.0356"/> <hi rend="bold">vā</hi> <hi rend="bold">Vayadhammānupassī vā</hi> <hi rend="bold">Samudayavayadhammānupassī vā</hi> <hi rend="bold">Atthi kāyoti vā panassā</hi> <p rend="bodytext"><hi rend="bold">Yāvadevā</hi>ti <pb ed="V" n="2.0320"/><pb ed="P" n="3.0766"/> payojanaparicchedavavatthāpanametaṃ. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – yā sā sati paccupaṭṭhitā hoti, sā na aññadatthāya. Atha kho yāvadeva ñāṇamattāya aparāparaṃ uttaruttari ñāṇapamāṇatthāya ceva satipamāṇatthāya ca, satisampajaññānaṃ vuḍḍhatthāyāti attho. <hi rend="bold">Anissito ca viharatī</hi>ti taṇhānissayadiṭṭhinissayānaṃ vasena anissitova viharati. <hi rend="bold">Na ca kiñci loke upādiyatī</hi>ti lokasmiṃ kiñci rūpaṃ vā…pe… viññāṇaṃ vā ‘‘ayaṃ me attā vā attaniyaṃ vā’’ti na gaṇhāti. <hi rend="bold">Evampī</hi>ti upari atthaṃ upādāya sampiṇḍanattho pi-kāro. Iminā pana padena bhagavā ānāpānapabbadesanaṃ niyyātetvā dasseti.</p> <hi rend="bold">Yāvadevā</hi> <pb ed="V" n="2.0320"/> <pb ed="P" n="3.0766"/> <hi rend="bold">Anissito ca viharatī</hi> <hi rend="bold">Na ca kiñci loke upādiyatī</hi> <hi rend="bold">Evampī</hi> <p rend="bodytext">Tattha assāsapassāsapariggāhikā sati dukkhasaccaṃ, tassā samuṭṭhāpikā purimataṇhā samudayasaccaṃ ubhinnaṃ appavatti nirodhasaccaṃ, dukkhaparijānano samudayapajahano nirodhārammaṇo ariyamaggo maggasaccaṃ. Evaṃ catusaccavasena ussakkitvā nibbutiṃ pāpuṇātīti idamekassa assāsapassāsavasena abhiniviṭṭhassa bhikkhuno yāva arahattā niyyānamukhanti.</p> <p rend="centre">Ānāpānapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.</p> <p rend="subhead">Iriyāpathapabbavaṇṇanā</p>
374. Now, just as a clever worker in mat and basket weaving, wishing to make coarse and fine mats, *caṅkoṭaka* boxes, cases, and the like, should get a mammoth bamboo, split it into four, and then take each bamboo section, split it further, and make that particular implement, just so, the Blessed One, wishing to enable beings to attain various kinds of special knowledge by way of the exposition of the Arousing of Mindfulness, divided into four the one mindfulness that is right (*sammāsatiṃ*) by way of the four arousings of mindfulness (*cattāro satipaṭṭhānā*).

What are the four? Here, monks, a monk dwells contemplating the body in the body (*kāye kāyānupassī viharati*), and so on. In this way, having divided [mindfulness] into four according to the object, then taking each arousing of mindfulness, and analyzing the body, he began to state the section on exposition by way of the method beginning with "And how, monks?" (*kathañca bhikkhave*).

There, the question beginning with "And how?" is a desire for detailed explanation.

Here, in brief, is the meaning: Monks, by what means does a monk dwell contemplating the body in the body? This is the method in all sections of questioning.

"Here, monks, a monk (*idha bhikkhave bhikkhū*)" means, monks, a monk in this Dispensation (*imasmiṃ sāsane*).

For in this context, the word "here" (*idha*) signifies the Dispensation of the Buddha as the basis for the person producing body-contemplation in all modes, and it denies that such a state exists in any other dispensation.

For it was said, "Only here, monks, is there an ascetic… empty are the other, opposing ways of thought with ascetics" (M.i.139).

Therefore, it was said, "Monks in this Dispensation (*imasmiṃ sāsane bhikkhū*)."

And how? "Gone to the forest, or to the foot of a tree, or to an empty place (*araññagato vā rukkhamūlagato vā suññāgāragato vā*)," this signifies that he has found an abode appropriate to the meditator for the culture of mindfulness (*satipaṭṭhānabhāvanānurūpasenāsanapariggahaparidīpanaṃ*).

For the mind of this monk has long been dissipated among visible data, etc., as its object, and it does not want to enter the road of meditation; it runs off the track like a chariot harnessed to a wild ox.

Therefore, just as a cowherd, wishing to tame a wild calf reared on the milk of a wild cow, after leading it away from the cow, ties it with a rope to a stout post dug into the ground in a secluded spot, so too, when a monk wants to tame his own wicked mind which has long been nourished on the taste of visible data, etc., as object for its food and drink, he should lead it away from visible and other objects and, having entered a forest or the foot of a tree or an empty place, tie it there with the rope of mindfulness to the post of the object of mindfulness-arousing.

And so his mind, even though it may dash to and fro, no longer getting the objects it was formerly used to, and finding it impossible to break the rope of mindfulness and get away, sits down, lies down, by that very object under the influence of access and absorption.

Therefore, the ancients said:

"Just as to a post one should tether a calf for taming,
So should one bind one's own mind firmly to the object of mindfulness."

In this way, this abode becomes appropriate in the meditator.

Therefore, it was said, "This signifies that he has found an abode favorable to the development of mindfulness."

Moreover, since it is not easy to bring to perfection this mindfulness of breathing meditation subject (*ānāpānassatikammaṭṭhānaṃ*), which is the chief thing in contemplation of the body, the basis for the attainment of special knowledge and a pleasant abiding in the here and now for all Buddhas, Paccekabuddhas, and disciples, without abandoning a village boundary crowded with the sounds of women, men, elephants, horses, etc., because sound is a thorn to absorption.

But in a non-village forest it is easy for the yogin, having taken up this meditation subject, to produce the fourth *jhāna* of mindfulness of breathing, and having made that very *jhāna* a foundation, to comprehend the *saṃkhāras* and attain the supreme fruit, Arahantship; therefore, the Blessed One, in pointing out an abode appropriate to him, said, "Gone to the forest," and so on.

For the Blessed One is like a master of the art of building sites. Just as a master of the art of building sites, having seen the proposed site for a town, thoroughly examines it, and directs, "Build the town here," and when the town is safely finished, he receives great honor from the royal family, so the Blessed One examines an abode as to its suitability for the meditator, and directs, "Devote yourself to the meditation subject here," and later on, when the meditator has devoted himself to the meditation subject there and has attained Arahantship in due course, he receives great honor, [thinking,] "Verily, the Blessed One was a *sammāsambuddho*."

This monk, however, is called like a leopard.

Just as a great leopard king lurks in a grass wilderness or a jungle wilderness or a rock wilderness in the forest and seizes wild beasts—the wild buffalo, wild ox, boar, etc.—so too, the monk who devotes himself to his meditation subject in the forest, etc., gains successively the four paths and the four noble fruits.

Therefore, the ancients said:

"Just as a leopard, lurking, seizes wild beasts,
So too, this son of the Buddha, yoked to yoga, an observer,
Having entered the forest, gains the supreme fruit."

Therefore, the Blessed One, in pointing out a forest abode as a place likely to hasten his advancement toward effort, swiftness, and fitness, said "Gone to the forest," and so on.
Hereafter in this section on mindfulness of breathing, what should be said has already been stated in the *Visuddhimagga*.

Just as, monks, a clever turner or... This is just a simile. "...either internally in the body," this is just an application. And there, the future is not discussed; only the past is.

Just as, monks, a clever turner or... "...either internally in the body..." What is not discussed is the future; there, "clever" means skilled.

"When drawing a long [line]," that means at the time of drawing large kettle drums etc., stretching out the hands and feet, he draws a long [line].

"When drawing a short [line]," that means at the time of drawing small toothpicks, needles, etc., slowly he draws a short [line].

"Just so, indeed," that means in this way also, the monk, knowing, "When breathing in long, I am breathing in long," because of the duration and because of the limited nature of the breaths that are occurring... He trains, "...I will breathe out"....

To him thus training, with regard to the sign of the in-breath and the out-breath, the four *jhānas* arise. Then, having emerged from those *jhānas*, he either examines the in-breath and the out-breath, or the factors of *jhāna*.

There, the practitioner of mindfulness of breathing [thinks]: "Supported by what are these in-breaths and out-breaths? Supported by a basis."

*Vatthu* means the coarse body (*karajakāya*). The coarse body means the four great elements (*mahābhūta*) and derived materiality (*upādārūpa*). Thus he cognizes form (*rūpa*).

Then, with regard to that object, he names the pentad of sense-impression (*phassa*).

Thus, having grasped name and form (*nāmarūpa*), seeking its cause, seeing dependent origination (*paṭiccasamuppāda*) beginning with ignorance (*avijjā*), [and realizing] "This is merely a mass of phenomena arisen from causes and conditions; there is no other being or person," having dispelled doubt, establishing the three characteristics on name and form together with their conditions, developing insight (*vipassanā*), in due course, he attains arahantship.

This is the portal to emancipation up to arahantship for a certain monk.

The practitioner of *jhāna* also [thinks]: "Supported by what are these factors of *jhāna*? Supported by a basis. *Vatthu* means the coarse body (*karajakāya*). The factors of *jhāna* are name (*nāma*), the coarse body is form (*rūpa*)." Having determined name and form in this way, seeking its cause, seeing the causal pattern beginning with ignorance (*avijjā*), [and realizing] "This is merely a mass of phenomena arisen from causes and conditions; there is no other being or person," having dispelled doubt, establishing the three characteristics on name and form together with their conditions, developing insight (*vipassanā*), in due course, he attains arahantship.

This is the portal to emancipation up to arahantship for a certain monk.

"...either internally," that is, in this way, one dwells contemplating the body in the body of one's own in-breath and out-breath.

"...or externally," that is, in the body of the in-breath and out-breath of another.

"...or both internally and externally," that is, at one time in one's own, at another time in the body of the in-breath and out-breath of another.

By this, the occasion for the arising of skill in the practice has been explained, and the time for wandering has been spoken of.

But at one time, both are not obtained.

"...either internally," "...or externally," "...or both internally and externally," "...contemplating origination-phenomena:" Just as, namely, dependent on the bellows of a smith, and the leathern tube, and appropriate effort, the wind moves back and forth, so likewise, dependent on the coarse body of the monk, and the nostrils, and the mind, the body of the in-breath and out-breath moves back and forth.

The things beginning with the body are origination-phenomena. Seeing them, it is said, "He dwells contemplating origination-phenomena in the body."

"...contemplating dissolution-phenomena:" Just as when the skin of the bellows has been removed, and the leathern tube is broken, and appropriate effort is lacking, that wind does not proceed, just so, when the body is broken, and the nostrils are destroyed, and the mind has ceased, the body of the in-breath and out-breath does not proceed. Thus, seeing the cessation of the in-breath and out-breath as [resulting] from the cessation of the body etc., it is said, "He dwells contemplating dissolution-phenomena in the body."

"...contemplating origination-and-dissolution phenomena," that means contemplating origination at one time, and dissolution at another time.

"...'there is a body'," or indeed, for him, there is just a body, not a being, not a person, not a woman, not a man, not a self (*ātmā*), not something belonging to a self, not "I," not "mine," not anyone, not belonging to anyone. Thus mindfulness is present to him.

"...contemplating origination-phenomena," "...either contemplating dissolution-phenomena," "...contemplating origination-and-dissolution phenomena," "...'there is a body'," "to the extent necessary," this establishes the demarcation of purpose.

This is said: That mindfulness that is present is not for another purpose.

But rather, just for a measure of knowledge, for a greater and greater, higher and higher measure of knowledge, and for a measure of mindfulness, that is, for the increase of mindfulness and clear comprehension, is the meaning.

"And he dwells unattached," that is, he dwells unattached by way of attachment to craving (*taṇhā*) and attachment to views (*diṭṭhi*).

"And he clings to nothing in the world," that is, in the world, he does not grasp any form (*rūpa*) or...or consciousness (*vijñāṇa*) [thinking], "This is my self (*ātmā*) or something belonging to a self."

"Even so," that means the *pi*-sound is for collecting together the meaning based on the foregoing.
However, with this passage, the Blessed One, having brought forth the teaching on the ānāpāna section, shows it.

“As long as one dwells without relying [on anything] and does not cling to anything in the world," even in this way...

There, mindfulness that takes hold of the in-breath and out-breath is the truth of suffering (duḥkhasatya). The former craving that gives rise to it is the truth of origination (samudayasatya). The non-occurrence of both is the truth of cessation (nirodhasatya). The noble path (āryamārga) whose object is cessation, abandoning origination, understanding suffering, is the truth of the path (mārgasatya).

Thus, having striven by way of the Four Truths (catursatya), a person arrives at peace (nirvṛti). This is the portal to emancipation (niyyānamukha) up to arahantship of the bhikkhu devoted to meditation on breathing.

The Ānāpāna Section is finished.

Explanation of the Iriyāpatha Section
375
<p n="375" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">375</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Puna <pb ed="V" n="2.0216"/> caparaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu gacchanto vā ‘gacchāmī’ti pajānāti, ṭhito vā ‘ṭhitomhī’ti pajānāti, nisinno vā ‘nisinnomhī’ti pajānāti, sayāno vā ‘sayānomhī’ti pajānāti, yathā yathā vā panassa kāyo paṇihito hoti, tathā tathā naṃ pajānāti. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati, bahiddhā vā kāye kāyānupassī <pb ed="M" n="2.0233"/> viharati, ajjhattabahiddhā vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati. Samudayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati, vayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati, samudayavayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati. ‘Atthi kāyo’ti vā panassa sati paccupaṭṭhitā hoti yāvadeva ñāṇamattāya paṭissatimattāya anissito ca viharati, na ca kiñci loke upādiyati. Evampi kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati.</p> <p rend="centre">Iriyāpathapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.</p> <p rend="subhead">Kāyānupassanā sampajānapabbaṃ</p>
“Furthermore, *bhikkhus*, when walking, a *bhikkhu* understands, ‘I am walking’; when standing, he understands, ‘I am standing’; when sitting, he understands, ‘I am sitting’; when lying down, he understands, ‘I am lying down’; or he understands accordingly however his body (*kāya*) is disposed.

In this way, he abides contemplating the body (*kāya*) as a body (*kāya*) internally (*ajjhattaṃ*), or he abides contemplating the body (*kāya*) as a body (*kāya*) externally (*bahiddhā*), or he abides contemplating the body (*kāya*) as a body (*kāya*) both internally and externally (*ajjhattabahiddhā*).

Or he abides contemplating arising phenomena (*samudayadhamma*) in the body (*kāyasmiṃ*), contemplating vanishing phenomena (*vayadhamma*) in the body (*kāyasmiṃ*), he abides contemplating both arising and vanishing phenomena (*samudayavayadhamma*) in the body (*kāyasmiṃ*).

Or else, mindfulness that “there is body (*kāyo*)” is present to him just to the extent necessary for knowledge (*ñāṇamattāya*) and awareness (*paṭissatimattāya*). And he abides independent (*anissito*), not clinging to anything in the world (*loke upādiyati*).

That is how a *bhikkhu*, abides contemplating the body (*kāya*) as a body (*kāya*).

The section on deportment is concluded.

The section on mindfulness of the body (*kāyānupassanā sampajānapabbaṃ*).
<p n="375" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">375</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> Evaṃ <pb ed="M" n="2.0357"/> assāsapassāsavasena kāyānupassanaṃ vibhajitvā idāni iriyāpathavasena vibhajituṃ <hi rend="bold">puna capara</hi>ntiādimāha. Tattha kāmaṃ soṇasiṅgālādayopi gacchantā ‘‘gacchāmā’’ti jānanti, na panetaṃ evarūpaṃ jānanaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ. Evarūpañhi jānanaṃ sattūpaladdhiṃ na pajahati, attasaññaṃ na ugghāṭeti, kammaṭṭhānaṃ vā satipaṭṭhānabhāvanā vā na hoti. Imassa pana bhikkhuno jānanaṃ sattūpaladdhiṃ pajahati, attasaññaṃ ugghāṭeti kammaṭṭhānañceva satipaṭṭhānabhāvanā ca hoti. Idañhi ‘‘ko gacchati, kassa gamanaṃ, kiṃ kāraṇā gacchatī’’ti evaṃ sampajānanaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ. Ṭhānādīsupi eseva nayo.</p> <p rend="bodytext">Tattha ko gacchatīti? Na koci satto vā puggalo vā gacchati. Kassa gamananti? Na kassaci sattassa vā puggalassa vā gamanaṃ. Kiṃ kāraṇā gacchatīti <pb ed="P" n="3.0767"/>? Cittakiriyavāyodhātuvipphārena gacchati. Tasmā esa evaṃ pajānāti – ‘‘gacchāmī’’ti cittaṃ uppajjati <pb ed="V" n="2.0321"/>, taṃ vāyaṃ janeti, vāyo viññattiṃ janeti, cittakiriyavāyodhātuvipphārena sakalakāyassa purato abhinīhāro gamananti vuccati. Ṭhānādīsupi eseva nayo.</p> <pb ed="P" n="3.0767"/> <pb ed="V" n="2.0321"/> <p rend="bodytext">Tatrāpi hi ‘‘tiṭṭhāmī’’ti cittaṃ uppajjati, taṃ vāyaṃ janeti, vāyo viññattiṃ janeti, cittakiriyavāyodhātuvipphārena sakalakāyassa koṭito paṭṭhāya ussitabhāvo ṭhānanti vuccati. ‘‘Nisīdāmī’’ti cittaṃ uppajjati, taṃ vāyaṃ janeti, vāyo viññattiṃ janeti, cittakiriyavāyodhātuvipphārena heṭṭhimakāyassa samiñjanaṃ uparimakāyassa ussitabhāvo nisajjāti vuccati. ‘‘Sayāmī’’ti cittaṃ uppajjati, taṃ vāyaṃ janeti, vāyo viññattiṃ janeti, cittakiriyavāyodhātuvipphārena sakalasarīrassa tiriyato pasāraṇaṃ sayananti vuccatīti.</p> <p rend="bodytext">Tassa evaṃ pajānato evaṃ hoti ‘‘satto gacchati, satto tiṭṭhatī’’ti vuccati, atthato pana koci satto gacchanto vā ṭhito vā natthi. Yathā pana ‘‘sakaṭaṃ gacchati, sakaṭaṃ tiṭṭhatī’’ti vuccati, na ca kiñci sakaṭaṃ nāma gacchantaṃ vā ṭhitaṃ vā atthi, cattāro pana goṇe yojetvā chekamhi sārathimhi pājente ‘‘sakaṭaṃ gacchati, sakaṭaṃ tiṭṭhatī’’ti vohāramattameva <pb ed="M" n="2.0358"/> hoti, evameva ajānanaṭṭhena sakaṭaṃ viya kāyo, goṇā viya cittajavātā, sārathi viya cittaṃ. ‘‘Gacchāmi tiṭṭhāmī’’ti citte uppanne vāyodhātu viññattiṃ janayamānā uppajjati, cittakiriyavāyodhātuvipphārena gamanādīni pavattanti, tato ‘‘satto gacchati, satto tiṭṭhati, ahaṃ gacchāmi, ahaṃ tiṭṭhāmī’’ti vohāramattaṃ hoti. Tenāha –</p> <pb ed="M" n="2.0358"/> <p rend="gatha1">‘‘Nāvā mālutavegena, jiyāvegena tejanaṃ;</p> <p rend="gathalast">Yathā yāti tathā kāyo, yāti vātāhato ayaṃ.</p> <p rend="gatha1">Yantaṃ <pb ed="P" n="3.0768"/> suttavaseneva, cittasuttavasenidaṃ;</p> <pb ed="P" n="3.0768"/> <p rend="gathalast">Payuttaṃ kāyayantampi, yāti ṭhāti nisīdati.</p> <p rend="gatha1">Ko nāma ettha so satto, yo vinā hetupaccaye;</p> <p rend="gathalast">Attano ānubhāvena, tiṭṭhe vā yadi vā vaje’’ti.</p> <p rend="bodytext">Tasmā evaṃ hetupaccayavaseneva pavattāni gamanādīni sallakkhento esa ‘‘gacchanto vā gacchāmīti <pb ed="V" n="2.0322"/> pajānāti, ṭhito vā, nisinno vā, sayāno vā sayānomhīti pajānātī’’ti veditabbo.</p> <pb ed="V" n="2.0322"/> <p rend="bodytext"><hi rend="bold">Yathā yathā vā panassa kāyo paṇihito hoti, tathā tathā naṃ pajānātī</hi>ti sabbasaṅgāhikavacanametaṃ. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – yena yena vā ākārenassa kāyo ṭhito hoti, tena tena naṃ pajānāti. Gamanākārena ṭhitaṃ gacchatīti pajānāti. Ṭhānanisajjasayanākārena ṭhitaṃ sayānoti pajānātīti.</p> <hi rend="bold">Yathā yathā vā panassa kāyo paṇihito hoti, tathā tathā naṃ pajānātī</hi> <p rend="bodytext"><hi rend="bold">Iti ajjhattaṃ vā</hi>ti evaṃ attano vā catuiriyāpathapariggaṇhanena kāye kāyānupassī viharati. <hi rend="bold">Bahiddhā vā</hi>ti parassa vā catuiriyāpathapariggaṇhanena. <hi rend="bold">Ajjhattabahiddhā vā</hi>ti kālena attano, kālena parassa catuiriyāpathapariggaṇhanena kāye kāyānupassī viharati. <hi rend="bold">Samudayadhammānupassī vā</hi>tiādīsu pana avijjāsamudayā rūpasamudayotiādinā nayena pañcahākārehi rūpakkhandhassa samudayo ca vayo ca nīharitabbo. Tañhi sandhāya idha ‘‘samudayadhammānupassī vā’’tiādi vuttaṃ. <hi rend="bold">Atthi kāyoti vā panassā</hi>tiādi vuttasadisameva.</p> <hi rend="bold">Iti ajjhattaṃ vā</hi> <hi rend="bold">Bahiddhā vā</hi> <hi rend="bold">Ajjhattabahiddhā vā</hi> <hi rend="bold">Samudayadhammānupassī vā</hi> <hi rend="bold">Atthi kāyoti vā panassā</hi> <p rend="bodytext">Idhāpi <pb ed="M" n="2.0359"/> catuiriyāpathapariggāhikā sati dukkhasaccaṃ, tassā samuṭṭhāpikā purimataṇhā samudayasaccaṃ, ubhinnaṃ appavatti nirodhasaccaṃ, dukkhaparijānano samudayapajahano nirodhārammaṇo ariyamaggo maggasaccaṃ. Evaṃ catusaccavasena ussakkitvā nibbutiṃ pāpuṇātīti idamekassa catuiriyāpathapariggāhakassa bhikkhuno yāva arahattā niyyānamukhanti.</p> <pb ed="M" n="2.0359"/> <p rend="centre">Iriyāpathapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.</p> <p rend="subhead">Catusampajaññapabbavaṇṇanā</p>
375. Thus, having divided the contemplation of the body as a body by way of inhalation and exhalation, now to divide it by way of the deportment, he says, beginning with, "Again, moreover."

There, though dogs, jackals, and so on, while going, know "we are going," this is not said referring to that kind of awareness.

Indeed, awareness of that sort does not relinquish belief in a being, does not knock out the perception of a soul, and neither becomes a subject of meditation nor the development of the Arousing of Mindfulness.

But the knowledge of this *bhikkhu* casts out the belief in a being, uproots the perception of a being, is a subject of meditation and is the cultivation of the Arousing of Mindfulness.

Indeed, these words, "Who goes? Whose going is it? On account of what does the going take place?" are said referring to the knowledge of the going of the meditating *bhikkhu*.

The same is the method of exposition as regards the other postures: standing and so forth.

There, who goes? No living being or person whatsoever goes.

Whose going is it? Not the going of any living being or person.

On account of what does the going take place? On account of the diffusion of the process of oscillation born of mental activity.

Therefore, this yogi knows thus: If there arises the thought, "I shall go," that thought produces the process of oscillation; the process of oscillation produces expression. The moving on of the whole body through the diffusion of the process of oscillation is called going.

The same is the method of exposition as regards the other postures: standing and so forth.

There, too, if there arises the thought, "I shall stand," that thought produces the process of oscillation. The process of oscillation produces bodily expression. The raising upright of the whole body from below owing to the diffusion of the process of oscillation is called standing.

If there arises the thought "I shall sit," that thought produces the process of oscillation. The process of oscillation produces bodily expression. The bending of the lower part of the body and the raising upright of the upper part of the body owing to the diffusion of the process of oscillation is called sitting.

If there arises the thought, "I shall lie down," that thought produces the process of oscillation. The process of oscillation produces bodily expression. The straightening or the spreading of the whole body horizontally or across, owing to the diffusion of the process of oscillation, is called lying down.

To him who thus knows, it occurs thus: “A being goes, a being stands,” is said, but in reality, no being whatsoever goes or stands.

Just as it is said, "A cart goes, a cart stands," and there is no such thing as a cart that goes or stands, but when four oxen are yoked and a clever driver is driving, it is merely a verbal convention that "A cart goes, a cart stands." Just so, owing to not knowing, the body is like a cart, the winds born of mental activity are like oxen, and the mind is like a driver.

When there arises the thought, "I shall go, I shall stand," the wind element arises generating expression. Through the diffusion of the process of oscillation born of mental activity, going and so forth occur; therefore, "A being goes, a being stands, I go, I stand" is merely a verbal convention.

Therefore, he said:

"As a ship with the force of the wind, propelled by the force of a bowstring,

so the body goes, struck by the wind."

"Just as a machine goes by the power of string, this is by the power of mental string;

the body-machine, being applied, goes, stands, sits."

"Who is that being here, who, without causes and conditions,

would stand or would go by the power of himself?"

Accordingly this yogi, who considers by way of causes and conditions, the states of going, standing and so forth, knows well that he is going, when he is in the state of going, that he is standing when he stands, that he is sitting when he sits, and that he is lying down when he lies down, as it is told in the passage in the discourse beginning with the words: "When he is going, a *bhikkhu* understands: 'I am going.'"

Or, in whatever way his body is disposed, he knows it is (disposed) in that way—this is a comprehensive statement.

This is what is said: In whatever way his body is disposed, in that way he knows it.

He knows it is going—the body is disposed in the mode of going.

He knows it is lying down—the body is disposed in the mode of standing, sitting or lying down.

Or, in whatever way his body is disposed, he knows it is (disposed) in that way, that is, either internally, thus examining his own four modes of deportment, he dwells contemplating the body as a body;

or externally, that is, examining the four modes of deportment of another;

or both internally and externally, that is, at one time examining his own, at another time another's four modes of deportment, he dwells contemplating the body as a body.
But in "<hi rend="bold">samudayadhammānupassī vā</hi>tiādi," the arising of the form aggregate and its passing away should be determined in five ways by the method beginning with "with the arising of ignorance, the arising of form."
Here, "<hi rend="bold">samudayadhammānupassī vā</hi>tiādi" is stated with that in mind.

"<hi rend="bold">atthi kāyoti vā panassā</hi>" is just like what was stated.

"<hi rend="bold">iti ajjhattaṃ vā</hi> <hi rend="bold">bahiddhā vā</hi> <hi rend="bold">ajjhattabahiddhā vā</hi> <hi rend="bold">samudayadhammānupassī vā</hi> <hi rend="bold">atthi kāyoti vā panassā</hi>

Here also, the mindfulness which examines the four modes of deportment is the *duḥkhasatya* (duḥkhasaccaṃ), the truth of suffering; the pre-craving which brings about that mindfulness is the *samudayasatya* (samudayasaccaṃ), the truth of origination; the non-occurrence of both is the *nirodhasatya* (nirodhasaccaṃ), the truth of cessation; the *āryamārga* (ariyamaggo), the Noble Path, which understands suffering, abandons origination, and takes cessation as its object, is the *mārgasatya* (maggasaccaṃ), the truth of the path.

Thus, the *bhikṣu* (bhikkhuno) having endeavored by way of the Four Truths (*catusaccavasena*), reaches *nirvāṇa* (nibbutiṃ). This is indeed the means of deliverance (*niyyānamukhanti*) up to *arhatship* (*arahattā*) of one who lays hold of the four modes of deportment (*catuiriyāpathapariggāhakassa*).

The section on deportment is finished.

Explanation of the section on the fourfold clear comprehension (*catusampajaññapabbavaṇṇanā*).
376
<p n="376" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">376</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu abhikkante paṭikkante sampajānakārī hoti, ālokite vilokite sampajānakārī hoti, samiñjite pasārite sampajānakārī hoti, saṅghāṭipattacīvaradhāraṇe sampajānakārī <pb ed="T" n="2.0328"/> hoti, asite pīte khāyite sāyite sampajānakārī hoti, uccārapassāvakamme sampajānakārī hoti, gate ṭhite nisinne sutte jāgarite bhāsite tuṇhībhāve sampajānakārī hoti. Iti <pb ed="P" n="2.0293"/> ajjhattaṃ vā…pe… evampi kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati.</p> <p rend="centre">Sampajānapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.</p> <p rend="subhead">Kāyānupassanā paṭikūlamanasikārapabbaṃ</p>
“Again, *bhikkhus*, a *bhikkhu* acts with full awareness (*sampajānakārī*) when going forward and returning; acts with full awareness when looking ahead and looking away; acts with full awareness when flexing and extending his limbs; acts with full awareness when wearing his robes and carrying his outer robe and bowl (*saṅghāṭipattacīvaradhāraṇe*); acts with full awareness when eating (*asite*), drinking (*pīte*), consuming food (*khāyite*), and tasting (*sāyite*); acts with full awareness when defecating (*uccāra*) or urinating (*passāvakamme*); acts with full awareness when walking (*gate*), standing (*ṭhite*), sitting (*nisinne*), sleeping (*sutte*), waking up (*jāgarite*), talking (*bhāsite*), and remaining silent (*tuṇhībhāve*).

In this way, internally… and so forth… Thus, *bhikkhus*, a *bhikkhu* abides contemplating the body as body.

The section on full awareness is finished.

The section on contemplating the body through repulsiveness.
<p n="376" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">376</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> Evaṃ iriyāpathavasena kāyānupassanaṃ vibhajitvā idāni catusampajaññavasena vibhajituṃ <hi rend="bold">puna capara</hi>ntiādimāha <pb ed="P" n="3.0769"/>. Tattha <hi rend="bold">abhikkante</hi>tiādīni <hi rend="bold">sāmaññaphale</hi> vaṇṇitāni. <hi rend="bold">Iti ajjhattaṃ vā</hi>ti evaṃ catusampajaññapariggaṇhanena attano vā kāye, parassa vā kāye, kālena vā attano, kālena vā parassa kāye kāyānupassī viharati. Idhāpi <hi rend="bold">samudayavayadhammānupassī</hi>tiādīsu <pb ed="V" n="2.0323"/> rūpakkhandhasseva samudayo ca vayo ca nīharitabbo. Sesaṃ vuttasadisameva.</p> <p rend="bodytext">Idha catusampajaññapariggāhikā sati dukkhasaccaṃ, tassā samuṭṭhāpikā purimataṇhā samudayasaccaṃ, ubhinnaṃ appavatti nirodhasaccaṃ, vuttappakāro ariyamaggo maggasaccaṃ. Evaṃ catusaccavasena ussakkitvā nibbutiṃ pāpuṇātīti idamekassa catusampajaññapariggāhakassa bhikkhuno vasena yāva arahattā niyyānamukhanti.</p> <p rend="centre">Catusampajaññapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.</p> <p rend="subhead">Paṭikūlamanasikārapabbavaṇṇanā</p>
376.
Thus, having analyzed body contemplation by way of the modes of deportment, now, in order to analyze it by way of the four kinds of clear comprehension ( *catu sampajañña*), [the text] begins with, "And further."
There, "In going forwards" and so forth have been described in the section on the fruit of ordinary life.
"Thus internally": Thus, by way of laying hold of the fourfold comprehension, one dwells contemplating the body in one's own body or in another's body, or at one time in one's own body and at another time in another's body.
Here also, in "contemplating the arising and passing away of phenomena" and so forth, the arising and passing away of the aggregate of form should be brought out.
The remainder is just like what has been stated.

Here, the mindfulness which lays hold of the fourfold clear comprehension is the truth of suffering ( *duḥkha-satya*); the prior craving which originates that mindfulness is the truth of origination ( *samudaya-satya*); the non-occurrence of either is the truth of cessation ( *nirodha-satya*); the Noble Path already stated is the truth of the path ( *mārga-satya*).
Thus, having striven by way of the Four Truths ( *catusatya*), one reaches peace. This is indeed the means of deliverance up to *arhat*-ship of one who lays hold of the fourfold clear comprehension.

The chapter on the fourfold clear comprehension is finished.

Explanation of the chapter on attention to repulsiveness.
377
<p n="377" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">377</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Puna <pb ed="V" n="2.0217"/> caparaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu imameva kāyaṃ uddhaṃ pādatalā adho kesamatthakā tacapariyantaṃ pūraṃ nānappakārassa asucino paccavekkhati – ‘atthi imasmiṃ kāye kesā lomā nakhā dantā taco, maṃsaṃ nhāru aṭṭhi aṭṭhimiñjaṃ vakkaṃ, hadayaṃ yakanaṃ kilomakaṃ pihakaṃ papphāsaṃ, antaṃ antaguṇaṃ <pb ed="M" n="2.0234"/> udariyaṃ karīsaṃ <note>karīsaṃ matthaluṅgaṃ (ka.)</note>, pittaṃ semhaṃ pubbo lohitaṃ sedo medo, assu vasā kheḷo siṅghāṇikā lasikā mutta’nti.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, ubhatomukhā putoḷi <note>mūtoḷī (syā.), mutoli (pī.)</note> pūrā nānāvihitassa dhaññassa, seyyathidaṃ sālīnaṃ vīhīnaṃ muggānaṃ māsānaṃ tilānaṃ <pb ed="T" n="2.0329"/> taṇḍulānaṃ. Tamenaṃ cakkhumā puriso muñcitvā paccavekkheyya – ‘ime sālī, ime vīhī ime muggā ime māsā ime tilā ime taṇḍulā’ti. Evameva kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu imameva kāyaṃ uddhaṃ pādatalā adho kesamatthakā tacapariyantaṃ pūraṃ nānappakārassa asucino paccavekkhati – ‘atthi imasmiṃ kāye kesā lomā…pe… <pb ed="P" n="2.0294"/> mutta’nti.</p> <note>mūtoḷī (syā.), mutoli (pī.)</note> <pb ed="T" n="2.0329"/> <pb ed="P" n="2.0294"/> <p rend="bodytext">Iti ajjhattaṃ vā…pe… evampi kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati.</p> <p rend="centre">Paṭikūlamanasikārapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.</p> <p rend="subhead">Kāyānupassanā dhātumanasikārapabbaṃ</p>
Again, bhikkhus, a bhikkhu reviews this same body up from the soles of the feet and down from the top of the hair, bounded by skin, as full of many kinds of impurity thus: ‘In this body there are head-hairs, body-hairs, nails, teeth, skin, flesh, sinews, bones, bone-marrow, kidneys, heart, liver, diaphragm, spleen, lungs, large intestines, small intestines, contents of the stomach, feces ( *karīsaṃ* means filth), bile, phlegm, pus, blood, sweat, fat, tears, grease, spittle, snot, oil of the joints, and urine.’

Just as though there were a bag with an opening at both ends full of many sorts of grain, such as hill rice (*sālīnaṃ*), red rice (*vīhīnaṃ*), mung beans (*muggānaṃ*), kidney beans (*māsānaṃ*), sesame seeds (*tilānaṃ*), and husked rice (*taṇḍulānaṃ*), and a man with good eyesight were to open it and review it thus: ‘This is hill rice, this is red rice, these are mung beans, these are kidney beans, these are sesame seeds, this is husked rice’; so too, a bhikkhu reviews this same body up from the soles of the feet and down from the top of the hair, bounded by skin, as full of many kinds of impurity thus: ‘In this body there are head-hairs…and urine.’

In this way he remains focused internally…and that is how a bhikkhu abides contemplating body as body.

The section on reflection on repulsiveness is finished.

The section on contemplation of the body: reflection on the elements.
<p n="377" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">377</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> Evaṃ catusampajaññavasena kāyānupassanaṃ vibhajitvā idāni paṭikūlamanasikāravasena vibhajituṃ <hi rend="bold">puna capara</hi>ntiādimāha. Tattha <hi rend="bold">imameva kāya</hi>ntiādīsu yaṃ vattabbaṃ siyā, taṃ sabbaṃ sabbākārena vitthārato visuddhimagge kāyagatāsatikammaṭṭhāne vuttaṃ. <hi rend="bold">Ubhatomukhā</hi>ti heṭṭhā ca upari cāti dvīhi mukhehi yuttā. <hi rend="bold">Nānāvihitassā</hi>ti nānāvidhassa.</p> <p rend="bodytext">Idaṃ <pb ed="M" n="2.0360"/> panettha opammasaṃsandanaṃ – ubhatomukhā putoḷi viya hi cātumahābhūtiko kāyo, tattha missetvā pakkhittanānāvidhadhaññaṃ viya kesādayo dvattiṃsākārā, cakkhumā puriso viya yogāvacaro, tassa taṃ putoḷiṃ muñcitvā paccavekkhato nānāvidhadhaññassa pākaṭakālo viya yogino dvattiṃsākārassa vibhūtakālo veditabbo. <hi rend="bold">Iti ajjhattaṃ vā</hi>ti evaṃ kesādipariggaṇhanena attano vā kāye, parassa vā kāye, kālena vā attano, kālena vā parassa kāye kāyānupassī viharati. Ito paraṃ vuttanayameva. Kevalañhi idha dvattiṃsākārapariggāhikā sati dukkhasaccanti <pb ed="P" n="3.0770"/> evaṃ yojanaṃ katvā niyyānamukhaṃ veditabbaṃ. Sesaṃ purimasadisamevāti.</p> <pb ed="M" n="2.0360"/> <hi rend="bold">Iti ajjhattaṃ vā</hi> <pb ed="P" n="3.0770"/> <p rend="centre">Paṭikūlamanasikārapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.</p> <p rend="subhead">Dhātumanasikārapabbavaṇṇanā</p>
377. Thus, having analyzed body contemplation (kāyānupassanā) by way of the four clear comprehensions (catu-sampajañña), now, in order to analyze it by way of contemplating repulsiveness (*paṭikūla-manasikāra*), he begins with "And further" (*puna capara*).

There, whatever should be stated in the beginning with "Indeed, this body" (*imameva kāya*), all that has been stated in every way, in detail, in the *Visuddhimagga*, in the section on mindfulness of the body (kāyagatāsati-kammaṭṭhāna).

"*Ubhatomukhā* means being joined by two openings, both below and above.

"*Nānāvihitassā*" means of various kinds (*nānāvidhassa*).

Here, moreover, is the application of the simile: Like a bag with two openings (*ubhatomukhā putoḷi*) is the body (kāyo) made of the four great elements (cātumahābhūtiko). There, like various grains (*nānāvidhadhaññaṃ*) thrown in after mixing, are the thirty-two parts (dvattiṃsākārā) beginning with the hair (*kesādayo*). Like a man with sight (*cakkhumā puriso*) is the yogi (yogāvacaro). Like the time when the various grains (*nānāvidhadhaññassa*) become visible to one reflecting after releasing that bag (*putoḷiṃ muñcitvā paccavekkhato*), is the time when the yogi should understand the clear manifestation (*vibhūtakālo veditabbo*) of the thirty-two parts (dvattiṃsākārassa).

"*Iti ajjhattaṃ vā* means thus, by laying hold of things beginning with hair etc. (*kesādi-pariggaṇhanena*), one dwells contemplating the body in the body (kāyānupassī viharati), either in one's own body (*attano vā kāye*), or in another's body (*parassa vā kāye*), or at one time in one's own (*kālena vā attano*), at another time in another's (*kālena vā parassa kāye*).

From here on, the exposition is just according to the method already stated (*vuttanayama eva*).

"The mindfulness (sati) which lays hold of the thirty-two parts (dvattiṃsākāra-pariggāhikā) is the Truth of Suffering (dukkhasaccaṃ)." Thus, having made the connection (evaṃ yojanaṃ katvā), the portal to deliverance (*niyyānamukhaṃ*) should be understood (*veditabbaṃ*).

The rest is just the same as before (sesaṃ purimasadisameva).

*Iti ajjhattaṃ vā*

The section on contemplating repulsiveness (*paṭikūlamanasikārapabbaṃ*) is finished (*niṭṭhitaṃ*).

Explanation of the section on contemplating the elements (*dhātumanasikārapabbavaṇṇanā*).
378
<p n="378" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">378</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu imameva kāyaṃ yathāṭhitaṃ yathāpaṇihitaṃ dhātuso paccavekkhati – ‘atthi imasmiṃ kāye pathavīdhātu āpodhātu tejodhātu vāyodhātū’ti.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Seyyathāpi <pb ed="V" n="2.0218"/>, bhikkhave, dakkho goghātako vā goghātakantevāsī vā gāviṃ vadhitvā catumahāpathe bilaso <pb ed="T" n="2.0330"/> vibhajitvā nisinno assa, evameva kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu imameva kāyaṃ yathāṭhitaṃ yathāpaṇihitaṃ dhātuso paccavekkhati – ‘atthi imasmiṃ kāye pathavīdhātu āpodhātu tejodhātu vāyodhātū’ti.</p> <pb ed="V" n="2.0218"/> <pb ed="T" n="2.0330"/> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Iti ajjhattaṃ vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati…pe… <pb ed="P" n="2.0295"/> evampi kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati.</p> <pb ed="P" n="2.0295"/> <p rend="centre">Dhātumanasikārapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.</p> <p rend="subhead">Kāyānupassanā navasivathikapabbaṃ</p>
Again, monks, a monk reviews this same body, however it is placed, however disposed, as consisting of elements thus: ‘In this body there are the earth element (*pathavīdhātu*), the water element (*āpodhātu*), the fire element (*tejodhātu*), and the air element (*vāyodhātu*).’

Just as a skilled butcher or his apprentice, having killed a cow, would sit at a crossroads cutting it up into pieces, so too, monks, a monk reviews this same body however it is placed, however disposed, as consisting of elements thus: ‘In this body there are the earth element (*pathavīdhātu*), the water element (*āpodhātu*), the fire element (*tejodhātu*), and the air element (*vāyodhātu*).’

Thus, he abides contemplating the body as body internally… And so, monks, a monk abides contemplating body as body.

The section on reflection on the elements is finished.

Section on contemplation of the body: the nine charnel grounds.
<p n="378" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">378</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> Evaṃ <pb ed="V" n="2.0324"/> paṭikūlamanasikāravasena kāyānupassanaṃ vibhajitvā idāni dhātumanasikāravasena vibhajituṃ <hi rend="bold">puna capara</hi>ntiādimāha. Tatthāyaṃ opammasaṃsandanena saddhiṃ atthavaṇṇanā – yathā koci goghātako vā tasseva vā bhattavetanabhato antevāsiko gāviṃ vadhitvā vinivijjhitvā catasso disā gatānaṃ mahāpathānaṃ vemajjhaṭṭhānasaṅkhāte catumahāpathe koṭṭhāsaṃ koṭṭhāsaṃ katvā nisinno assa, evameva bhikkhu catunnaṃ iriyāpathānaṃ yena kenaci ākārena ṭhitattā yathāṭhitaṃ, yathāṭhitattā ca yathāpaṇihitaṃ kāyaṃ ‘‘atthi imasmiṃ kāye pathavīdhātu…pe… vāyodhātū’’ti evaṃ paccavekkhati.</p> <p rend="bodytext">Kiṃ vuttaṃ hoti – yathā goghātakassa gāviṃ posentassāpi āghātanaṃ āharantassāpi āharitvā tattha bandhitvā ṭhapentassapi vadhentassāpi vadhitaṃ mataṃ passantassāpi tāvadeva gāvīti saññā na antaradhāyati, yāva naṃ padāletvā bilaso na vibhajati. Vibhajitvā nisinnassa panassa gāvīti saññā antaradhāyati, maṃsasaññā pavattati. Nāssa evaṃ <pb ed="M" n="2.0361"/> hoti – ‘‘ahaṃ gāviṃ vikkiṇāmi, ime gāviṃ harantī’’ti. Atha khvassa ‘‘ahaṃ maṃsaṃ vikkiṇāmi, ime maṃsaṃ haranti’’ cceva hoti; evameva imassāpi bhikkhuno pubbe bālaputhujjanakāle gihibhūtassāpi pabbajitassāpi tāvadeva sattoti vā puggaloti vā saññā na antaradhāyati, yāva imameva kāyaṃ yathāṭhitaṃ yathāpaṇihitaṃ ghanavinibbhogaṃ katvā dhātuso na paccavekkhati. Dhātuso paccavekkhato panassa sattasaññā antaradhāyati, dhātuvaseneva cittaṃ santiṭṭhati. Tenāha bhagavā – ‘‘‘imameva kāyaṃ yathāṭhitaṃ yathāpaṇihitaṃ dhātuso paccavekkhati ‘atthi imasmiṃ kāye pathavīdhātu āpodhātu tejodhātu vāyodhātū’ti. Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, dakkho goghātako vā…pe… vāyodhātū’’ti. Goghātako viya hi yogī, gāvīti saññā viya sattasaññā, catumahāpatho viya catuiriyāpatho, bilaso vibhajitvā <pb ed="P" n="3.0771"/> nisinnabhāvo viya dhātuso paccavekkhaṇanti ayamettha pāḷivaṇṇanā.
