Because in these enlightenment factors, the meditator effectively gets enlightened from the time he begins strenuous contemplation on insight, it is called *saṃbodhi*, or complete enlightenment. {10} The sevenfold completeness or harmony, beginning with mindfulness, by which he awakes, effectively rises from the sleep of the defilements, or penetrates the Truths, is *saṃbodhi*. The components of that *saṃbodhi* or of the harmony called *saṃbodhi* are the *saṃbojjhaṅga*, the factors of enlightenment. {11} Hence it was said, “The enlightenment factor known as mindfulness.” In the other factors of enlightenment, the word-meaning should be understood in the same way.

*Asanta* means non-existent, not existing through lack of attainment. *Yathā ca anuppannassā* = “How of the non-arisen.” First, is the enlightenment factor of mindfulness. There are things which condition the enlightenment factor of mindfulness, and an abundance of right reflection on them is the reason that is conducive to the arising of the non-arisen enlightenment factor of mindfulness and for the increase, the expansion and completion by culture of the arisen enlightenment factor. Thus it comes into being: just mindfulness comprises the things which condition the enlightenment factor of mindfulness. Right reflection has the same character throughout. When right reflection occurs plentifully there, the enlightenment factor of mindfulness arises.

Besides, four things lead to the arising of the mindfulness enlightenment factor: mindfulness with clear comprehension (smṛti-saṃprajanya), avoiding persons who are confused in mind, cultivation of persons who keep mindfulness ready for application, and inclining towards that. In explanation it is said: Mindfulness arises through mindfulness with clear comprehension in the seven positions beginning with that of “going forwards”;{12} Mindfulness arises through the avoiding of persons who are confused in mind like crows that come cawing to food thrown; through association with persons who keep mindfulness ready for application like the Elder Tissadatta{13} and the Elder Abhaya {14}; and through the state of mind tending for originating mindfulness in all postures, in all. And the bhikkhu knows that the completion by culture of the enlightenment factor of mindfulness brought into being by these four ways takes place by means of the attainment of the path of arahantship.

Regarding the enlightenment factor of investigation of mental objects (dharmavicayasambojjhaṅga), “There are karmically good and karmically bad things… right and wrong counterparts of bright and dark things, and an abundance of right reflection on them is the reason conducive to the arising of the non-arisen enlightenment factor of the investigation of mental objects and for the increase, expansion and the completion of culture of that enlightenment factor when it has arisen. {15}

Besides, seven things lead to the arising of this enlightenment factor: Inquiring about the aggregates and so forth; the purification of the basis ; imparting evenness to the controlling faculties; avoiding the ignorant; associating with the wise; reflecting on the profound difference of the hard-to-perceive processes of the aggregates, modes , sense-bases and so forth; and the inclining towards the development of the enlightenment factor of the investigation of mental objects. There, *paripucchakatā* is frequent questioning closely associated with the meaning of the aggregates (skandha), elements (dhātu), sense-bases (āyatana), faculties (indriya), strengths (bala), enlightenment factors (bojjhaṅga), members of the path (maggāṅga), limbs of jhāna (jhānāṅga), serenity (samatha), and insight (vipaśyanā). *Vatthuvisadakiriyā* is cleansing the internal and the external basis. For when his head hair, nails and body hair are long, or when the body is soaked with sweat, then the internal basis is unclean and unpurified. But when an old dirty smelly robe is worn or when the lodging is dirty, then the external basis is unclean and unpurified. So personal cleanliness should be secured by shaving, hair-cutting, nail-paring, the use of pectoral emetics and of purgatives which make the body light, and by shampooing, bathing and doing other necessary things, at the proper time. In similar way external cleanliness should be brought about by darning, washing and dyeing one’s robes, and by smearing the floor of one’s house with clay and the like to smoothen and clean it, and by doing other necessary things to keep the house clean and tidy. When the internal and external bases are unclean, then the knowledge in the consciousness and consciousness- concomitants that arise is unpurified, like the light of a lamp’s flame that arises with an unpurified lamp-bowl, wick and oil as its support; But when the internal and external bases are clean, then the knowledge in the consciousness and consciousness-concomitants that arise is clean and purified, like the light of a lamp’s flame that arises with a purified lamp bowl, wick and oil as its support. Hence it was said, “Bringing about cleanliness of the basis (vatthuvisadakiriyā) leads to the arising of the enlightenment factor of investigation of mental objects (dhammavicayasambojjhaṅga).”
0339"/>
paripucchakatā vatthuvisadakiriyā
indriyasamattapaṭipādanā nāma saddhādīnaṃ indriyānaṃ samabhāvakaraṇaṃ.
For if his faith faculty (*saddhindriyaṃ*) is strong and the others weak, then the energy faculty (*vīriyindriyaṃ*) cannot perform its function of exerting (*paggahakiccaṃ*), the mindfulness faculty (*satindriyaṃ*) its function of establishing (*upaṭṭhānakiccaṃ*), the concentration faculty (*samādhindriyaṃ*) its function of not distracting (*avikkhepakiccaṃ*), and the understanding faculty (*paññindriyaṃ*) its function of seeing (*dassanakiccaṃ*).
Maintaining balanced faculties is called “the practice of balancing the faculties ( *indriyasamattapaṭipādanā*) of faith (*saddhā*), and the rest.
So in that case the faith faculty should be modified either by reviewing the individual essences of the states concerned, that is, the objects of attention or by not giving them attention in the way in which the faith faculty becomes excessive.
And this is illustrated by the story of the Elder Vakkali.
If however the controlling faculty of energy (*vīriyindriyaṃ*) becomes too powerful then neither will the faculty of faith (*saddhindriyaṃ*) be able to do its work of arousing faith in a settled way in its object nor will the remaining controlling faculties be able to perform their functions.
So in that case the energy faculty should be modified by developing tranquility, and so on.
And this should be illustrated by the story of the Elder Soṇa .
Even thus should the incapacity of the rest of the spiritual faculties to function effectively when one of them has become over-active and powerful, be understood.

visesato panettha saddhāpaññānaṃ samādhivīriyānañca samataṃ pasaṃsanti.
In particular, here, they praise the balance of faith and wisdom, and of concentration and energy.
For one strong in faith and weak in understanding has confidence uncritically and groundlessly.
One strong in understanding and weak in faith errs on the side of cunning and is as hard to cure as one sick of a disease caused by medicine.
Such a person thinks that wholesome karma arises with just the intention to do good. Going along the wrong way, by a species of thought beyond the limits of reason, and doing neither almsgiving nor other similar good deeds, he is born in a state of woe.
With the balancing of the two a man has confidence only when there are grounds for it.
Then idleness overpowers one strong in concentration (*samādhi*) and weak in energy (*vīriya*), since concentration (*samādhi*) favours idleness.
Agitation overpowers one strong in energy and weak in concentration (*samādhi*), since energy (*vīriya*) favours agitation.
and concentration (*samādhi*) coupled with energy (*vīriya*) cannot lapse into agitation. So these two should be balanced;
for absorption comes with the balancing of the two.

apica samādhikammikassa balavatīpi saddhā vaṭṭati.
Again, concentration and faith should be balanced. One working on concentration needs strong faith,
since it is with such faith and confidence that he reaches absorption.
Then there is balancing of concentration and understanding. One working on concentration needs strong unification,
since that is how he reaches absorption.
and one working on insight needs strong understanding,
since that is how he reaches penetration of characteristics;
but with the balancing of the two he reaches absorption as well.
Strong mindfulness, however, is needed in all instances;
Strong mindfulness is met everywhere since it protects the mind from falling into restlessness belonging to faith, energy and wisdom and from falling into indolence belonging to concentration.
So it is as desirable in all instances as a seasoning of salt in all sauces, as a prime minister in all the king’s business.
Therefore it was said by the Blessed One, “But mindfulness is stated by the Blessed One as essential in all instances. What is the reason? Because mindfulness is a refuge for the mind, and mindfulness is presence for protection, and without mindfulness there is no control or restraint of the mind.”
Turning away from persons of poor wisdom is avoiding from afar dull-witted individuals who do not dive into the distinctions of the aggregates etc.
Association with wise folk is fellowship with persons possessed of the knowledge of rise and fall through the laying hold of all the fifty characteristics.
Profound investigative review of knowledge is the review of the distinctions of profound wisdom that occur in the profound aggregates, etc.

Being intent on that is being intent on a mind that inclines, leans, and tends toward establishing the enlightenment factor of investigation of the Dharma in places of sitting, etc.

Thus, one understands that the completion by culture of the arising wisdom is through the path of *arhatship*.

Avoiding persons of poor wisdom and cultivating persons of wisdom.

The energy enlightenment factor: "There are, monks, the element of exertion, the element of striving, the element of effort; there, thorough attention is made abundant; this is food for the arising of the energy enlightenment factor that has not yet arisen, or for the increase, expansion, culture, and completion of the energy enlightenment factor that has arisen," thus there is arising.

Furthermore, eleven things conduce to the arising of the energy enlightenment factor: reviewing the fear of the lower realms, showing the benefit, reviewing the path to be trodden, making offerings of alms-food, reviewing the greatness of inheritance, reviewing the greatness of the Teacher, reviewing the greatness of birth, reviewing the greatness of those living the holy life, avoiding lazy persons, cultivating energetic persons, and being intent on that.

There, in the hells, even at the time of experiencing great suffering beginning from the cause of the five kinds of bondage, in the animal realm, even when seized by net-casters, worms, etc., even at the time of being pierced by the goad, thorns, etc., and being burdened by cart-drawing, etc., in the realm of the departed, even for many thousands of years, even for one Buddha-interval, even when tormented by hunger and thirst, in the Kālakañcika asuras, even at the time of experiencing suffering from wind, heat, etc., with a self consisting of merely bone and skin to the extent of sixty or eighty cubits, it is not possible to generate the energy enlightenment factor; "This is the time for you, monk, to exert energy," thus, reviewing the fear of the lower realms, the energy enlightenment factor also arises for one who reviews.

It is not possible for the lazy to obtain the nine supramundane *dharmas*; it is only possible for the energetic; this is the benefit of energy. Thus, the energy enlightenment factor also arises for one who shows the benefit.

"The path trodden by all the Buddhas, Private Buddhas, and great disciples is to be trodden by you, and that is not possible for the lazy to tread," thus, the energy enlightenment factor also arises for one who reviews the path to be trodden.

Those who support him with alms-food, etc., these people are neither relatives, nor servants, nor do they give these excellent robes, etc., thinking, "We will live depending on him."

But, expecting great fruit from their actions, they give.

Even the Teacher did not allow these requisites to you, thinking, "Having made use of these requisites, he will live comfortably, with a strong and firm body."

But these requisites were allowed to you thinking, "Having made the ascetic practice, he will be released from the suffering of the round," therefore, now, you, living lazily, will not increase that alms-food.

For, indeed, increase of alms-food is only for the energetic. Thus, the energy enlightenment factor also arises for one who reviews the making of offerings of alms-food, like the venerable Ayyamitta.

The *thera* lived in a cave named Kassaka.

And in the village to which he went for alms, there was a certain great laywoman who, having made the *thera* her son, looked after him.

One day, going to the forest, she said to her daughter: "Dear, in such and such a place there is old rice, in such and such a place there is ghee, in such and such a place there is milk, in such and such a place there is molasses; when your brother Ayyamitta comes, cook food and give it to him together with milk, ghee, and molasses, and you too should eat.

But I ate the cooked stale leftover food with gruel yesterday."

"What will you eat in the day, mother?" "Having put in greens, and having cooked sour gruel with broken rice, keep it, mother."

The *thera*, having robed himself and just as he was taking out his bowl, heard that sound and admonished himself: "The great laywoman ate stale leftover food with gruel, and during the day she will eat gruel made with grain and leaves; but for you, she indicates the old rice, etc.; depending on that, this one expects neither field, nor property, nor food, nor clothing, but she gives, desiring three attainments; will you be able to give those attainments to her, or will you not be able to do so? This alms-food, however, should not be taken by you with passion, with aversion, and with delusion." Having put the bowl in his bag, and having untied the knot, turning back, going to that very Kassaka cave, having placed the bowl on the lower seat and the robe on the robe-rack, resolving on energy, "I will not go out without attaining *arhatship*," he sat down.

Having been earnest for a long time, the recluse, having developed insight, having attained *arhatship* even before mealtime, and the great destroyer of the defilements, smiling like an opening lotus, sat down.

A deity dwelling in a tree at the cave entrance:

"Homage to you, thoroughbred man, homage to you, supreme man;

whose defilements are destroyed, you are worthy of offerings, sir."

Having uttered an exclamation of joy, she said: "Venerable sir, having given alms to the *arhats* like you who have entered for alms, the elderly women will be released from suffering."

The *thera*, having gotten up, having opened the door, observing the time, knowing that it was still early dawn, taking his bowl and robe, entered the village.

That girl too, having prepared the food, thinking, "Now my brother will come, now he will come," sat watching the door.

She, when the *thera* arrived at the house door, taking the bowl, filling it with milk-rice alms mixed with ghee and molasses, placed it in his hands.

The *thera*, having given thanks, saying, "May there be happiness," departed.

That girl too, stood looking at him as he departed.
At that time, the Elder's complexion was exceedingly clear, his senses especially pure, and his face shone brilliantly like a ripe palm fruit freed from its stalk.

The great lay woman came from the forest and asked, "Dear mother, has my brother come?"
She told her everything that had happened.
The lay woman, knowing that her son’s work of renunciation had reached its peak that day, said, “Dear mother, your brother delights in the Buddha’s Dispensation; he is not dissatisfied.”

It is a great inheritance from the Teacher, namely, the seven noble treasures, and it cannot be obtained by the lazy.
Just as parents disown a wayward son, saying, "This is not our son," and he does not receive the inheritance when they die, so too, the lazy do not receive this noble inheritance; only the energetic receive it. Thus, reflection on the greatness of inheritance arises.

Moreover, your Teacher is great. When the Teacher was conceived in his mother's womb, at his renunciation, at his enlightenment, at the turning of the wheel of the Dharma, at the miracle of the twin phenomena, at his descent from the heavens, at the relinquishing of his life force, and at the time of his *parinirvana*, the ten-thousand world system trembled. Is it fitting for you to be lazy after having gone forth into the Dispensation of such a Teacher? Thus, reflection on the greatness of the Teacher arises.

Moreover, by birth, you are not of low birth now. You were born into the lineage of the *Śākya* kings, which has come down in an unbroken line from the Great Elect (*Mahāsammata*). You are the grandson of King Śuddhodana and Queen Mahāmāyā, and the younger brother of Rāhula. Is it fitting for you to be lazy after having become such a son of the Conqueror (*Jina*)? Thus, reflection on the greatness of birth arises.

Moreover, Sāriputta and Mahāmoggallāna and the eighty great disciples realized the supramundane Dharma through energy alone. You are sharing the holy life with them. Are you not undertaking the path? Thus, reflection on the greatness of those who share the holy life arises.

Even for one who avoids lazy individuals who are like a python that has filled its belly and lies still, and who cultivates energetic individuals who are intent, for one whose mind is inclined and disposed to arouse energy in places for sitting and lying down, diligence arises. The yogi understands that the culture of this enlightenment factor arisen thus comes to completion through the path of arahantship.

Concerning the *pīti-saṃbojjhaṅga*: “There are, monks, things that are conducive to the enlightenment factor of joy. Abundant, systematic attention to them is the food conducive to the arising of the unarisen enlightenment factor of joy and for the increase, expansion, and completion by culture of the enlightenment factor of joy when it has arisen." Thus, its arising occurs.
There, joy itself is called things that are conducive to the enlightenment factor of joy.
Systematic attention, causing its arising, is called systematic attention.

Moreover, eleven things lead to the arising of the joy enlightenment factor: mindfulness of the Buddha, the Dharma, the *Saṃgha*, virtue, generosity, mindfulness of deities, mindfulness of peace, avoidance of rough persons, cultivation of refined persons, reviewing inspiring discourses, and being resolved upon that.

Even for one who recollects the virtues of the Buddha, the enlightenment factor of joy arises, pervading the entire body up to the [level of] access [concentration]. Also, for one who recollects the virtues of the Dharma and the *Saṃgha*, for one who reflects on the precepts of fourfold purity, kept unbroken for a long time, for a householder who reflects on the ten precepts and the five precepts, for one who, in times of famine or fear, gives excellent food to those sharing the holy life and reflects on their generosity, thinking, "Thus, indeed, we have given," for a householder who, at such a time, reflects on the alms given to the virtuous, for one who reflects on possessing the qualities with which the deities, endowed, have attained to divinity, for one who reflects, "The defilements suppressed by attainment do not arise for sixty or seventy years," for one who avoids rough persons who, in seeing *cetiya*s, enlightenment trees, and elders, have a rough manner revealed by acting without respect, and who lack confidence and affection for the Buddha and the like, who are like dirt on a donkey's back, for one who cultivates refined persons who have abundant confidence in the Buddha and the like, and who are mild in mind, for one who reflects on the inspiring *sūtra*s that illuminate the virtues of the Triple Gem, for one whose mind is inclined and disposed to arouse joy in places for sitting and lying down, joy arises.
The yogi understands that the culture of this enlightenment factor arisen thus comes to completion through the path of arahantship.

Concerning the *passaddhi-saṃbojjhaṅga*: "There are, monks, pliancy of body and pliancy of mind. Abundant, systematic attention to them is the food conducive to the arising of the unarisen enlightenment factor of pliancy, and for the increase, expansion, and completion by culture of the enlightenment factor of pliancy when it has arisen." Thus, its arising occurs.

Moreover, seven things lead to the arising of the enlightenment factor of pliancy: cultivating fine food, cultivating comfortable weather, cultivating comfortable postures, judgment according to the middle way, avoiding individuals with tense bodies, cultivating individuals with pliable bodies, and resolution upon that.

Indeed, even for one who eats fine, soothing, and agreeable food, pliancy arises from the hot and cold seasons, from cultivating agreeable seasons and postures in places for sitting and the like.
But this is not said with reference to one who, being of great character, is tolerant of all weather conditions and postures.
Pliancy arises for one who has association and dissociation, by avoiding unsuitable weather and postures and by cultivating suitable ones.
Judgment according to the middle way is reflection on one’s own deed as one’s own property and another’s deed as that of others’ property.
By this judgment according to the middle way, it arises.
Pliancy also arises for one who avoids tense-bodied individuals who wander about harming others with sticks and clubs, and for one who cultivates pliant-bodied individuals with restrained feet and hands, and for one whose mind is inclined and disposed to produce pliancy in places for sitting and lying down.
Thus, he understands that the culture of this enlightenment factor comes to completion through the path of arahantship.

The enlightenment factor of concentration: "There are, monks, things that are the sign of quietude and the sign of non-confusion. Therein, frequent right reflection is the nourishment for the arising of the unarisen enlightenment factor of concentration, and for the increase, expansion, and completion by culture of the arisen enlightenment factor of concentration." Thus, its arising occurs.

There the sign of quietude is just the quietude and in the sense of non-distraction is the sign of non-confusion to be taken.

The enlightenment factor of concentration: Moreover, eleven things lead to the arising of the enlightenment factor of concentration: clarity of the object, proper management of the faculties, skill in the sign, at the right time elevating the mind, at the right time restraining the mind, at the right time gladdening the mind, at the right time practicing equanimity, avoiding persons who are not collected in mind, associating with persons who are collected in mind, reviewing the meditative absorption and liberation, and inclination for that.

Therein, clarity of the object and proper management of the faculties should be understood in the manner already stated.

Clarity of the object, proper management of the faculties: Skill in the sign means skill in grasping the kasiṇa sign.

Elevating the mind at the right time: When the mind is sluggish due to excessive sluggishness of effort, etc., at that time elevating it by bringing into being the enlightenment factors of investigation of the Dhamma, effort, and joy.

Elevating the mind at the right time: When the mind is agitated due to excessive arousal of effort, etc., at that time restraining it by bringing into being the enlightenment factors of tranquility, concentration, and equanimity.

Gladdening the mind at the right time: When the mind is listless owing to sluggishness in the exercise of understanding or to failure to attain the bliss of peace, then at that time he should stimulate it by reviewing the eight grounds for a sense of urgency.

The eight grounds for a sense of urgency are birth, aging, sickness, and death, the four woeful states, and the five kinds of suffering: suffering rooted in past, suffering rooted in future, suffering rooted in present.

And he generates confidence by reflecting on the qualities of the Triple Gem. This is called "gladdening the mind at the right time".

Skill in the sign, elevating the mind at the right time, elevating the mind at the right time

The enlightenment factor of equanimity: "There are, monks, things that are the basis of the enlightenment factor of equanimity. Therein, frequent right reflection is the nourishment for the arising of the unarisen enlightenment factor of equanimity, and for the increase, expansion, and completion by culture of the arisen enlightenment factor of equanimity." Thus, its arising occurs.

There equanimity is just the things that are the basis of the enlightenment factor of equanimity.

The enlightenment factor of equanimity: Moreover, five things lead to the arising of the enlightenment factor of equanimity: maintaining neutrality towards beings, maintaining neutrality towards formations, avoiding persons who are attached to beings and formations, cultivating persons who maintain neutrality towards beings and formations, and inclination for that.

There, in two ways he brings about maintaining neutrality towards beings: "You have come by your own karma and will depart by your own karma; even he has come by his own karma and will depart by his own karma; who then are you attached to?" Thus, by reflection on beings as possessors of their own deeds, and "In the highest sense there is really no living being, so who then are you attached to?" Thus, by reflection on beings as without essence.

In two ways he brings about maintaining neutrality towards formations: "This robe, gradually undergoing change of color and aging, will become a foot-wiper to be discarded at the end of a stick. But if it should belong to a master, he would not allow it to be destroyed in that way." Thus, by reflection on its lack of ownership, and "This is unstable and temporary." Thus, by reflection on its temporary nature.

Just as with the robe, so too the application should be made with the bowl and other things.

Maintaining neutrality towards beings, maintaining neutrality towards formations

Avoiding persons who are attached to beings and formations: Here, the person, whether a householder or a monk, is attached to his sons, daughters, disciples, pupils, or preceptors. He himself performs such tasks as cutting their hair, needlework, washing robes, dyeing, receiving alms, and cooking. Not seeing them even for a moment, he looks here and there like a frightened deer, saying "Where is so-and-so novice? Where is so-and-so youth?". Even when he is asked by another to send someone for a moment for the purpose of cutting hair, etc., he does not give [permission], saying "We do not have them do even our own work, you would take them and exhaust them." This is called one who is attached to beings.

Avoiding persons who are attached to beings and formations: But he who is attached to his robe, bowl, plate, knife, staff, etc., does not allow another to even touch them. When asked for something temporarily, he says "We do not even enjoy these things that we are attached to, how could we give them to you?". This is called one who is attached to formations.
But whoever among these two things is neutral and indifferent, this is called *sattasaṅkhāramajjhatto*.

Thus, this enlightenment factor of equanimity arises even for one who carefully avoids a person delighting in beings, and for one who cultivates a person who is neutral towards beings, and also for one whose mind is inclined, prone, and directed towards producing that in sitting and lying down, etc.

The yogi understands that the culture of this enlightenment factor arisen thus comes to completion through the path of arahantship.

*Saṅkhārakelāyano, sattasaṅkhāramajjhatto.*

*Iti ajjhattaṃ vā:* Thus, either by taking up the seven enlightenment factors in oneself, or in another, or at one time in oneself, or at one time in another, he dwells contemplating phenomena in phenomena.

Here, origination and dissolution should be known by way of the origination and dissolution of the enlightenment factors.

From here on the exposition is just according to the manner already stated. {36}

Indeed, the mindfulness that lays hold of the enlightenment factors is the Truth of Suffering. Thus the portal of deliverance for the bhikkhu who lays hold of the enlightenment factors should be understood.

The rest is as before.

*Iti ajjhattaṃ vā*

The Enlightenment Factors Section is finished.

Explanation of the Four Noble Truths Section
386
<p n="386" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">386</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati catūsu ariyasaccesu. Kathañca pana, bhikkhave, bhikkhu dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati catūsu ariyasaccesu? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu ‘idaṃ dukkha’nti yathābhūtaṃ pajānāti, ‘ayaṃ dukkhasamudayo’ti yathābhūtaṃ pajānāti, ‘ayaṃ dukkhanirodho’ti yathābhūtaṃ pajānāti, ‘ayaṃ dukkhanirodhagāminī paṭipadā’ti yathābhūtaṃ pajānāti.</p> <p rend="centre">Paṭhamabhāṇavāro niṭṭhito.</p> <p rend="subhead">Dukkhasaccaniddeso</p>
“Again, monks, a monk abides contemplating mind-objects as mind-objects in respect of the Four Noble Truths ( *catūsu ariyasaccesu* ).

And how, monks, does a monk abide contemplating mind-objects as mind-objects in respect of the Four Noble Truths? Here, monks, a monk understands as it actually is: ‘This is suffering ( *idaṃ dukkhaṃ* )’; ‘This is the origin of suffering ( *ayaṃ dukkhasamudayo* )’; ‘This is the cessation of suffering ( *ayaṃ dukkhanirodho* )’; ‘This is the path leading to the cessation of suffering ( *ayaṃ dukkhanirodhagāminī paṭipadā* )’.

The first section for recitation is finished.

Explanation of the Truth of Suffering ( *dukkhasaccaniddeso* ).
<p n="386" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">386</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> Evaṃ <pb ed="M" n="2.0386"/> sattabojjhaṅgavasena dhammānupassanaṃ vibhajitvā idāni catusaccavasena vibhajituṃ <hi rend="bold">puna capara</hi>ntiādimāha. Tattha <hi rend="bold">idaṃ dukkhanti yathābhūtaṃ pajānātī</hi>ti ṭhapetvā taṇhaṃ tebhūmakadhamme ‘‘idaṃ dukkha’’nti yathāsabhāvato pajānāti, tasseva kho pana dukkhassa janikaṃ samuṭṭhāpikaṃ purimataṇhaṃ ‘‘ayaṃ dukkhasamudayo’’ti, ubhinnaṃ appavattinibbānaṃ ‘‘ayaṃ dukkhanirodho’’ti, dukkhaparijānanaṃ samudayapajahanaṃ nirodhasacchikaraṇaṃ ariyamaggaṃ ‘‘ayaṃ dukkhanirodhagāminipaṭipadā’’ti yathāsabhāvato pajānātīti attho. Avasesā ariyasaccakathā ṭhapetvā jātiādīnaṃ padabhājanakathaṃ visuddhimagge vitthāritāyeva.</p> <p rend="subhead">Dukkhasaccaniddesavaṇṇanā</p>
Thus, having distinguished the contemplation of the Dhamma in terms of the seven factors of enlightenment, he now says "<hi rend="bold">Puna capara</hi>" and so on, in order to distinguish it in terms of the Four Noble Truths.

There, having established "<hi rend="bold">This is suffering, he understands it as it really is</hi>," he understands craving for the three realms as "This is suffering" according to its own nature; he understands the former craving, which is the generator and originator of that very suffering, as "This is the origin of suffering"; he understands the cessation of both, which is non-arising, as "This is the cessation of suffering"; he understands the noble path, which is the full knowledge of suffering, the abandoning of its origin, and the realization of its cessation, as "This is the path leading to the cessation of suffering," according to its own nature.

The remaining discussion of the Noble Truths, except for the word division concerning birth and so on, has already been extensively explained in the *Visuddhimagga*.

Explanation of the Discourse on the Truth of Suffering
387
<p n="387" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">387</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Katamañca <pb ed="T" n="2.0341"/><pb ed="V" n="2.0228"/><pb ed="P" n="2.0305"/>, bhikkhave, dukkhaṃ ariyasaccaṃ? Jātipi dukkhā, jarāpi dukkhā, maraṇampi dukkhaṃ, sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsāpi dukkhā, appiyehi sampayogopi dukkho, piyehi vippayogopi dukkho <note>appiyehi…pe… vippayogo dukkhotipāṭho ceva taṃniddeso ca katthaci na dissati, aṭṭhakathāyaṃpi taṃsaṃvaṇṇanā natthi</note>, yampicchaṃ na labhati tampi dukkhaṃ, <hi rend="bold">saṅkhittena pañcupādānakkhandhā</hi><note>pañcupādānakkhandhāpi (ka.)</note><hi rend="bold">dukkhā.</hi></p>
387. "And what, monks, is the noble truth of suffering (duḥkhaṃ āryasatyaṃ)? Birth (jāti) is suffering (duḥkhā), aging (jarā) is suffering (duḥkhā), death (maraṇaṃ) is suffering (duḥkhaṃ), sorrow (śoka), lamentation (parideva), pain (duḥkha), grief (daurmanasya), and despair (upāyāsā) are suffering (duḥkhā), association with the unpleasant (appiyehi sampayogo) is suffering (duḥkho), separation from the pleasant (piyehi vippayogo) is suffering (duḥkho), and not getting what one wants (yam picchaṃ na labhati) is suffering (tampi dukkhaṃ). In brief, the five aggregates of clinging (saṅkhittena pañcupādānakkhandhā) are suffering (dukkhā)."
388
<p n="388" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">388</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Katamā ca, bhikkhave, jāti? Yā tesaṃ tesaṃ sattānaṃ tamhi tamhi sattanikāye jāti sañjāti okkanti abhinibbatti khandhānaṃ pātubhāvo āyatanānaṃ paṭilābho, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, jāti.</p>
388. “And what, monks, is birth (jāti)? Whatever birth, taking birth (sañjāti), descent (okkanti), coming-to-be (abhinibbatti), appearance of aggregates (khandhānaṃ pātubhāvo), & acquisition of sense spheres (āyatanānaṃ paṭilābho) of the various beings in this or that group of beings, that is called birth (jāti).
<p n="388" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">388</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> Padabhājane <pb ed="V" n="2.0348"/> pana <hi rend="bold">katamā ca, bhikkhave, jātī</hi>ti bhikkhave, yā jātipi dukkhāti evaṃ vuttā jāti, sā katamāti evaṃ sabbapucchāsu attho veditabbo. <hi rend="bold">Yā tesaṃ tesaṃ sattāna</hi>nti idaṃ ‘‘imesaṃ nāmā’’ti niyamābhāvato sabbasattānaṃ pariyādānavacanaṃ. <hi rend="bold">Tamhi tamhi sattanikāye</hi>ti idampi sabbasattanikāyapariyādānavacanaṃ jananaṃ <hi rend="bold">jāti</hi> savikārānaṃ paṭhamābhinibbattakkhandhānametaṃ adhivacanaṃ. <hi rend="bold">Sañjātī</hi>ti idaṃ tassā eva upasaggamaṇḍitavevacanaṃ. Sā eva anupaviṭṭhākārena okkamanaṭṭhena <hi rend="bold">okkanti.</hi> Nibbattisaṅkhātena abhinibbattanaṭṭhena <hi rend="bold">abhinibbatti.</hi> Iti ayaṃ catubbidhāpi sammutikathā nāma <pb ed="P" n="3.0798"/>. <hi rend="bold">Khandhānaṃ pātubhāvo</hi>ti ayaṃ pana paramatthakathā. Ekavokārabhavādīsu ekacatupañcabhedānaṃ khandhānaṃyeva pātubhāvo, na puggalassa, tasmiṃ pana sati puggalo pātubhūtoti vohāramattaṃ hoti. <hi rend="bold">Āyatanānaṃ paṭilābho</hi>ti āyatanāni pātubhavantāneva paṭiladdhāni nāma honti, so tesaṃ pātubhāvasaṅkhāto paṭilābhoti attho.</p>
388.
In the *padabhājane*. Moreover, monks, what is *jāti*? Monks, that *jāti* which is said to be suffering, what is that? Thus, in all the questions, the meaning should be understood.

"*Yā tesaṃ tesaṃ sattāna*": This is a term for including all beings because there is no restriction such as "these are their names."

"*Tamhi tamhi sattanikāye*": This too is a term for including all collections of beings. *Jāti* is a term for the first aggregates that have arisen along with their transformations.

"*Sañjātī*": This is a decorated term for that.

That itself, by way of not sitting down, by way of the meaning of entering, is *okkanti*.

Designated as *nibbatti*, by way of the meaning of fully arising, is *abhinibbatti*.

Thus, this fourfold conventional speech is called.

"*Khandhānaṃ pātubhāvo*": This, moreover, is the ultimate discourse.

Among the *ekavokārabhavādīsu*, the arising of the aggregates is only the arising of the aggregates of the one, four, or five kinds, not of the person (*pudgala*). But when that is present, the person has arisen; this is merely a convention.

"*Āyatanānaṃ paṭilābho*": The *āyatana*s, as they arise, are said to have been obtained. That, designated as the arising of those things, is the meaning of obtaining.
389
<p n="389" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">389</hi>. ‘‘Katamā <pb ed="M" n="2.0244"/> ca, bhikkhave, jarā? Yā tesaṃ tesaṃ sattānaṃ tamhi tamhi sattanikāye jarā jīraṇatā khaṇḍiccaṃ pāliccaṃ valittacatā āyuno saṃhāni indriyānaṃ paripāko, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, jarā.</p>
389.

And what, monks, is aging (jarā)? Whatever aging, decrepitude (jīraṇatā), brokenness (khaṇḍiccaṃ), graying (pāliccaṃ), wrinkling (valittacatā), decline of life-force (āyuno saṃhāni), weakening of the faculties (indriyānaṃ paripāko) of the various beings in this or that group of beings (sattānam tamhi tamhi sattanikāye), that is called aging (jarā).
<p n="389" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">389</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi><hi rend="bold">Jarā</hi>ti sabhāvaniddeso. <hi rend="bold">Jīraṇatā</hi>ti ākārabhāvaniddeso. <hi rend="bold">Khaṇḍicca</hi>ntiādi vikāraniddeso. Daharakālasmiñhi dantā samasetā honti. Teyeva paripaccante anukkamena vaṇṇavikāraṃ āpajjitvā tattha tattha pattanti. Atha patitañca ṭhitañca upādāya khaṇḍitadantā khaṇḍitā <pb ed="M" n="2.0387"/> nāma. Khaṇḍitānaṃ bhāvo <hi rend="bold">khaṇḍicca</hi>nti vuccati. Anukkamena paṇḍarabhūtāni kesalomāni palitāni nāma. Palitāni sañjātāni assāti palito, palitassa bhāvo <hi rend="bold">pāliccaṃ.</hi> Jarāvātappahārena sositamaṃsalohitatāya valiyo tacasmiṃ assāti valittaco, tassa bhāvo <hi rend="bold">valittacatā.</hi> Ettāvatā dantakesalomatacesu vikāradassanavasena pākaṭībhūtā pākaṭajarā dassitā.</p> <p rend="bodytext">Yatheva hi udakassa vā vātassa vā aggino vā tiṇarukkhādīnaṃ saṃbhaggapalibhaggatāya vā jhāmatāya vā gatamaggo pākaṭo hoti, na ca so gatamaggo tāneva udakādīni, evameva jarāya dantādīnaṃ khaṇḍiccādivasena gatamaggo pākaṭo, cakkhuṃ ummiletvāpi gayhati, na ca khaṇḍiccādīneva jarā. Na hi jarā cakkhuviññeyyā hoti. Yasmā pana jaraṃ pattassa āyu hāyati, tasmā jarā ‘‘āyuno saṃhānī’’ti phalūpacārena vuttā. Yasmā daharakāle suppasannāni sukhumampi attano visayaṃ sukheneva ca gaṇhanasamatthāni cakkhādīni indriyāni jaraṃ pattassa paripakkāni ālulitāni avisadāni oḷārikampi attano visayaṃ gahetuṃ asamatthāni honti, tasmā ‘‘indriyānaṃ paripāko’’tipi phalūpacāreneva vuttā.</p>
389. *Jarā* means a designation of nature.

*Jīraṇatā* means a designation of the state of being.

*Khaṇḍicca* etc. means a designation of change.

In early youth, the teeth are uniform.

These very [teeth], when they mature, gradually undergo changes in color and reach various states.

Then, depending on what has fallen out and what remains, they are called broken teeth (*khaṇḍita-danta*).

The state of having broken teeth is called *khaṇḍicca*.

Gradually, the hairs of the head and body become white; these are called gray hairs (*palita*).

Because they have become gray, they are called *palito*, and the state of *palita* is *pāliccaṃ*.

Because the skin becomes wrinkled (*valī*) due to the striking force of the wind of old age (*jarāvāta-ppahārena*) which has dried up the flesh and blood, it is called wrinkled skin (*valittaco*), and the state of *valittaco* is *valittacatā*.

Thus, apparent aging (*pākaṭajarā*) is shown through the changes seen in the teeth, hair of the head and body, and skin.

Just as the course taken by water, wind, or fire is evident from the damaged and broken state, or the burnt state, of grass, trees, etc., and yet the course that has been taken is not the water, etc., itself, so too the course taken by aging is evident through brokenness of teeth, etc., and it is apprehended by opening the eyes, but the brokenness, etc., themselves are not aging.

Indeed, aging is not cognizable by the eye (*cakkhuviññeyyā*).

Since the life-span (*āyus*) of one who has reached aging dwindles, aging is called “decline of life (*āyuno saṃhānī*)” as a metaphor of its effect.

Since the eye faculty (*cakkhādīni indriyāni*), etc. — which in early youth are quite clear, very easily grasp even subtle objects of their respective domains, and are efficient — become deficient, confused (*ālulitāni*), unclear (*avisadāni*), and unable to grasp even gross (*oḷārikaṃ*) objects when one has reached old age, therefore it is called “weakness of faculties (*indriyānaṃ paripāko*)” also as a metaphor of its effect.
390
<p n="390" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">390</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Katamañca, bhikkhave, maraṇaṃ? Yaṃ <note>aṭṭhakathā oloketabbā</note> tesaṃ tesaṃ sattānaṃ tamhā tamhā sattanikāyā cuti cavanatā bhedo antaradhānaṃ maccu maraṇaṃ kālakiriyā khandhānaṃ bhedo kaḷevarassa nikkhepo jīvitindriyassupacchedo, idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, maraṇaṃ.</p>
390. "And what, monks, is death (maraṇa)? That which, in whatever beings, of whatever group of beings, is the falling away (cyuti), deceasing (cavanatā), breaking up (bheda), disappearance (antaradhānaṃ), dying (maccu), death (maraṇaṃ), completion of time (kālakiriyā), break up of the aggregates (khandhānaṃ bheda), casting off of the body (kaḷevarassa nikkhepo), interruption in the life faculty (jīvitindriyassupacchedo), this, monks, is called death (maraṇa)."
<p n="390" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">390</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> Maraṇaniddese <pb ed="V" n="2.0349"/><hi rend="bold">ya</hi>nti maraṇaṃ sandhāya napuṃsakaniddeso, yaṃ maraṇaṃ cutīti vuccati, cavanatāti vuccatīti ayamettha yojanā. Tattha <hi rend="bold">cutī</hi>ti sabhāvaniddeso. <hi rend="bold">Cavanatā</hi>ti ākārabhāvaniddeso. Maraṇaṃ pattassa khandhā bhijjanti ceva antaradhāyanti ca adassanaṃ gacchanti, tasmā taṃ <hi rend="bold">bhedo antaradhāna</hi>nti vuccati. <hi rend="bold">Maccumaraṇa</hi>nti maccumaraṇaṃ, na khaṇikamaraṇaṃ. <hi rend="bold">Kālakiriyā</hi>ti maraṇakālakiriyā. Ayaṃ sabbāpi sammutikathāva. <hi rend="bold">Khandhānaṃ bhedo</hi>ti ayaṃ pana paramatthakathā. Ekavokārabhavādīsu ekacatupañcabhedānaṃ khandhānaṃyeva bhedo, na <pb ed="P" n="3.0799"/> puggalassa, tasmiṃ pana sati puggalo matoti vohāramattaṃ hoti.</p> <p rend="bodytext"><hi rend="bold">Kaḷevarassa nikkhepo</hi>ti attabhāvassa nikkhepo. Maraṇaṃ pattassa hi niratthaṃva kaliṅgaraṃ attabhāvo patati, tasmā taṃ kaḷevarassa nikkhepoti vuttaṃ. Jīvitindriyassa upacchedo pana sabbākārato paramatthato maraṇaṃ. Etadeva <pb ed="M" n="2.0388"/> sammutimaraṇanti pi vuccati. Jīvitindriyupacchedameva hi gahetvā lokiyā ‘‘tisso mato, phusso mato’’ti vadanti.</p> <hi rend="bold">Kaḷevarassa nikkhepo</hi> <pb ed="M" n="2.0388"/>
390.
In the explanation of death (*maraṇa-niddeśe*): *yaṃti maraṇaṃ sandhāya napuṃsakaniddeso*, *yaṃ maraṇaṃ cutīti vuccati, cavanatāti vuccatīti ayamettha yojanā* – "regarding death, [there is] a neuter designation; that which is death is called *cuti*; *cavanatā* is said – this is the connection here."
There, *cutī* means designation by nature (*sabhāvaniddeso*).
*Cavanatā* means designation by characteristic nature (*ākārabhāvaniddeso*).
To one who has attained death (*maraṇaṃ pattassa*), the aggregates (*khandhā*) are destroyed (*bhijjanti*), disappear (*antaradhāyanti*), and go to invisibility (*adassanaṃ gacchanti*); therefore, that is called "destruction and disappearance" (*bhedo antaradhānaṃti vuccati*).
*Maccumaraṇaṃti* means continuous death (*maccumaraṇaṃ*), not momentary death (*khaṇikamaraṇaṃ*).
*Kālakiriyāti* means the action of the time of death (*maraṇakālakiriyā*).
This is all conventional language (*sammutikathāva*).
*Khandhānaṃ bhedoti* – this is the ultimate meaning (*paramatthakathā*).
Among those with characteristics such as singularity, etc. (*ekavokārabhavādīsu*), the destruction of the aggregates (*khandhānaṃ*) is itself destruction, not of the person (*puggalassa*); when that exists (*tasmiṃ pana sati*), it is merely a conventional expression (*vohāramattaṃ hoti*) to say "the person is dead" (*puggalo matoti*).

*Kaḷevarassa nikkhepoti* means the abandonment of selfhood (*attabhāvassa nikkhepo*).
For one who has attained death (*maraṇaṃ pattassa*), selfhood (*attabhāvo*) falls away (*patati*) like useless rubbish (*niratthaṃva kaliṅgaraṃ*); therefore, that is called "the rejection of the body" (*kaḷevarassa nikkhepoti vuttaṃ*).
The cutting off (*upacchedo*) of the life faculty (*jīvitindriyassa*) is, in every way (*sabbākārato*), in the ultimate sense (*paramatthato*), death (*maraṇaṃ*).
This very thing is also called conventional death (*sammutimaraṇaṃti pi vuccati*).
Having taken hold of just the cutting off of the life faculty (*jīvitindriyupacchedameva hi gahetvā*), worldly people (*lokiyā*) say, "Tissa is dead, Phussa is dead" (*tisso mato, phusso mato*tti vadanti).

*Kaḷevarassa nikkhepo*
391
<p n="391" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">391</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, soko? Yo kho, bhikkhave, aññataraññatarena byasanena samannāgatassa aññataraññatarena <pb ed="P" n="2.0306"/> dukkhadhammena phuṭṭhassa soko socanā socitattaṃ antosoko antoparisoko, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, soko.</p>
Monks, what is sorrow? Monks, whatever sorrow, sorrowing, sadness, inward sorrow, inward sadness of anyone suffering from misfortune, touched by a painful thing, that is called sorrow.
<p n="391" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">391</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi><hi rend="bold">Byasanenā</hi>ti ñātibyasanādīsu yena kenaci byasanena. <hi rend="bold">Dukkhadhammenā</hi>ti vadhabandhādinā dukkhakāraṇena. <hi rend="bold">Phuṭṭhassā</hi>ti ajjhotthaṭassa abhibhūtassa. <hi rend="bold">Soko</hi>ti yo ñātibyasanādīsu vā vadhabandhanādīsu vā aññatarasmiṃ sati tena abhibhūtassa uppajjati socanalakkhaṇo soko. <hi rend="bold">Socitatta</hi>nti socitabhāvo. Yasmā panesa abbhantare sosento parisosento uppajjati, tasmā <hi rend="bold">antosoko antoparisoko</hi>ti vuccati.</p>
"By *byasanenā*," it means by any *byasana* among loss of relatives (*ñātibyasanādi*), and so forth.

"By *dukkadhammenā*," it means by a cause of suffering (*dukkha-kāraṇena*) such as execution (*vadha*), imprisonment (*bandha*), and so forth.

"By *phuṭṭhassā*," it means overwhelmed (*ajjhotthaṭassa*) and overcome (*abhibhūtassa*).

"Sorrow (*śoko*)," it means the sorrow (*śoko*) which arises, having the characteristic of lamentation (*socanalakkhaṇo*), in one who is overcome (*abhibhūtassa*) by either loss of relatives (*ñātibyasanādi*) or execution and imprisonment (*vadhabandhanādi*), and so forth, when that exists.

"By *socitatta*," it means the state of being afflicted (*socitabhāvo*).

Because this dries up (*sosento*) and completely dries up (*parisosento*) internally (*abbhantare*), therefore it is called (*vuccati*) "inner sorrow" (*antośoko*) and "inner complete sorrow" (*antopariśoko*).
392
<p n="392" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">392</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, paridevo? Yo kho, bhikkhave, aññataraññatarena byasanena samannāgatassa aññataraññatarena dukkhadhammena phuṭṭhassa ādevo paridevo ādevanā paridevanā ādevitattaṃ paridevitattaṃ, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave paridevo.</p>
392. Monks, what is lamentation? Monks, whatever crying, grieving, lamenting, weeping, wailing, lamentation of anyone suffering from misfortune, touched by a painful thing, that is called lamentation.
<p n="392" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">392</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Mayhaṃ dhītā, mayhaṃ putto’’ti evaṃ ādissa ādissa devanti paridevanti etenāti <hi rend="bold">ādevo.</hi> Taṃ taṃ vaṇṇaṃ parikittetvā devanti etenāti <hi rend="bold">paridevo.</hi> Tato parā dve tasseva bhāvaniddesā.</p>
392. "I have a daughter, I have a son," thus, again and again, they lament, therefore it is called *ādevo*.

Praising that, that quality, they lament, therefore it is called *paridevo*.

Thereafter, there are two further explanations of feeling (bhāva).
393
<p n="393" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">393</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Katamañca <pb ed="T" n="2.0342"/>, bhikkhave, dukkhaṃ? Yaṃ kho, bhikkhave, kāyikaṃ dukkhaṃ kāyikaṃ asātaṃ kāyasamphassajaṃ dukkhaṃ asātaṃ vedayitaṃ, idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, dukkhaṃ.</p>
Monks, what is suffering? Monks, whatever bodily pain (kāyikaṃ dukkhaṃ), bodily discomfort (kāyikaṃ asātaṃ), painful or uncomfortable feeling born of bodily contact (kāyasamphassajaṃ dukkhaṃ asātaṃ vedayitaṃ) is, that, monks, is called suffering (dukkhaṃ).
<p n="393" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">393</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi><hi rend="bold">Kāyika</hi>nti kāyapasādavatthukaṃ. Dukkhamanaṭṭhena <hi rend="bold">dukkhaṃ. Asāta</hi>nti amadhuraṃ. <hi rend="bold">Kāyasamphassajaṃ dukkha</hi>nti kāyasamphassato jātaṃ dukkhaṃ. <hi rend="bold">Asātaṃ vedayita</hi>nti amadhuraṃ vedayitaṃ.</p>
*Kāyika* means having a physical basis.

*Dukkhaṃ* [means] *duḥkha* due to [its] undesirable nature.

*Asātaṃ* means unpleasant.

*Kāyasamphassajaṃ dukkhaṃ* means suffering born from physical contact.

*Asātaṃ vedayitaṃ* means unpleasant feeling.
394
<p n="394" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">394</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Katamañca <pb ed="V" n="2.0229"/>, bhikkhave, domanassaṃ? Yaṃ kho, bhikkhave, cetasikaṃ dukkhaṃ cetasikaṃ asātaṃ manosamphassajaṃ dukkhaṃ asātaṃ vedayitaṃ, idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, domanassaṃ.</p>
Monks, what is sadness (*daumanasya*)? Monks, whatever mental pain, mental discomfort, pain or discomfort born of mental contact, that is called sadness.
<p n="394" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">394</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi><hi rend="bold">Cetasika</hi>nti cittasampayuttaṃ. Sesaṃ dukkhe vuttanayameva.</p>
394. *cetasika* means associated with mind (*cittasampayuttaṃ*).

The rest is as already stated in the section on suffering (*dukkhe vuttanayameva*).
395
<p n="395" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">395</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, upāyāso? Yo kho, bhikkhave, aññataraññatarena byasanena samannāgatassa aññataraññatarena dukkhadhammena phuṭṭhassa āyāso upāyāso āyāsitattaṃ upāyāsitattaṃ, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, upāyāso.</p>
‘‘And what, monks, is distress (upāyāso)? Whenever, monks, anyone suffering from misfortune is touched by something of a painful nature, that is distress, great distress, affliction with distress, with great distress; this, monks, is called distress (upāyāso).
<p n="395" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">395</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi><hi rend="bold">Āyāso</hi>ti saṃsīdanavisīdanākārappatto cittakilamatho. Balavataraṃ āyāso <hi rend="bold">upāyāso.</hi> Tato parā dve attattaniyābhāvadīpakā bhāvaniddesā.</p>
<p n="395" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">395</hi><hi rend="dot">.
</hi><hi rend="bold">āyāso</hi>ti: weariness is the mental fatigue consisting of sinking and collapsing.
A stronger weariness is <hi rend="bold">upāyāsa</hi>.
</hi> After that, two further instructions on mental development elucidate the absence of self and what belongs to self ( *ātman* and *ātmānīya* ).
</p>
396
<p n="396" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">396</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Katamo <pb ed="M" n="2.0245"/> ca, bhikkhave, appiyehi sampayogo dukkho? Idha yassa te honti aniṭṭhā akantā amanāpā rūpā saddā gandhā rasā phoṭṭhabbā dhammā, ye vā panassa te honti anatthakāmā ahitakāmā aphāsukakāmā ayogakkhemakāmā, yā tehi saddhiṃ saṅgati samāgamo samodhānaṃ missībhāvo, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, appiyehi sampayogo dukkho.</p>
"396. Furthermore, monks, what is the suffering of association with the unpleasant (appiyehi sampayogo dukkho)? Here, whatever unwanted (aniṭṭhā), disliked (akantā), unpleasant (amanāpā) sights (rūpā), sounds (saddā), smells (gandhā), tastes (rasā), tangibles (phoṭṭhabbā), or mental objects (dhammā) there are; or whoever wishes for their harm (anatthakāmā), injury (ahitakāmā), discomfort (aphāsukakāmā), or insecurity (ayogakkhemakāmā); the encounter (saṅgati), meeting (samāgamo), union (samodhānaṃ), mixing (missībhāvo) with them—this is called, monks, the suffering of association with the unpleasant (appiyehi sampayogo dukkho)."
397
<p n="397" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">397</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, piyehi vippayogo dukkho? Idha yassa te honti iṭṭhā kantā manāpā rūpā saddā gandhā rasā phoṭṭhabbā dhammā, ye vā panassa te honti atthakāmā hitakāmā phāsukakāmā yogakkhemakāmā mātā vā pitā vā bhātā vā bhaginī vā mittā vā amaccā vā ñātisālohitā vā, yā tehi saddhiṃ asaṅgati asamāgamo asamodhānaṃ amissībhāvo, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, piyehi vippayogo dukkho.</p>
Monks, what is the suffering of separation from the loved? Here, whoever has desirable, pleasing, attractive sights (rūpa), sounds (sadda), smells (gandha), tastes (rasa), tangibles (phoṭṭhabba), or mind-objects (dhamma); or whoever encounters well-wishers (atthakāmā), wishers of good (hitakāmā), of comfort (phāsukakāmā), of security (yogakkhemakāmā), mother (mātā) or father (pitā) or brother (bhātā) or sister (bhaginī) or friends (mittā) or colleagues (amaccā) or blood-relations (ñātisālohitā), and then is deprived of such concourse (asaṅgati), intercourse (asamāgamo), connection (asamodhānaṃ), or union (amissībhāvo), that, monks, is called being separated from the loved (piyehi vippayogo dukkho).
398
<p n="398" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">398</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Katamañca <pb ed="T" n="2.0343"/><pb ed="P" n="2.0307"/>, bhikkhave, yampicchaṃ na labhati tampi dukkhaṃ? Jātidhammānaṃ, bhikkhave, sattānaṃ evaṃ icchā uppajjati – ‘aho vata mayaṃ na jātidhammā assāma, na ca vata no jāti āgaccheyyā’ti. Na kho panetaṃ icchāya pattabbaṃ, idampi yampicchaṃ na labhati tampi dukkhaṃ. Jarādhammānaṃ, bhikkhave, sattānaṃ evaṃ icchā uppajjati – ‘aho vata mayaṃ na jarādhammā assāma, na ca vata no jarā āgaccheyyā’ti. Na kho panetaṃ icchāya pattabbaṃ, idampi yampicchaṃ na labhati tampi dukkhaṃ. Byādhidhammānaṃ, bhikkhave, sattānaṃ evaṃ icchā uppajjati ‘aho vata mayaṃ na byādhidhammā assāma, na ca vata no byādhi āgaccheyyā’ti. Na kho panetaṃ icchāya pattabbaṃ, idampi yampicchaṃ na labhati tampi dukkhaṃ. Maraṇadhammānaṃ, bhikkhave, sattānaṃ evaṃ icchā uppajjati ‘aho vata mayaṃ na maraṇadhammā assāma, na ca vata no maraṇaṃ āgaccheyyā’ti. Na kho panetaṃ icchāya pattabbaṃ, idampi yampicchaṃ na labhati tampi dukkhaṃ. Sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsadhammānaṃ, bhikkhave, sattānaṃ evaṃ <pb ed="V" n="2.0230"/> icchā uppajjati ‘aho vata mayaṃ na sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsadhammā assāma, na ca vata no sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsadhammā āgaccheyyu’nti. Na kho panetaṃ icchāya pattabbaṃ, idampi yampicchaṃ na labhati tampi dukkhaṃ.</p>
398.
"And what, monks, is the suffering of not getting what one wants (yampicchaṃ na labhati tampi dukkhaṃ)? In beings subject to birth (jātidhammānaṃ sattānaṃ), monks, the wish arises, ‘Oh that we were not subject to birth (na jātidhammā assāma), and that birth (jāti) might not come to us (na ca jāti āgaccheyyā)!’

But this cannot be gained by wishing (na kho panetaṃ icchāya pattabbaṃ). That is not getting what one wants (yampicchaṃ na labhati) as suffering (tampi dukkhaṃ).

In beings subject to aging (jarādhammānaṃ sattānaṃ), monks, the wish arises, ‘Oh that we were not subject to aging (na jarādhammā assāma), and that aging (jarā) might not come to us!’

But this cannot be gained by wishing (na kho panetaṃ icchāya pattabbaṃ). That is not getting what one wants (yampicchaṃ na labhati) as suffering (tampi dukkhaṃ).

In beings subject to illness (byādhidhammānaṃ sattānaṃ), monks, the wish arises, ‘Oh that we were not subject to illness (na byādhidhammā assāma), and that illness (byādhi) might not come to us!’

But this cannot be gained by wishing (na kho panetaṃ icchāya pattabbaṃ). That is not getting what one wants (yampicchaṃ na labhati) as suffering (tampi dukkhaṃ).

In beings subject to death (maraṇadhammānaṃ sattānaṃ), monks, the wish arises, ‘Oh that we were not subject to death (na maraṇadhammā assāma), and that death (maraṇaṃ) might not come to us!’

But this cannot be gained by wishing (na kho panetaṃ icchāya pattabbaṃ). That is not getting what one wants (yampicchaṃ na labhati) as suffering (tampi dukkhaṃ).

In beings subject to sorrow, lamentation, pain, distress, and despair (sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsadhammānaṃ sattānaṃ), monks, the wish arises, ‘Oh that we were not subject to sorrow, lamentation, pain, distress, and despair (na sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsadhammā assāma), and that sorrow, lamentation, pain, distress, and despair (sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsadhammā) might not come to us!’

But this cannot be gained by wishing (na kho panetaṃ icchāya pattabbaṃ). That is not getting what one wants (yampicchaṃ na labhati) as suffering (tampi dukkhaṃ)."
<p n="398" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">398</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi><hi rend="bold">Jātidhammāna</hi>nti <pb ed="V" n="2.0350"/> jātisabhāvānaṃ. <hi rend="bold">Icchā uppajjatī</hi>ti taṇhā uppajjati. <hi rend="bold">Aho vatā</hi>ti patthanā. <hi rend="bold">Na kho panetaṃ icchāyā</hi>ti evaṃ jātiyā anāgamanaṃ vinā maggabhāvanaṃ na icchāya pattabbaṃ. <hi rend="bold">Idampī</hi>ti etampi upari sesāni upādāya pikāro. <hi rend="bold">Yampiccha</hi>nti yenapi dhammena alabbhaneyyavatthuṃ icchanto na labhati, taṃ alabbhaneyya vatthumhi icchanaṃ dukkhaṃ. Esa nayo sabbattha.</p>
"***jātidhammāna***nti" means *jāti* nature.

"***icchā uppajjatī***ti" means *taṇhā* arises.

"***aho vatā***ti" means desire.

"***na kho panetaṃ icchāyā***ti" means thus, without the path practice, not returning by means of *jāti* is not attainable by desire.

"***idampī***ti" means even this, taking the remainder into account, is a *pikāro*.

"***yampiccha***nti" means by whatever *dhamma* one desiring an unattainable thing does not obtain it, that desire for an unattainable thing is suffering (*dukkha*).

This method applies to all the other cases.
399
<p n="399" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">399</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Katame <pb ed="M" n="2.0246"/> ca, bhikkhave, saṅkhittena pañcupādānakkhandhā dukkhā? Seyyathidaṃ – rūpupādānakkhandho, vedanupādānakkhandho, saññupādānakkhandho, saṅkhārupādānakkhandho, viññāṇupādānakkhandho. Ime vuccanti, bhikkhave, saṅkhittena pañcupādānakkhandhā dukkhā. Idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, dukkhaṃ ariyasaccaṃ.</p> <p rend="subhead">Samudayasaccaniddeso</p>
Monks, what in brief, are the five aggregates of clinging that are suffering (pañcupādānakkhandhā duhkha)? They are: the aggregate of material form as an object of clinging (rūpupādānakkhandha), the aggregate of feeling as an object of clinging (vedanupādānakkhandha), the aggregate of perception as an object of clinging (saññupādānakkhandha), the aggregate of mental formations as an object of clinging (saṅkhārupādānakkhandha), and the aggregate of consciousness as an object of clinging (viññāṇupādānakkhandha). These, monks, are called, in brief, the five aggregates of clinging that are suffering. This, monks, is called the noble truth of suffering (dukkhaṃ ariyasaccaṃ).

Explanation of the Truth of Origin
<p n="399" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">399</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> Khandhaniddese <pb ed="M" n="2.0389"/> rūpañca taṃ upādānakkhandho cāti <hi rend="bold">rūpupādānakkhandho</hi> evaṃ sabbattha.</p> <p rend="subhead">Samudayasaccaniddesavaṇṇanā</p>
Section 399.
In the *khandha*-analysis, rūpa and that which is the aggregate of clinging (*upādānakkhandha*) is the *rūpupādānakkhandha*; thus everywhere.

Explanation of the analysis of the truth of arising (*samudayasaccaniddesavaṇṇanā*).
400
<p n="400" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">400</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Katamañca <pb ed="P" n="2.0308"/>, bhikkhave, dukkhasamudayaṃ <note>dukkhasamudayo (syā.)</note> ariyasaccaṃ? Yāyaṃ taṇhā ponobbhavikā <note>ponobhavikā (sī. pī.)</note> nandīrāgasahagatā <note>nandirāgasahagatā (sī. syā. pī.)</note> tatratatrābhinandinī, seyyathidaṃ – kāmataṇhā bhavataṇhā vibhavataṇhā.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Sā kho panesā, bhikkhave, taṇhā kattha uppajjamānā uppajjati, kattha nivisamānā nivisati? Yaṃ loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā <pb ed="T" n="2.0344"/> taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati.</p> <pb ed="T" n="2.0344"/> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Kiñca loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ? Cakkhu loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati. Sotaṃ loke…pe… ghānaṃ loke… jivhā loke… kāyo loke… mano loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Rūpā loke… saddā loke… gandhā loke… rasā loke… phoṭṭhabbā loke… dhammā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Cakkhuviññāṇaṃ loke… sotaviññāṇaṃ loke… ghānaviññāṇaṃ loke… jivhāviññāṇaṃ loke… kāyaviññāṇaṃ loke… manoviññāṇaṃ loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Cakkhusamphasso <pb ed="V" n="2.0231"/> loke… sotasamphasso loke… ghānasamphasso loke… jivhāsamphasso <pb ed="P" n="2.0309"/> loke… kāyasamphasso loke… manosamphasso loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati.</p> <pb ed="V" n="2.0231"/> <pb ed="P" n="2.0309"/> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Cakkhusamphassajā <pb ed="M" n="2.0247"/> vedanā loke… sotasamphassajā vedanā loke… ghānasamphassajā vedanā loke… jivhāsamphassajā vedanā loke… kāyasamphassajā vedanā loke… manosamphassajā vedanā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha <pb ed="T" n="2.0345"/> nivisamānā nivisati.</p> <pb ed="M" n="2.0247"/> <pb ed="T" n="2.0345"/> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Rūpasaññā loke… saddasaññā loke… gandhasaññā loke… rasasaññā loke… phoṭṭhabbasaññā loke… dhammasaññā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Rūpasañcetanā loke… saddasañcetanā loke… gandhasañcetanā loke… rasasañcetanā loke… phoṭṭhabbasañcetanā loke… dhammasañcetanā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Rūpataṇhā loke… saddataṇhā loke… gandhataṇhā loke… rasataṇhā loke… phoṭṭhabbataṇhā loke… dhammataṇhā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Rūpavitakko loke… saddavitakko loke… gandhavitakko loke… rasavitakko loke… phoṭṭhabbavitakko loke… dhammavitakko loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Rūpavicāro loke… saddavicāro loke… gandhavicāro loke… rasavicāro loke… phoṭṭhabbavicāro loke… dhammavicāro loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā <pb ed="P" n="2.0310"/> uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati. Idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, dukkhasamudayaṃ ariyasaccaṃ.</p> <pb ed="P" n="2.0310"/> <p rend="subhead">Nirodhasaccaniddeso</p>
400. "And what, monks, is the noble truth of the origin of suffering ( *duḥkhasamudayaṃ āryasatyaṃ*)? It is that craving (*tṛṣṇā*) which gives rise to rebirth, bound up with pleasure and lust, finding fresh delight now here, now there: that is to say, craving for sensual pleasure (*kāmatṛṣṇā*), craving for existence (*bhavatṛṣṇā*), and craving for non-existence (*vibhavatṛṣṇā*).

"But where, monks, does this craving arise, and where does it establish itself? Wherever there is what is likable and agreeable in the world, there this craving arises, and there it establishes itself.

"And what is endearing & alluring in terms of the world? The eye (*cakṣu*) in the world is agreeable and pleasurable, and there this craving arises, and there it dwells. The ear (*śrotra*) ... the nose (*ghāṇa*) ... the tongue (*jihvā*) ... the body (*kāya*) ... the mind (*manas*) in the world is agreeable and pleasurable, and there this craving arises, and there it dwells.

"Forms (*rūpa*) in the world... sounds (*śabda*) ... smells (*gandha*) ... tastes (*rasa*) ... tangible objects (*sparśa*) ... ideas (*dharma*) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable, and there this craving arises, and there it dwells.

"Eye-consciousness (*cakṣurvijñāna*) ... ear-consciousness (*śrotravijñāna*) ... nose-consciousness (*ghrāṇavijñāna*) ... tongue-consciousness (*jihvāvijñāna*) ... body-consciousness (*kāyavijñāna*) ... mind-consciousness (*manovijñāna*) in the world is agreeable and pleasurable, and there this craving arises, and there it dwells.

"Eye-contact (*cakṣu-saṃsparśa*) ... ear-contact (*śrotra-saṃsparśa*) ... nose-contact (*ghrāṇa-saṃsparśa*) ... tongue-contact (*jihvā-saṃsparśa*) ... body-contact (*kāya-saṃsparśa*) ... mind-contact (*mano-saṃsparśa*) in the world is agreeable and pleasurable, and there this craving arises, and there it dwells.

"Feeling born of eye-contact (*cakṣu-saṃsparśa-jā vedanā*) ... feeling born of ear-contact (*śrotra-saṃsparśa-jā vedanā*) ... feeling born of nose-contact (*ghrāṇa-saṃsparśa-jā vedanā*) ... feeling born of tongue-contact (*jihvā-saṃsparśa-jā vedanā*) ... feeling born of body-contact (*kāya-saṃsparśa-jā vedanā*) ... feeling born of mind-contact (*mano-saṃsparśa-jā vedanā*) in the world is agreeable and pleasurable, and there this craving arises, and there it dwells.

"Perception of forms (*rūpasaṃjñā*) ... perception of sounds (*śabdasaṃjñā*) ... perception of smells (*gandhasaṃjñā*) ... perception of tastes (*rasasaṃjñā*) ... perception of tangible objects (*sparśasaṃjñā*) ... perception of ideas (*dharmasaṃjñā*) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable, and there this craving arises, and there it dwells.

"Intention for forms (*rūpasaṃcetanā*) ... intention for sounds (*śabdasaṃcetanā*) ... intention for smells (*gandhasaṃcetanā*) ... intention for tastes (*rasasaṃcetanā*) ... intention for tangible objects (*sparśasaṃcetanā*) ... intention for ideas (*dharmasaṃcetanā*) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable, and there this craving arises, and there it dwells.

"Craving for forms (*rūpatṛṣṇā*) ... craving for sounds (*śabdatṛṣṇā*) ... craving for smells (*gandhatṛṣṇā*) ... craving for tastes (*rasatṛṣṇā*) ... craving for tangible objects (*sparśatṛṣṇā*) ... craving for ideas (*dharmatṛṣṇā*) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable, and there this craving arises, and there it dwells.

"Thought directed at forms (*rūpavitarka*) ... thought directed at sounds (*śabdavitarka*) ... thought directed at smells (*gandhavitarka*) ... thought directed at tastes (*rasavitarka*) ... thought directed at tangible objects (*sparśavitarka*) ... thought directed at ideas (*dharmavitarka*) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable, and there this craving arises, and there it dwells.

"Evaluation of forms (*rūpavicāra*) ... evaluation of sounds (*śabdavicāra*) ... evaluation of smells (*gandhavicāra*) ... evaluation of tastes (*rasavicāra*) ... evaluation of tangible objects (*sparśavicāra*) ... evaluation of ideas (*dharmavicāra*) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable, and there this craving arises, and there it dwells.

This, monks, is called the noble truth of the origin of suffering (*duḥkhasamudayaṃ āryasatyaṃ*)."

Explanation of the Truth of Cessation (*nirodhasatyanirdeśa*).
<p n="400" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">400</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi><hi rend="bold">Yāyaṃ taṇhā</hi>ti yā ayaṃ taṇhā. <hi rend="bold">Ponobbhavikā</hi>ti punabbhavakaraṇaṃ punobbhavo, punobbhavo sīlaṃ assāti <hi rend="bold">ponobbhavikā.</hi> Nandīrāgena saha gatāti <hi rend="bold">nandīrāgasahagatā.</hi> Nandīrāgena saddhiṃ atthato ekattameva gatāti vuttaṃ hoti. <hi rend="bold">Tatratatrābhinandinī</hi>ti <pb ed="P" n="3.0800"/> yatra yatra attabhāvo, tatra tatra abhinandinī. Rūpādīsu vā ārammaṇesu tatra tatra abhinandinī, rūpābhinandinī sadda, gandha, rasa, phoṭṭhabba, dhammābhinandinīti attho. <hi rend="bold">Seyyathida</hi>nti nipāto. Tassa sā katamā ceti attho. Kāme taṇhā <hi rend="bold">kāmataṇhā,</hi> pañcakāmaguṇikarāgassetaṃ nāmaṃ. Bhave taṇhā <hi rend="bold">bhavataṇhā,</hi> bhavapatthanāvasena uppannassa sassatadiṭṭhisahagatassa rūpārūpabhavarāgassa ca jhānanikantiyā cetaṃ adhivacanaṃ. Vibhave taṇhā <hi rend="bold">vibhavataṇhā,</hi> ucchedadiṭṭhisahagatarāgassetaṃ adhivacanaṃ.</p> <p rend="bodytext">Idāni tassā taṇhāya vatthuṃ vitthārato dassetuṃ <hi rend="bold">sā kho panesā</hi>tiādimāha. Tattha <hi rend="bold">uppajjatī</hi>ti jāyati. <hi rend="bold">Nivisatī</hi>ti punappunaṃ pavattivasena patiṭṭhahati. <hi rend="bold">Yaṃ loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpa</hi>nti yaṃ lokasmiṃ piyasabhāvañceva madhurasabhāvañca. <hi rend="bold">Cakkhu loke</hi>tiādīsu lokasmiñhi cakkhādīsu mamattena abhiniviṭṭhā sattā sampattiyaṃ patiṭṭhitā attano cakkhuṃ ādāsatalādīsu nimittaggahaṇānusārena vippasannaṃ pañcapasādaṃ suvaṇṇavimāne ugghāṭitamaṇisīhapañjaraṃ viya maññanti, sotaṃ rajatapanāḷikaṃ viya, pāmaṅgasuttaṃ viya ca maññanti, ‘‘tuṅganāsā’’ti laddhavohāraṃ ghānaṃ vaṭṭitvā ṭhapitaharitālavaṭṭaṃ viya maññanti, jivhaṃ rattakambalapaṭalaṃ viya mudusiniddhamadhurasadaṃ maññanti, kāyaṃ sālalaṭṭhiṃ viya, suvaṇṇatoraṇaṃ viya ca maññanti, manaṃ aññesaṃ manena asadisaṃ uḷāraṃ maññanti. Rūpaṃ suvaṇṇakaṇikārapupphādivaṇṇaṃ viya, saddaṃ mattakaravīka kokilamandadhamitamaṇivaṃsanigghosaṃ viya, attanā paṭiladdhāni catusamuṭṭhānikagandhārammaṇādīni <pb ed="V" n="2.0351"/> ‘‘kassaññassa evarūpāni atthī’’ti maññanti. Tesaṃ evaṃ maññamānānaṃ tāni cakkhādīni piyarūpāni ceva sātarūpāni ca honti. Atha nesaṃ tattha anuppannā ceva taṇhā uppajjati <pb ed="M" n="2.0390"/>, uppannā ca taṇhā punappunaṃ pavattivasena nivisati. Tasmā bhagavā ‘‘cakkhu loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjatī’’tiādimāha. Tattha <hi rend="bold">uppajjamānā</hi>ti yadā uppajjamānā hoti, tadā ettha uppajjatīti attho. Esa nayo sabbattha.</p> <hi rend="bold">sā kho panesā</hi> <hi rend="bold">uppajjatī</hi> <hi rend="bold">Nivisatī</hi> <hi rend="bold">Yaṃ loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpa</hi> <hi rend="bold">Cakkhu loke</hi> <pb ed="V" n="2.0351"/> <pb ed="M" n="2.0390"/> <hi rend="bold">uppajjamānā</hi> <p rend="subhead">Nirodhasaccaniddesavaṇṇanā</p>
400. *yāyaṃ taṇhā* means this craving which is this craving.

*ponobbhavikā* means that which causes rebirth, rebirth is virtue, *ponobbhavikā* possesses virtue.

*nandīrāgena saha gatāti* means accompanied by delight and greed, *nandīrāgasahagatā*.

*nandīrāgena saddhiṃ atthato ekattameva gatāti vuttaṃ hoti* means that it is identical in meaning with delight and greed.

*tatratatrābhinandinī* means concerned with this and that: wherever personality is generated there is concern with that.

*rūpādīsu vā ārammaṇesu tatra tatra abhinandinī, rūpābhinandinī sadda, gandha, rasa, phoṭṭhabba, dhammābhinandinīti attho* means concerned with this and that in forms, etc., or objects, rejoicing in form, sound, odor, taste, tactile object, mental object.

*seyyathida*nti is a particle.

*tassa sā katamā ceti attho* means what is that?

*kāme taṇhā kāmataṇhā,* means craving in desire is desire-craving; this is a name for the greed that has the five sense qualities as its objects.

*bhave taṇhā bhavataṇhā,* means craving in existence is existence-craving; this term is an epithet for the attachment to existence in the realms of form and the formless, which arises through wanting to be reborn and is accompanied by the eternalist view and delight in absorption (*dhyāna*).

*vibhave taṇhā vibhavataṇhā,* means craving in non-existence is non-existence-craving; this term is an epithet for the greed accompanied by the annihilationist view.

Now, to show in detail the basis of that craving, he said, "*sā kho panesā*," and so forth.

There, *uppajjati* means it is born.

*nivisatī* means it becomes repeatedly established through continuity.

*yaṃ loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpa* means whatever in the world is of an endearing and pleasurable nature.

*cakkhu loke* etc., means in the world, beings are attached with possessiveness to the eye, etc., established in prosperity. They consider their own eye—clear, possessing the five kinds of luminosity, like a jewel cage opened in a golden airplane—in accordance with how they apprehend signs in mirrors, etc. They consider the ear like a silver pipe, and they consider the nose, which has gained the designation "high-nosed," like a circle of orpiment placed in a circle. They consider the tongue like a red blanket, soft, smooth, and of sweet taste. They consider the body like a *sāla* rod, and they consider the mind, unlike the minds of others, magnificent.

Form is like the color of golden *kaṇikāra* flowers, etc. Sound is like the sound of a drunk *karavīka* bird, a cuckoo, the soft sound of a blown jeweled flute. They think, "Others do not have such things," the four elements, the object of smell, etc., which they have obtained for themselves.

In this way, those who think in this way, those eyes, etc., are endearing and pleasurable.

Then, craving, which has not yet arisen in them, arises; and craving, which has arisen, repeatedly dwells through continuity.

Therefore, the Blessed One said, "The eye in the world is agreeable and pleasurable: therein this craving arises, therein it dwells," and so forth.

There, *uppajjamānā* means when it is in the process of arising, then craving arises in that.

This method applies to all the other cases .

*sā kho panesā, uppajjati, nivisatī, yaṃ loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpa, cakkhu loke, uppajjamānā*

Explanation of the discourse on the truth of cessation.
401
<p n="401" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">401</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Katamañca <pb ed="V" n="2.0232"/>, bhikkhave, dukkhanirodhaṃ <note>dukkhanirodho (syā.)</note> ariyasaccaṃ? Yo tassāyeva <pb ed="T" n="2.0346"/> taṇhāya asesavirāganirodho cāgo paṭinissaggo mutti anālayo.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Sā <pb ed="M" n="2.0248"/> kho panesā, bhikkhave, taṇhā kattha pahīyamānā pahīyati, kattha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati? Yaṃ loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati.</p> <pb ed="M" n="2.0248"/> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Kiñca loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ? Cakkhu loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati. Sotaṃ loke…pe… ghānaṃ loke… jivhā loke… kāyo loke… mano loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Rūpā loke… saddā loke… gandhā loke… rasā loke… phoṭṭhabbā loke… dhammā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Cakkhuviññāṇaṃ loke… sotaviññāṇaṃ loke… ghānaviññāṇaṃ loke… jivhāviññāṇaṃ loke… kāyaviññāṇaṃ loke… manoviññāṇaṃ loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Cakkhusamphasso loke… sotasamphasso loke… ghānasamphasso loke… jivhāsamphasso loke… kāyasamphasso loke… manosamphasso <pb ed="P" n="2.0311"/> loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati.</p> <pb ed="P" n="2.0311"/> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Cakkhusamphassajā vedanā loke… sotasamphassajā vedanā loke <pb ed="T" n="2.0347"/>… ghānasamphassajā vedanā loke… jivhāsamphassajā vedanā loke… kāyasamphassajā vedanā loke… manosamphassajā vedanā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati.</p> <pb ed="T" n="2.0347"/> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Rūpasaññā <pb ed="V" n="2.0233"/> loke… saddasaññā loke… gandhasaññā loke… rasasaññā loke… phoṭṭhabbasaññā loke… dhammasaññā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati.</p> <pb ed="V" n="2.0233"/> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Rūpasañcetanā <pb ed="M" n="2.0249"/> loke… saddasañcetanā loke… gandhasañcetanā loke… rasasañcetanā loke… phoṭṭhabbasañcetanā loke… dhammasañcetanā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati.</p> <pb ed="M" n="2.0249"/> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Rūpataṇhā loke… saddataṇhā loke… gandhataṇhā loke… rasataṇhā loke… phoṭṭhabbataṇhā loke… dhammataṇhā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Rūpavitakko loke… saddavitakko loke… gandhavitakko loke… rasavitakko loke… phoṭṭhabbavitakko loke… dhammavitakko loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Rūpavicāro loke… saddavicāro loke… gandhavicāro loke… rasavicāro loke… phoṭṭhabbavicāro loke… dhammavicāro loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ <pb ed="T" n="2.0348"/>, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati. Idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, dukkhanirodhaṃ ariyasaccaṃ.</p> <pb ed="T" n="2.0348"/> <p rend="subhead">Maggasaccaniddeso</p>
401. "And what, monks, is the noble truth of the cessation of suffering (duḥkhanirodhaṃ āryasatyaṃ)? It is the complete fading away and cessation of that very thirst (tṛṣṇā), its abandonment, relinquishment, liberation, and detachment.

"And where, monks, is this thirst abandoned, where does it cease? Wherever in the world there is what is agreeable and pleasurable (priyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ), there this thirst is abandoned, there it ceases.

"And what in the world is agreeable and pleasurable? The eye (cakṣu) in the world is agreeable and pleasurable; there this thirst is abandoned, there it ceases. The ear (śrotra) in the world... the nose (ghāṇa) in the world... the tongue (jihvā) in the world... the body (kāya) in the world... the mind (manas) in the world is agreeable and pleasurable; there this thirst is abandoned, there it ceases.

"Forms (rūpa) in the world... sounds (śabda) in the world... smells (gandha) in the world... tastes (rasa) in the world... tactile objects (sparśa) in the world... mental objects (dharma) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this thirst is abandoned, there it ceases.

"Eye-consciousness (cakṣurvijñānaṃ) in the world... ear-consciousness (śrotravijñānaṃ) in the world... nose-consciousness (ghāṇavijñānaṃ) in the world... tongue-consciousness (jihvāvijñānaṃ) in the world... body-consciousness (kāyavijñānaṃ) in the world... mind-consciousness (manovijñānaṃ) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this thirst is abandoned, there it ceases.

"Eye-contact (cakṣuḥsamsparśa) in the world... ear-contact (śrotrasamsparśa) in the world... nose-contact (ghāṇasamsparśa) in the world... tongue-contact (jihvāsamsparśa) in the world... body-contact (kāyasamsparśa) in the world... mind-contact (manasamsparśa) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this thirst is abandoned, there it ceases.

"Feeling born of eye-contact (cakṣuḥsamsparśajā vedanā) in the world... feeling born of ear-contact (śrotrasamsparśajā vedanā) in the world... feeling born of nose-contact (ghāṇasamsparśajā vedanā) in the world... feeling born of tongue-contact (jihvāsamsparśajā vedanā) in the world... feeling born of body-contact (kāyasamsparśajā vedanā) in the world... feeling born of mind-contact (manasamsparśajā vedanā) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this thirst is abandoned, there it ceases.

"Perception of form (rūpasaṃjñā) in the world... perception of sound (śabdasaṃjñā) in the world... perception of smell (gandhasaṃjñā) in the world... perception of taste (rasasaṃjñā) in the world... perception of tactile object (sparśasaṃjñā) in the world... perception of mental object (dharmasaṃjñā) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this thirst is abandoned, there it ceases.
