"Volition regarding form (rūpasaṃcetanā) in the world... volition regarding sound (śabdasaṃcetanā) in the world... volition regarding smell (gandhasaṃcetanā) in the world... volition regarding taste (rasasaṃcetanā) in the world... volition regarding tactile object (sparśasaṃcetanā) in the world... volition regarding mental object (dharmasaṃcetanā) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this thirst is abandoned, there it ceases.

"Craving for form (rūpataṇhā) in the world... craving for sound (śabdataṇhā) in the world... craving for smell (gandhataṇhā) in the world... craving for taste (rasataṇhā) in the world... craving for tactile object (sparśataṇhā) in the world... craving for mental object (dharmataṇhā) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this thirst is abandoned, there it ceases.

"Thinking about form (rūpavitarka) in the world... thinking about sound (śabdavitarka) in the world... thinking about smell (gandhavitarka) in the world... thinking about taste (rasavitarka) in the world... thinking about tactile object (sparśavitarka) in the world... thinking about mental object (dharmavitarka) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this thirst is abandoned, there it ceases.

"Pondering on form (rūpavicāra) in the world... pondering on sound (śabdavicāra) in the world... pondering on smell (gandhavicāra) in the world... pondering on taste (rasavicāra) in the world... pondering on tactile object (sparśavicāra) in the world... pondering on mental object (dharmavicāra) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this thirst is abandoned, there it ceases.

This, monks, is called the noble truth of the cessation of suffering (duḥkhanirodhaṃ āryasatyaṃ)."

*Explanation of the Truth of the Path*
<p n="401" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">401</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi><hi rend="bold">Asesavirāganirodho</hi>tiādīni sabbāni nibbānavevacanāneva. Nibbānañhi āgamma taṇhā asesā virajjati nirujjhati, tasmā taṃ ‘‘tassāyeva taṇhāya asesavirāganirodho’’ti vuccati. Nibbānañca āgamma taṇhā cajiyati paṭinissajjiyati vimuccati na <pb ed="P" n="3.0801"/> allīyati, tasmā nibbānaṃ ‘‘cāgo paṭinissaggo mutti anālayo’’ti vuccati. Ekameva hi nibbānaṃ, nāmāni panassa sabbasaṅkhatānaṃ nāmapaṭipakkhavasena anekāni honti. Seyyathidaṃ, asesavirāgo asesanirodho cāgo paṭinissaggo mutti anālayo rāgakkhayo dosakkhayo mohakkhayo taṇhakkhayo anuppādo appavattaṃ animittaṃ appaṇihitaṃ anāyūhanaṃ appaṭisandhi anupapatti agati ajātaṃ ajaraṃ abyādhi amataṃ asokaṃ aparidevaṃ anupāyāsaṃ asaṃkiliṭṭhanti.</p> <p rend="bodytext">Idāni maggena chinnāya nibbānaṃ āgamma appavattipattāyapi ca taṇhāya yesu vatthūsu tassā uppatti dassitā, tattheva abhāvaṃ dassetuṃ <hi rend="bold">sā kho panesā</hi>tiādimāha. Tattha yathā puriso khette jātaṃ tittaalābuvalliṃ disvā aggato paṭṭhāya mūlaṃ pariyesitvā chindeyya, sā anupubbena milāyitvā apaññattiṃ gaccheyya. Tato tasmiṃ khette tittaalābu niruddhā pahīnāti vucceyya, evameva khette tittaalābu viya cakkhādīsu taṇhā. Sā ariyamaggena mūlacchinnā nibbānaṃ āgamma appavattiṃ gacchati. Evaṃ gatā pana tesu vatthūsu khette tittaalābu viya na paññāyati.</p> <hi rend="bold">sā kho panesā</hi> <p rend="bodytext">Yathā ca aṭavito core ānetvā nagarassa dakkhiṇadvāre ghāteyyuṃ, tato aṭaviyaṃ corā matāti vā māritāti vā vucceyyuṃ, evaṃ aṭaviyaṃ corā viya cakkhādīsu taṇhā. Sā dakkhiṇadvāre corā viya nibbānaṃ āgamma niruddhattā nibbāne niruddhā. Evaṃ niruddhā panetesu <pb ed="V" n="2.0352"/> vatthūsu aṭaviyaṃ corā viya na paññāyati, tenassā tattheva nirodhaṃ dassento <pb ed="M" n="2.0391"/> ‘‘cakkhu loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhatī’’tiādimāha.</p> <pb ed="V" n="2.0352"/> <pb ed="M" n="2.0391"/> <p rend="subhead">Maggasaccaniddesavaṇṇanā</p>
401. "Asesavirāganirodho," and so forth, are all synonyms for *nirvāṇa*. For, having reached *nirvāṇa*, craving is completely extinguished and ceases. Therefore, that is called "the complete fading away and cessation of that very craving." And having reached *nirvāṇa*, craving is relinquished, abandoned, and liberated; it does not cling. Therefore, *nirvāṇa* is called "relinquishment, abandonment, liberation, non-attachment." Indeed, *nirvāṇa* is one, but its names are many, according to the opposing aspects of all conditioned things. Namely, complete fading away, complete cessation, relinquishment, abandonment, liberation, non-attachment, destruction of passion, destruction of hatred, destruction of delusion, destruction of craving, non-arising, non-occurrence, signless, undirected, non-accumulation, non-rebirth, non-reappearance, no-destiny, unborn, unaging, without disease, deathless, without sorrow, without lamentation, without tribulation, uncorrupted.

Now, in order to show the absence in those things in which the arising of craving, cut off by the path and attained to non-occurrence having reached *nirvāṇa*, was shown, he says, beginning with "sā kho panesā." There, just as a man, seeing a bitter gourd vine growing in a field, might cut it, seeking out the root from the top, and it would gradually wither away and go to non-cognition; then it would be said, "The bitter gourd in that field is stopped, abandoned"; even so, craving in the eye and so forth is like the bitter gourd in the field. Cut off at the root by the noble path, it goes to non-occurrence having reached *nirvāṇa*. But, having gone thus, it is not perceived in those things, like the bitter gourd in the field.

sā kho panesā Just as having brought thieves from the forest, they might kill them at the southern gate of the city, then it would be said, "The thieves are dead or killed in the forest"; even so, craving in the eye and so forth is like the thieves in the forest. Because of being stopped having reached *nirvāṇa*, like the thieves at the southern gate, it is stopped in *nirvāṇa*. But, having been stopped thus, it is not perceived in these things, like the thieves in the forest. Therefore, showing its cessation right there, he says, beginning with "The eye in the world is agreeable and pleasurable, therein this craving is abandoned, therein it ceases."

Explanation of the Discourse on the Truth of the Path
402
<p n="402" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">402</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Katamañca, bhikkhave, dukkhanirodhagāminī paṭipadā ariyasaccaṃ? Ayameva ariyo aṭṭhaṅgiko maggo seyyathidaṃ – sammādiṭṭhi sammāsaṅkappo sammāvācā sammākammanto sammāājīvo sammāvāyāmo sammāsati sammāsamādhi.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Katamā ca, bhikkhave, sammādiṭṭhi? Yaṃ <pb ed="P" n="2.0312"/> kho, bhikkhave, dukkhe ñāṇaṃ, dukkhasamudaye ñāṇaṃ, dukkhanirodhe ñāṇaṃ, dukkhanirodhagāminiyā paṭipadāya ñāṇaṃ, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, sammādiṭṭhi.</p> <pb ed="P" n="2.0312"/> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Katamo <pb ed="V" n="2.0234"/> ca, bhikkhave, sammāsaṅkappo? Nekkhammasaṅkappo abyāpādasaṅkappo avihiṃsāsaṅkappo, ayaṃ vuccati bhikkhave, sammāsaṅkappo.</p> <pb ed="V" n="2.0234"/> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Katamā <pb ed="M" n="2.0250"/> ca, bhikkhave, sammāvācā? Musāvādā veramaṇī <note>veramaṇi (ka.)</note> pisuṇāya vācāya veramaṇī pharusāya vācāya veramaṇī samphappalāpā veramaṇī, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, sammāvācā.</p> <pb ed="M" n="2.0250"/> <note>veramaṇi (ka.)</note> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, sammākammanto? Pāṇātipātā veramaṇī adinnādānā veramaṇī kāmesumicchācārā veramaṇī, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, sammākammanto.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, sammāājīvo? Idha, bhikkhave, ariyasāvako micchāājīvaṃ pahāya sammāājīvena jīvitaṃ kappeti, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, sammāājīvo.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, sammāvāyāmo? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu anuppannānaṃ pāpakānaṃ akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ anuppādāya chandaṃ janeti vāyamati vīriyaṃ <pb ed="T" n="2.0349"/> ārabhati cittaṃ paggaṇhāti padahati; uppannānaṃ pāpakānaṃ akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ pahānāya chandaṃ janeti vāyamati vīriyaṃ ārabhati cittaṃ paggaṇhāti padahati; anuppannānaṃ kusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ uppādāya chandaṃ janeti vāyamati vīriyaṃ ārabhati cittaṃ paggaṇhāti padahati; uppannānaṃ kusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ ṭhitiyā asammosāya bhiyyobhāvāya <pb ed="P" n="2.0313"/> vepullāya bhāvanāya pāripūriyā chandaṃ janeti vāyamati vīriyaṃ ārabhati cittaṃ paggaṇhāti padahati. Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, sammāvāyāmo.</p> <pb ed="T" n="2.0349"/> <pb ed="P" n="2.0313"/> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Katamā ca, bhikkhave, sammāsati? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati ātāpī sampajāno satimā vineyya loke abhijjhādomanassaṃ; vedanāsu vedanānupassī viharati ātāpī sampajāno satimā vineyya loke abhijjhādomanassaṃ; citte cittānupassī viharati ātāpī sampajāno satimā vineyya loke abhijjhādomanassaṃ; dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati ātāpī sampajāno satimā vineyya loke abhijjhādomanassaṃ. Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, sammāsati.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, sammāsamādhi? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu vivicceva kāmehi vivicca akusalehi dhammehi savitakkaṃ savicāraṃ vivekajaṃ pītisukhaṃ paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Vitakkavicārānaṃ vūpasamā ajjhattaṃ <pb ed="M" n="2.0251"/> sampasādanaṃ cetaso ekodibhāvaṃ avitakkaṃ <pb ed="V" n="2.0235"/> avicāraṃ samādhijaṃ pītisukhaṃ dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Pītiyā ca virāgā upekkhako ca viharati, sato ca sampajāno, sukhañca kāyena paṭisaṃvedeti, yaṃ taṃ ariyā ācikkhanti ‘upekkhako satimā sukhavihārī’ti tatiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Sukhassa ca pahānā dukkhassa ca pahānā pubbeva somanassadomanassānaṃ atthaṅgamā <hi rend="bold">adukkhamasukhaṃ upekkhāsatipārisuddhiṃ</hi> catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave <pb ed="T" n="2.0350"/>, sammāsamādhi. Idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, dukkhanirodhagāminī paṭipadā ariyasaccaṃ.</p> <pb ed="M" n="2.0251"/> <pb ed="V" n="2.0235"/> <hi rend="bold">adukkhamasukhaṃ upekkhāsatipārisuddhiṃ</hi> <pb ed="T" n="2.0350"/>
402. “And what, monks, is the Noble Truth of the Path Leading to the Cessation of Suffering? It is simply this Noble Eightfold Path, namely: right view (*sammā-diṭṭhi*), right intention (*sammā-saṅkappa*), right speech (*sammā-vācā*), right action (*sammā-kammanta*), right livelihood (*sammā-ājīva*), right effort (*sammā-vāyāma*), right mindfulness (*sammā-sati*), right concentration (*sammā-samādhi*).

“And what, monks, is right view (*sammā-diṭṭhi*)? It is, monks, the knowledge of suffering (*dukkha*), the knowledge of the origin of suffering (*dukkha-samudaya*), the knowledge of the cessation of suffering (*dukkha-nirodha*), and the knowledge of the way of practice leading to the cessation of suffering (*dukkha-nirodha-gāminī paṭipadā*). This is called right view (*sammā-diṭṭhi*).

“And what, monks, is right intention (*sammā-saṅkappa*)? The intention of renunciation (*nekkhammasaṅkappa*), the intention of non-ill-will (*abyāpādasaṅkappa*), the intention of harmlessness (*avihiṃsāsaṅkappa*). This is called right intention (*sammā-saṅkappa*).

“And what, monks, is right speech (*sammā-vācā*)? Abstaining from lying (*musāvādā veramaṇī*), from divisive speech (*pisuṇāya vācāya veramaṇī*), from abusive speech (*pharusāya vācāya veramaṇī*), and from idle chatter (*samphappalāpā veramaṇī*). This is called right speech (*sammā-vācā*).

“And what, monks, is right action (*sammā-kammanta*)? Abstaining from killing (*pāṇātipātā veramaṇī*), abstaining from stealing (*adinnādānā veramaṇī*), abstaining from sexual misconduct (*kāmesumicchācārā veramaṇī*). This is called right action (*sammā-kammanta*).

“And what, monks, is right livelihood (*sammā-ājīva*)? Here, monks, a noble disciple, having abandoned wrong livelihood, makes a living by right livelihood. This is called right livelihood (*sammā-ājīva*).

“And what, monks, is right effort (*sammā-vāyāma*)? Here, monks, a monk generates desire, endeavors, stirs up energy, exerts his mind, and strives to prevent the arising of unarisen evil unwholesome mental states; he generates desire, endeavors, stirs up energy, exerts his mind, and strives to overcome evil unwholesome mental states that have arisen; he generates desire, endeavors, stirs up energy, exerts his mind, and strives to produce unarisen wholesome mental states; he generates desire, endeavors, stirs up energy, exerts his mind, and strives to maintain wholesome mental states that have arisen, not to let them fade, to increase them, to develop them, and to bring them to perfection. This is called right effort (*sammā-vāyāma*).

“And what, monks, is right mindfulness (*sammā-sati*)? Here, monks, a monk abides contemplating the body in the body (*kāye kāyānupassī viharati*), ardent (*ātāpī*), clearly aware (*sampajāno*), and mindful (*satimā*), having removed covetousness and displeasure in regard to the world; he abides contemplating feelings in feelings (*vedanāsu vedanānupassī viharati*), ardent, clearly aware, and mindful, having removed covetousness and displeasure in regard to the world; he abides contemplating mind in mind (*citte cittānupassī viharati*), ardent, clearly aware, and mindful, having removed covetousness and displeasure in regard to the world; he abides contemplating mind-objects in mind-objects (*dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati*), ardent, clearly aware, and mindful, having removed covetousness and displeasure in regard to the world. This is called right mindfulness (*sammā-sati*).

“And what, monks, is right concentration (*sammā-samādhi*)? Here, monks, quite secluded from sense pleasures, secluded from unwholesome states, a monk enters and dwells in the first *jhāna*, which is accompanied by thought and examination, with rapture and pleasure born of seclusion. With the stilling of thought and examination, he enters and dwells in the second *jhāna*, which has internal assurance and unification of mind, is without thought and examination, and has rapture and happiness born of concentration. With the fading of rapture, he remains equanimous, mindful, and alert, and experiences pleasure with the body. He enters and dwells in the third *jhāna*, of which the noble ones declare, 'Equanimous and mindful, one has a pleasant abiding.' With the abandoning of pleasure and pain—as with the earlier disappearance of elation and distress—he enters and remains in the fourth *jhāna*, which has purity of mindfulness through equanimity, neither pleasure nor pain (*adukkhamasukhaṃ upekkhāsatipārisuddhiṃ*). This is called right concentration (*sammā-samādhi*).

This, monks, is called the Noble Truth of the Path Leading to the Cessation of Suffering.
<p n="402" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">402</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi><hi rend="bold">Ayamevā</hi>ti aññamaggapaṭikkhepanatthaṃ niyamanaṃ. <hi rend="bold">Ariyo</hi>ti taṃ taṃ maggavajjhehi kilesehi ārakattā ariyabhāvakarattā ca ariyo. <hi rend="bold">Dukkhe ñāṇa</hi>ntiādinā catusaccakammaṭṭhānaṃ dassitaṃ. Tattha purimāni dve saccāni vaṭṭaṃ, pacchimāni vivaṭṭaṃ. Tesu bhikkhuno vaṭṭe kammaṭṭhānābhiniveso hoti, vivaṭṭe natthi abhiniveso. Purimāni hi dve saccāni ‘‘pañcakkhandhā dukkhaṃ, taṇhā samudayo’’ti evaṃ saṅkhepena ca ‘‘katame pañcakkhandhā, rūpakkhandho’’tiādinā nayena vitthārena ca ācariyassa santike uggaṇhitvā vācāya punappunaṃ parivattento yogāvacaro kammaṃ karoti. Itaresu pana dvīsu saccesu nirodhasaccaṃ iṭṭhaṃ kantaṃ manāpaṃ, maggasaccaṃ iṭṭhaṃ kantaṃ manāpanti evaṃ savanena kammaṃ <pb ed="P" n="3.0802"/> karoti. So evaṃ karonto cattāri saccāni ekapaṭivedheneva paṭivijjhati ekābhisamayena abhisameti. Dukkhaṃ pariññāpaṭivedhena paṭivijjhati, samudayaṃ pahānapaṭivedhena, nirodhaṃ sacchikiriyāpaṭivedhena, maggaṃ bhāvanāpaṭivedhena paṭivijjhati. Dukkhaṃ pariññābhisamayena…pe… maggaṃ bhāvanābhisamayena abhisameti. Evamassa pubbabhāge dvīsu saccesu uggahaparipucchāsavanadhāraṇasammasanapaṭivedho hoti, dvīsu pana savanapaṭivedhoyeva. Aparabhāge tīsu kiccato paṭivedho hoti, nirodhe ārammaṇapaṭivedho. Paccavekkhaṇā pana pattasaccassa hoti. Ayañca ādikammiko, tasmā sā idha na vuttā.</p> <p rend="bodytext">Imassa ca bhikkhuno pubbe pariggahato ‘‘dukkhaṃ parijānāmi, samudayaṃ pajahāmi, nirodhaṃ sacchikaromi, maggaṃ bhāvemī’’ti ābhogasamannāhāramanasikārapaccavekkhaṇā natthi, pariggahato paṭṭhāya hoti. Aparabhāge pana dukkhaṃ pariññātameva…pe… maggo bhāvitova hoti. Tattha dve saccāni duddasattā gambhīrāni, dve gambhīrattā duddasāni. Dukkhasaccañhi uppattito pākaṭaṃ, khāṇukaṇṭakapahārādīsu ‘‘aho dukkha’’nti vattabbatampi āpajjati. Samudayampi khāditukāmatābhuñjitukāmatādivasena uppattito pākaṭaṃ. Lakkhaṇapaṭivedhato pana ubhayampi gambhīraṃ. Iti tāni duddasattā gambhīrāni. Itaresaṃ pana dvinnaṃ dassanatthāya payogo bhavaggagahaṇatthaṃ hatthappasāraṇaṃ <pb ed="M" n="2.0392"/> viya avīciphusanatthaṃ pādappasāraṇaṃ viya satadhā bhinnassa vālassa koṭiyā <pb ed="V" n="2.0353"/> koṭipādanaṃ viya ca hoti. Iti tāni gambhīrattā duddasāni. Evaṃ duddasattā gambhīresu gambhīrattā ca duddasesu catūsu saccesu uggahādivasena pubbabhāgañāṇuppattiṃ sandhāya idaṃ <hi rend="bold">dukkhe ñāṇa</hi>ntiādi vuttaṃ. Paṭivedhakkhaṇe pana ekameva taṃ ñāṇaṃ hoti.</p> <pb ed="M" n="2.0392"/> <pb ed="V" n="2.0353"/> <hi rend="bold">dukkhe ñāṇa</hi> <p rend="bodytext">Nekkhammasaṅkappādayo kāmabyāpādavihiṃsāviramaṇasaññānaṃ nānattā pubbabhāge nānā, maggakkhaṇe pana imesu tīsu ṭhānesu uppannassa akusalasaṅkappassa padapacchedato anuppattisādhanavasena maggaṅgaṃ pūrayamāno ekova kusalasaṅkappo uppajjati. Parassa pana uppajjamāne disvā ‘‘aho vata me evarūpā pāpakā akusaladhammā na uppajjeyyu’’nti evaṃ anuppannānaṃ pāpakānaṃ akusalānaṃ <pb ed="M" n="2.0393"/> dhammānaṃ anuppādāya. <hi rend="bold">Chandaṃ janetī</hi>ti tesaṃ anuppādakapaṭipattisādhakaṃ vīriyachandaṃ janeti. <hi rend="bold">Vāyamatī</hi>ti vāyāmaṃ karoti. <hi rend="bold">Vīriyaṃ ārabhatī</hi>ti <pb ed="V" n="2.0354"/> vīriyaṃ pavatteti. <hi rend="bold">Cittaṃ paggaṇhātī</hi>ti vīriyena cittaṃ paggahitaṃ karoti. <hi rend="bold">Padahatī</hi>ti kāmaṃ taco ca nhāru ca aṭṭhi ca avasissatūti padahanaṃ pavatteti.</p> <hi rend="bold">Anuppannāna</hi> <pb ed="M" n="2.0393"/> <hi rend="bold">Chandaṃ janetī</hi> <hi rend="bold">Vāyamatī</hi> <hi rend="bold">Vīriyaṃ ārabhatī</hi> <pb ed="V" n="2.0354"/> <hi rend="bold">Cittaṃ paggaṇhātī</hi> <hi rend="bold">Padahatī</hi> <p rend="bodytext"><hi rend="bold">Uppannāna</hi>nti samudācāravasena attano uppannapubbānaṃ. Idāni tādise na uppādessāmīti tesaṃ pahānāya chandaṃ janeti. <hi rend="bold">Anuppannānaṃ kusalāna</hi>nti appaṭiladdhānaṃ paṭhamajjhānādīnaṃ. <hi rend="bold">Uppannāna</hi>nti tesaṃyeva paṭiladdhānaṃ. <hi rend="bold">Ṭhitiyā</hi>ti punappunaṃ uppattipabandhavasena ṭhitatthaṃ. <hi rend="bold">Asammosāyā</hi>ti avināsanatthaṃ. <hi rend="bold">Bhiyyobhāvāyā</hi>ti uparibhāvāya. <hi rend="bold">Vepullāyā</hi>ti vipulabhāvāya. <hi rend="bold">Bhāvanāya pāripūriyā</hi>ti bhāvanāya paripūraṇatthaṃ. Ayampi sammāvāyāmo anuppannānaṃ akusalānaṃ anuppādanādicittānaṃ nānattā pubbabhāge nānā, maggakkhaṇe pana imesuyeva catūsu ṭhānesu kiccasādhanavasena maggaṅgaṃ pūrayamānaṃ ekameva kusalavīriyaṃ uppajjati. Ayaṃ <hi rend="bold">sammāvāyāmo</hi> nāma.</p> <hi rend="bold">Uppannāna</hi> <hi rend="bold">Anuppannānaṃ kusalāna</hi> <hi rend="bold">Uppannāna</hi> <hi rend="bold">Ṭhitiyā</hi> <hi rend="bold">Asammosāyā</hi> <hi rend="bold">Bhiyyobhāvāyā</hi> <hi rend="bold">Vepullāyā</hi> <hi rend="bold">Bhāvanāya pāripūriyā</hi> <hi rend="bold">sammāvāyāmo</hi> <p rend="bodytext">Sammāsatipi kāyādipariggāhakacittānaṃ nānattā pubbabhāge nānā, maggakkhaṇe pana catūsu ṭhānesu kiccasādhanavasena maggaṅgaṃ pūrayamānā ekāva sati uppajjati. Ayaṃ <hi rend="bold">sammāsati</hi> nāma.</p> <hi rend="bold">sammāsati</hi> <p rend="bodytext">Jhānāni pubbabhāgepi maggakkhaṇepi <pb ed="P" n="3.0804"/> nānā, pubbabhāge samāpattivasena nānā, maggakkhaṇe nānāmaggavasena. Ekassa hi paṭhamamaggo paṭhamajjhāniko hoti, dutiyamaggādayopi paṭhamajjhānikā vā dutiyajjhānādīsu aññatarajhānikā vā. Ekassapi paṭhamamaggo dutiyādīnaṃ aññatarajhāniko hoti, dutiyādayopi dutiyādīnaṃ aññatarajjhānikā vā paṭhamajjhānikā vā. Evaṃ cattāropi maggā jhānavasena sadisā vā asadisā vā ekaccasadisā vā honti. Ayaṃ panassa viseso pādakajjhānaniyamena hoti.</p> <pb ed="P" n="3.0804"/> <p rend="bodytext">Pādakajjhānaniyamena tāva paṭhamajjhānalābhino paṭhamajjhānā vuṭṭhāya vipassantassa uppanno maggo paṭhamajjhāniko hoti. Maggaṅgabojjhaṅgāni panettha paripuṇṇāneva honti. Dutiyajjhānato vuṭṭhāya vipassantassa uppanno dutiyajjhāniko hoti. Maggaṅgāni panettha satta honti. Tatiyajjhānato vuṭṭhāya vipassantassa uppanno tatiyajjhāniko. Maggaṅgāni panettha satta <pb ed="M" n="2.0394"/>, bojjhaṅgāni cha honti. Esa nayo catutthajjhānato vuṭṭhāya yāva nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ.</p> <pb ed="M" n="2.0394"/> <p rend="bodytext">Āruppe <pb ed="V" n="2.0355"/> catukkapañcakajjhānaṃ uppajjati, tañca lokuttaraṃ, no lokiyanti vuttaṃ, ettha kathanti? Etthāpi paṭhamajjhānādīsu yato vuṭṭhāya sotāpattimaggaṃ paṭilabhitvā arūpasamāpattiṃ bhāvetvā so āruppe uppanno, taṃ jhānikāvassa tattha tayo maggā uppajjanti. Evaṃ pādakajjhānameva niyameti.</p> <pb ed="V" n="2.0355"/> <p rend="bodytext">Keci pana therā ‘‘vipassanāya ārammaṇabhūtā khandhā niyamentī’’ti vadanti. Keci ‘‘puggalajjhāsayo niyametī’’ti vadanti. Keci ‘‘vuṭṭhānagāminivipassanā niyametī’’ti vadanti. Tesaṃ vādavinicchayo visuddhimagge vuṭṭhānagāminivipassanādhikāre vuttanayeneva veditabbo.</p> <p rend="bodytext"><hi rend="bold">Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, sammāsamādhī</hi>ti ayaṃ pubbabhāge lokiyo aparabhāge lokuttaro sammāsamādhīti vuccati.</p> <hi rend="bold">Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, sammāsamādhī</hi> <p rend="bodytext"><hi rend="bold">Iti ajjhattaṃ vā</hi>ti evaṃ attano vā cattāri saccāni pariggaṇhitvā, parassa vā, kālena vā attano, kālena vā parassa cattāri saccāni pariggaṇhitvā dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati. Samudayavayā panettha catunnaṃ saccānaṃ yathāsambhāvato uppattinivattivasena veditabbā. Ito paraṃ vuttanayameva. Kevalañhi idha catusaccapariggāhikā sati dukkhasaccanti evaṃ <pb ed="P" n="3.0805"/> yojanaṃ katvā saccapariggāhakassa bhikkhuno niyyānamukhaṃ veditabbaṃ, sesaṃ tādisamevāti.</p> <hi rend="bold">Iti ajjhattaṃ vā</hi> <pb ed="P" n="3.0805"/> <p rend="centre">Catusaccapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.</p>
402. " *Ayameva*" means a determining [factor] for the purpose of rejecting other paths. " *Ariyo*" means noble, because he is far from the defilements that are the faults of that path, and because he makes one noble.

"*Dukkhe ñāṇa*" etc. shows the four truths as the basis for practice. There, the former two truths are the round, and the latter are the turning away from the round. In these, a *bhikkhu* has an inclination towards the round as the basis for practice, but there is no inclination towards turning away from the round.

The former two truths, having learned from a teacher in brief, "the five aggregates are suffering, craving is the origin," and in detail in the manner of "*katame pañcakkhandhā, rūpakkhandho*" etc., the *yogāvacara* does the practice, repeatedly reciting the words. But in the other two truths, he does the practice by hearing "*nirodhasaccaṃ iṭṭhaṃ kantaṃ manāpaṃ, maggasaccaṃ iṭṭhaṃ kantaṃ manāpaṃ*."

Doing so, he penetrates the four truths with a single penetration, he comprehends with a single comprehension. He penetrates suffering with the penetration of thorough knowledge, he penetrates the origin with the penetration of abandoning, he penetrates cessation with the penetration of realization, he penetrates the path with the penetration of cultivation. He comprehends suffering with the comprehension of thorough knowledge…pe… he comprehends the path with the comprehension of cultivation. Thus, in the former part, he has the penetration of learning, questioning, hearing, holding, reflecting, and penetration in the two truths, but in the two, only the penetration of hearing. In the latter part, there is penetration in three ways, in cessation there is penetration of the object. But reviewing is of the attained truth. And this is the first practice, therefore it is not said here.

And for this *bhikkhu*, formerly, from grasping, there is no reviewing in the way of application, coordination, attention, "*dukkhaṃ parijānāmi, samudayaṃ pajahāmi, nirodhaṃ sacchikaromi, maggaṃ bhāvemī*," but it is from the time of grasping. But in the latter part, suffering is thoroughly known…pe… the path is cultivated.

There, two truths are difficult to see because of [their] reality, two are difficult to see because of [their] depth. For the truth of suffering is evident from its arising, and it also incurs the obligation to say "*aho dukkha*" in injuries from stumps, thorns, etc. The origin is also evident from its arising, in terms of the desire to eat, the desire to enjoy, etc. But in terms of the penetration of characteristics, both are deep. Thus, those are difficult to see because of [their] reality and deep. But for seeing the other two, the effort is like stretching out the hand to grasp the summit of existence, like stretching out the foot to touch *avīci*, and like placing the tip of a hair split into a hundred pieces on [another] tip. Thus, those are difficult to see because of [their] depth.

Thus, in the four truths that are difficult to see because of [their] reality and deep, and difficult to see because of [their] depth, "*dukkhe ñāṇa*" etc. is said in reference to the arising of knowledge in the former part in terms of learning etc. But at the moment of penetration, that knowledge is only one.

"*Dukkhe ñāṇa*"

Renunciation-thought etc. are different in the former part because of the difference in the perceptions of abstaining from sensual desire, ill will, and harmfulness, but at the moment of the path, only one skillful thought arises, filling the limb of the path in the manner of establishing non-arising from cutting off the cause of unskillful thought that arose in these three places. This is called "*sammāsaṅkappo*" (right intention).

"*Sammāsaṅkappo*"

Abstaining from false speech etc. are also different in the former part because of the difference in the perceptions of abstaining from false speech etc., but at the moment of the path, only one skillful abstaining arises, filling the limb of the path in the manner of establishing non-arising from cutting off the cause of unskillful immoral intention that arose in these four places. This is called "*sammāvācā*" (right speech).

"*Sammāvācā*"

Abstaining from killing etc. are also different in the former part because of the difference in the perceptions of abstaining from killing etc., but at the moment of the path, only one skillful abstaining arises, filling the limb of the path in the manner of establishing non-arising from cutting off the cause of unskillful immoral intention that arose in these three places through non-action. This is called "*sammākammanto*" (right action).

"*Sammākammanto*"

"*Micchāājīva*" (wrong livelihood) means wrong conduct of body and speech that is engaged in for the sake of food etc.

"*Pahāya*" means having avoided.
*Sammāājīvenā* means by a right way of livelihood that is praised by the Buddhas. *Jīvitaṃ kappetī* means he carries on the continuity of his life.

Right livelihood, too, is diverse in its preliminary stage due to the variety of intentions to abstain from deceit and so on. However, at the moment of the path, the single wholesome abstinence that arises in these very seven places, fulfilling the path factor in terms of accomplishing non-occurrence by cutting off the basis of wrong livelihood and bad moral conduct consciousness, is called *sammāājīvo* (right livelihood).

*Micchāājīva pahāya sammāājīvenā jīvitaṃ kappetī sammāājīvo*

*Anuppannāna*nti means for those evil, unwholesome qualities of *dhammas* that have not arisen for oneself in a certain existence or in a corresponding object, thinking, "Oh, may such evil, unwholesome qualities never arise in me!" Seeing them arising in another, *dhammānaṃ anuppādāya*—for the non-arising of those evil, unwholesome qualities. *Chandaṃ janetī* means he generates zeal ( *chanda*), energetic zeal ( *vīriyachandaṃ*), for accomplishing the practice that prevents their arising. *Vāyamatī* means he makes an effort. *Vīriyaṃ ārabhatī* means he initiates energy. *Cittaṃ paggaṇhātī* means he makes his mind uplifted with energy. *Padahatī* means he initiates striving, [thinking,] "Let only skin, sinew, and bone remain!"

*Anuppannāna chandaṃ janetī vāyamatī vīriyaṃ ārabhatī cittaṃ paggaṇhātī padahatī*

*Uppannāna*nti means for those that have previously arisen for oneself in terms of habitual practice. *Tesaṃ pahānāya chandaṃ janeti*—he generates zeal for abandoning them, [thinking,] "Now I will not let them arise in such a way!" *Anuppannānaṃ kusalāna*nti means for those wholesome [qualities] such as the first jhāna, etc., that have not been attained. *Uppannāna*nti means for those very [qualities] that have been attained. *Ṭhitiyā*ti means for their stability, in the sense of lasting in terms of the continuity of repeated arising. *Asammosāyā*ti means for their non-disappearance. *Bhiyyobhāvāyā*ti means for the state of being higher. *Vepullāyā*ti means for the state of being extensive. *Bhāvanāya pāripūriyā*ti means for the fulfillment of cultivation. This right effort ( *sammāvāyāmo*), too, is diverse in its preliminary stage due to the variety of minds regarding the non-arising of unarisen unwholesome qualities, etc. However, at the moment of the path, the single wholesome energy that arises in these very four places, fulfilling the path factor in terms of accomplishing the task, is called *sammāvāyāmo* (right effort).

*Uppannāna anuppannānaṃ kusalāna uppannāna ṭhitiyā asammosāyā bhiyyobhāvāyā vepullāyā bhāvanāya pāripūriyā sammāvāyāmo*

Right mindfulness ( *sammāsati*), too, is diverse in its preliminary stage due to the variety of minds that take up the body, etc. However, at the moment of the path, the single mindfulness that arises in the four places, fulfilling the path factor in terms of accomplishing the task, is called *sammāsati* (right mindfulness).

*Sammāsati*

The *jhānas* are diverse both in the preliminary stage and at the moment of the path. In the preliminary stage, they are diverse in terms of attainment; at the moment of the path, they are diverse according to the diversity of paths. For the first path of one [person] is jhāna-related to the first jhāna. The second path and so on can be jhāna-related to the first jhāna or to any jhāna among the second jhāna and so on. For even the first path of one [person] can be jhāna-related to any jhāna among the second and so on; the second and so on can be jhāna-related to any jhāna among the second and so on, or to the first jhāna. Thus, all four paths, in terms of jhāna, can be similar or dissimilar or similar in one respect. But this is his distinction due to governance by the basic jhāna.

In governance by the basic jhāna, the path that arises for one who has gained the first jhāna, when he emerges from the first jhāna and is contemplating, is jhāna-related to the first jhāna. The path factors and enlightenment factors are entirely complete here. The path that arises for one who emerges from the second jhāna and is contemplating is jhāna-related to the second jhāna. Here there are seven path factors. The path that arises for one who emerges from the third jhāna and is contemplating is jhāna-related to the third jhāna. Here there are seven0394"/>, and the enlightenment factors are six.

This is the method: from the fourth *jhāna* up to the peak of existence (*nevasaññānāsaññāyatana*).

</p> <pb ed="m" n="2.
0394"/> <p rend="bodytext">formless </pb ed="v" n="2.
0355"/> Four- or fivefold *jhāna* arises; and that is supramundane, not mundane, it is said. What is here? Here also, from the first *jhāna*, having risen, having attained the path of stream-entry (*sotāpattimagga*), having developed the formless attainment (*arūpasamāpatti*), he arises in the formless realm; the *jhānika* certainly has three paths arising there.

Thus, equanimity governs only the path.

</p> <pb ed="v" n="2.
0355"/> <p rend="bodytext">Some elders, however, say, "The aggregates (*khandhā*) that are the object of insight (*vipassanā*) govern."

Some say, "Personal inclination governs."

Some say, "Emerging insight (*vuṭṭhānagāminivipassanā*) governs."

The resolution of their arguments should be understood just by the method already stated in the section on emerging insight (*vuṭṭhānagāminivipassanā*) in the *Visuddhimagga*.

</p> <p rend="bodytext"><hi rend="bold">This is called, *bhikkhus*, right concentration (*sammāsamādhi*)</hi>—this is called mundane right concentration in the former part, and supramundane right concentration in the latter part.

</p> <hi rend="bold">This is called, *bhikkhus*, right concentration</hi> <p rend="bodytext"><hi rend="bold">Thus, internally</hi>—thus, either one's own four truths (*cattāri saccāni*) having grasped, or another's, or at one time one's own, at another time another's four truths having grasped, in phenomena, contemplating phenomena, he dwells.

Herein, the arising and dissolution of the four truths should be understood according to possibility by way of arising and ceasing.

From here on, it is just according to the manner already stated.

Because, indeed, here, mindfulness is the exclusive approhender of the four truths, and suffering is truth, thus </pb ed="p" n="3.
0805"/> having made a *yojana*, the means of escape for the *bhikkhu* who apprehends the truth should be understood; the rest is just the same.

</p> <hi rend="bold">Thus, internally</hi> </pb ed="p" n="3.
0805"/> <p rend="centre">The section on the four truths is finished.
</p>
403
<p n="403" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">403</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Iti ajjhattaṃ vā dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati, bahiddhā <pb ed="P" n="2.0314"/> vā dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati, ajjhattabahiddhā vā dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati. Samudayadhammānupassī vā dhammesu viharati, vayadhammānupassī vā dhammesu viharati, samudayavayadhammānupassī vā dhammesu viharati. ‘Atthi dhammā’ti vā panassa sati paccupaṭṭhitā hoti yāvadeva ñāṇamattāya paṭissatimattāya anissito ca viharati, na ca kiñci loke upādiyati. Evampi kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati catūsu ariyasaccesu.</p> <p rend="bodytext">Saccapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.</p> <p rend="centre">Dhammānupassanā niṭṭhitā.</p>
“In this way, he abides contemplating mind-objects as mind-objects internally, or he abides contemplating mind-objects as mind-objects externally, or he abides contemplating mind-objects as mind-objects both internally and externally. He abides contemplating arising phenomena in mind-objects, he abides contemplating vanishing-phenomena in mind-objects, he abides contemplating both arising and vanishing phenomena in mind-objects. Or else, mindfulness that “there are mind-objects” is present just to the extent necessary for knowledge and awareness. And he abides detached, not grasping at anything in the world. That is how a *bhikkhu* abides contemplating mind-objects as mind-objects in terms of the Four Noble Truths.

The chapter on truth is finished.

Contemplation of mind-objects is finished.
404
<p n="404" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">404</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Yo hi koci, bhikkhave, ime cattāro satipaṭṭhāne evaṃ bhāveyya sattavassāni, tassa dvinnaṃ phalānaṃ aññataraṃ phalaṃ pāṭikaṅkhaṃ diṭṭheva dhamme aññā; sati vā upādisese <pb ed="T" n="2.0351"/> anāgāmitā.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Tiṭṭhantu, bhikkhave, sattavassāni. Yo hi koci, bhikkhave, ime cattāro satipaṭṭhāne evaṃ bhāveyya cha vassāni…pe… pañca vassāni… cattāri vassāni… tīṇi vassāni… dve vassāni… ekaṃ vassaṃ… tiṭṭhatu, bhikkhave, ekaṃ vassaṃ. Yo hi koci, bhikkhave, ime cattāro satipaṭṭhāne evaṃ bhāveyya sattamāsāni, tassa dvinnaṃ phalānaṃ aññataraṃ phalaṃ pāṭikaṅkhaṃ diṭṭheva dhamme aññā; sati vā upādisese anāgāmitā.</p> <p rend="bodytext">‘‘Tiṭṭhantu <pb ed="M" n="2.0252"/>, bhikkhave, satta māsāni. Yo hi koci, bhikkhave, ime cattāro satipaṭṭhāne evaṃ bhāveyya cha māsāni…pe… pañca māsāni… cattāri māsāni… tīṇi māsāni <pb ed="V" n="2.0236"/> … dve māsāni… ekaṃ <pb ed="P" n="2.0315"/> māsaṃ… aḍḍhamāsaṃ… tiṭṭhatu, bhikkhave, aḍḍhamāso. Yo hi koci, bhikkhave, ime cattāro satipaṭṭhāne evaṃ bhāveyya sattāhaṃ, tassa dvinnaṃ phalānaṃ aññataraṃ phalaṃ pāṭikaṅkhaṃ diṭṭheva dhamme aññā; sati vā upādisese anāgāmitāti.</p> <pb ed="M" n="2.0252"/> <pb ed="V" n="2.0236"/> <pb ed="P" n="2.0315"/>
404. “Monks (bhikkhave), if anyone should develop these four foundations of mindfulness (*satipaṭṭhāna*) in this way for seven years (sattavassāni), one of two fruits (*phala*) can be expected for him: either knowledge (*aññā*) right here and now, or if there is a trace of clinging left, non-returning (*anāgāmitā*).

“Let alone seven years (sattavassāni), monks (bhikkhave). If anyone should develop these four foundations of mindfulness (*satipaṭṭhāna*) in this way for six years… for five years… for four years… for three years… for two years… for one year… Let alone one year, monks (bhikkhave).

Monks (bhikkhave), if anyone should develop these four foundations of mindfulness (*satipaṭṭhāna*) in this way for seven months (sattamāsāni), one of two fruits (*phala*) can be expected for him: either knowledge (*aññā*) right here and now, or if there is a trace of clinging left, non-returning (*anāgāmitā*).

“Let alone seven months (satta māsāni), monks (bhikkhave). If anyone should develop these four foundations of mindfulness (*satipaṭṭhāna*) in this way for six months… for five months… for four months… for three months… for two months… for one month… for half a month… Let alone half a month (aḍḍhamāso), monks (bhikkhave).

Monks (bhikkhave), if anyone should develop these four foundations of mindfulness (*satipaṭṭhāna*) in this way for seven days (sattāhaṃ), one of two fruits (*phala*) can be expected for him: either knowledge (*aññā*) right here and now, or if there is a trace of clinging left, non-returning (*anāgāmitā*).”
<p n="404" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">404</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> Ettāvatā ānāpānapabbaṃ catuiriyāpathapabbaṃ catusampajaññapabbaṃ dvattiṃsākāraṃ catudhātuvavatthānaṃ navasivathikā vedanānupassanā cittānupassanā nīvaraṇapariggaho khandhapariggaho āyatanapariggaho bojjhaṅgapariggaho saccapariggahoti ekavīsati kammaṭṭhānāni. Tesu ānāpānaṃ dvattiṃsākāraṃ navasivathikāti ekādasa appanākammaṭṭhānāni honti. Dīghabhāṇakamahāsīvatthero pana ‘‘navasivathikā ādīnavānupassanāvasena vuttā’’ti āha. Tasmā tassa matena dveyeva appanākammaṭṭhānāni, sesāni upacārakammaṭṭhānāni. Kiṃ panetesu sabbesu abhiniveso <pb ed="M" n="2.0395"/> jāyatīti? Na jāyati. Iriyāpathasampajaññanīvaraṇabojjhaṅgesu hi abhiniveso na jāyati, sesesu jāyatīti. <hi rend="bold">Mahāsīvatthero</hi> panāha ‘‘etesupi abhiniveso jāyati. Ayañhi ‘atthi nu kho me cattāro iriyāpathā udāhu natthi, atthi nu kho me catusampajaññaṃ udāhu <pb ed="V" n="2.0356"/> natthi, atthi nu kho me pañcanīvaraṇā udāhu natthi, atthi nu kho me sattabojjhaṅgā udāhu natthī’ti evaṃ pariggaṇhāti. Tasmā sabbattha abhiniveso jāyatī’’ti.</p> <p rend="bodytext"><hi rend="bold">Yo hi koci, bhikkhave</hi>ti yo hi koci, bhikkhave, bhikkhu vā bhikkhunī vā upāsako vā upāsikā vā. <hi rend="bold">Evaṃ bhāveyyā</hi>tiādito paṭṭhāya vuttena bhāvanānukkamena bhāveyya. <hi rend="bold">Pāṭikaṅkha</hi>nti paṭikaṅkhitabbaṃ icchitabbaṃ avassaṃbhāvīti attho. <hi rend="bold">Aññā</hi>ti arahattaṃ. <hi rend="bold">Sati vā upādisese</hi>ti upādānasese vā sati aparikkhīṇe. <hi rend="bold">Anāgāmitā</hi>ti anāgāmibhāvo.</p> <hi rend="bold">Yo hi koci, bhikkhave</hi> <hi rend="bold">Evaṃ bhāveyyā</hi> <hi rend="bold">Pāṭikaṅkha</hi> <hi rend="bold">Aññā</hi> <hi rend="bold">Sati vā upādisese</hi> <hi rend="bold">Anāgāmitā</hi> <p rend="bodytext">Evaṃ sattannaṃ vassānaṃ vasena sāsanassa niyyānikabhāvaṃ dassetvā puna tato appatarepi kāle dassento <hi rend="bold">tiṭṭhantu, bhikkhave</hi>tiādimāha. Sabbampi cetaṃ majjhimassa veneyyapuggalassa vasena vuttaṃ, tikkhapaññaṃ pana sandhāya ‘‘pātova anusiṭṭho sāyaṃ visesaṃ adhigamissati, sāyaṃ <pb ed="P" n="3.0806"/> anusiṭṭho pāto visesaṃ adhigamissatī’’ti vuttaṃ. Iti bhagavā ‘‘evaṃ niyyānikaṃ, bhikkhave, mama sāsana’’nti dassetvā ekavīsatiyāpi ṭhānesu arahattanikūṭena desitaṃ desanaṃ niyyātento ‘‘ekāyano ayaṃ, bhikkhave, maggo…pe… iti yaṃ taṃ vuttaṃ, idametaṃ paṭicca vutta’’nti āha. Sesaṃ uttānatthamevāti. Desanāpariyosāne pana tiṃsa bhikkhusahassāni arahatte patiṭṭhahiṃsūti.</p> <hi rend="bold">tiṭṭhantu, bhikkhave</hi> <pb ed="P" n="3.0806"/> <p rend="centre">Iti sumaṅgalavilāsiniyā dīghanikāyaṭṭhakathāyaṃ</p> <p rend="centre">Mahāsatipaṭṭhānasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.</p>
404. With this, the following twenty-one subjects of meditation have been stated: breathing (*ānāpāna*), the modes of deportment (*catuiriyāpatha*), mindfulness and clear comprehension (*catusampajañña*), the thirty-two parts of the body (*dvattiṃsākāra*), the determination of the four elements (*catudhātuvavatthāna*), the nine cemetery contemplations (*navasivathikā*), contemplation of feeling (*vedanānupassanā*), contemplation of consciousness (*cittānupassanā*), laying hold on the hindrances (*nīvaraṇapariggaha*), laying hold on the aggregates (*khandhapariggaha*), laying hold on the sense-bases (*āyatanapariggaha*), laying hold on the enlightenment factors (*bojjhaṅgapariggaha*), and laying hold on the truths (*saccapariggaha*).

Among these, breathing, the thirty-two parts, and the nine cemetery contemplations are eleven subjects of meditation which produce full absorption (*appanākammaṭṭhānāni*).

The Dīgha-bhāṇaka Mahāsīvatthera, however, said, "The nine cemetery contemplations (*navasivathikā*) are stated by way of contemplation of misery (*ādīnavānupassanāvasena*)."

Therefore, according to his view, only two subjects—those of breathing and the thirty-two parts—produce full absorption (*appanākammaṭṭhānāni*); the rest produce only partial absorption (*upacārakammaṭṭhānāni*).

Is there attachment in all of these? No, there is not.

Indeed, there is no attachment in the modes of deportment, mindfulness and clear comprehension, the hindrances and the enlightenment factors, but there is attachment in the rest.

Mahāsīvatthera, however, says, "There is attachment even in these.

For this person ascertains thus: 'Do I have the four modes of deportment or do I not? Do I have mindfulness and clear comprehension or do I not? Do I have the five hindrances or do I not? Do I have the seven enlightenment factors or do I not?'

Therefore, there is attachment everywhere."

"If anyone, monks (*yo hi koci bhikkhave*)" means if any monk (*bhikkhu*) or nun (*bhikkhunī*) or layman (*upāsako*) or laywoman (*upāsikā*).

"Thus he should develop (*evaṃ bhāveyyā*)" means he should develop in the order of development stated from the beginning.

"Expected (*pāṭikaṅkha*)" means to be expected, to be desired, necessarily so, is the meaning.

"Knowledge (*aññā*)" means the state of an *arhat* (*arahattaṃ*).

"While the residue of existence remains (*sati vā upādisese*)" means while the residue of clinging remains (*upādānasese*) or is not exhausted (*aparikkhīṇe*).

"*Anāgāmitā*" means the state of a Non-Returner (*anāgāmibhāvo*).

If anyone, monks, should develop thus, it is to be expected that, while the residue of existence remains, it leads to *anāgāmitā*. Showing the state of leading outwards of the Dispensation (*sāsanassa niyyānikabhāvaṃ*) in dependence on seven years, and again showing it in a shorter time than that, he says, "May they remain, monks (*tiṭṭhantu, bhikkhave*)," and so forth.

All of this was stated in dependence on a person of middling teachability (*majjhimassa veneyyapuggalassa vasena*), but with reference to one of sharp wisdom (*tikkhapaññaṃ*), it was said, "If advised in the morning, he will attain distinction in the evening; if advised in the evening, he will attain distinction in the morning."

Thus, the Blessed One (*bhagavā*), having shown that "Thus, monks, my Dispensation leads outwards (*evaṃ niyyānikaṃ, bhikkhave, mama sāsana*)," bringing to a close the teaching (*desanaṃ*) that was crowned with *arhatship* (*arahattanikūṭena*) in twenty-one places, and bringing it out, said, "This is the only way, monks (*ekāyano ayaṃ, bhikkhave, maggo*)…pe… In this regard, what was said, this was said in dependence on that (*idametaṃ paṭicca vutta*)."

The rest is clear in meaning.

At the conclusion of the discourse (*desanāpariyosāne*), thirty thousand monks were established in *arhatship*.

May they remain, monks.

Thus ends the commentary on the *Mahāsatipaṭṭhāna Sutta* in the *Sumangalavilāsinī*, the commentary on the Dīgha Nikāya.
405
<p n="405" rend="bodytext"><hi rend="paranum">405</hi><hi rend="dot">.</hi> ‘‘Ekāyano ayaṃ, bhikkhave, maggo sattānaṃ visuddhiyā sokaparidevānaṃ samatikkamāya dukkhadomanassānaṃ atthaṅgamāya ñāyassa adhigamāya nibbānassa sacchikiriyāya yadidaṃ cattāro satipaṭṭhānāti. Iti yaṃ taṃ vuttaṃ, idametaṃ paṭicca vutta’’nti. Idamavoca bhagavā. Attamanā te bhikkhū bhagavato bhāsitaṃ abhinandunti.</p> <p rend="centre">Mahāsatipaṭṭhānasuttaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ navamaṃ.</p>
405. "Monks, this is the direct path, the only way (ekāyano maggo), for the purification of beings (sattānaṃ visuddhiyā), for overcoming sorrow and lamentation (sokaparidevānaṃ samatikkamāya), for the cessation of suffering and grief (dukkhadomanassānaṃ atthaṅgamāya), for attaining the right knowledge (ñāyassa adhigamāya), and for the realization of *nirvāṇa* (nibbānassa sacchikiriyāya)—namely, the four foundations of mindfulness (cattāro satipaṭṭhānāti)."

It is with reference to this that it was said (iti yaṃ taṃ vuttaṃ, idametaṃ paṭicca vutta’’nti).

This is what the Blessed One (bhagavā) said (idamavoca bhagavā).

The monks (bhikkhū), delighted (attamanā), rejoiced in the Blessed One's (bhagavato) words (bhāsitaṃ abhinandunti).

The Great Discourse on the Foundations of Mindfulness (mahāsatipaṭṭhānasuttaṃ), the ninth (navamaṃ), is concluded (niṭṭhitaṃ).
